could adjustment be reached.
He takes up his failure
to follow the example of Mr. Stimson last November in
addressing notes to the disputants. While expressing
full appreciation of the American motives in this move,
he felt, he says, that the time for a formal démarche
on the part of the Japanese Government, in. view of the
previous close contact, had not yet arrived since it
might eventually have drawn Japan into a discussion of
the merits and demerits of the issues raised.
This, he
feels, might eventually have forced further action in
regard to one or both of the disputants in order to make
the aémarche efrective.
As each Foreign Minister has done of late years,
in his address to the Diet, Baron Shidehara mentions
Japanese regret at the "discriminatory clause" contained
in the American Immigration Act of 1924 which, he states.
can not yet be regarded as a closed incident.
The
solution, however, he feels, must "flow from the friend-
ly understanding between the two peoples".
He then
adds that "it is patent to every one that such an under-
standing has of late made marked progress", in support
of which contention he refers to the cordial reception
by officials and the public in the United States extend-
ed to Japanese representatives to the London Naval Confer-
ence.
Both the comments of Baron Shidehara and of the
Premier regarding the Naval Conference I have dealt with
in my despatch No. 3 of January 27, 1930.
2/.
The speech of the Premier deals prinoipally with the
Naval

=== Page 8 === (confidence 0.99)
-4-
Naval Conference, economic reforms, the election law
and political corruption, unemployment, and such agrarian
problems as the condition of tenant farmers and the ad-
justment of the supply and demand of rice. The speech
contains nothing of new interest and little that is
concrete.
After reviewing with pride the economio re-
forms of the present Government, which culminated in the
removal of the gold embargo on January llth, he states
that the Government will continue its policy of retrench-
ment and at the same time will promote industry and
foreign trade.
With respect to labor and agrarian
problems his comments are indefinite, each problem as
yet being, apparently, still in varying stages of investi-
gation. Among related measures to be drawn up he mentions
a bill to improve the condition of tenant farmers and a
bill for the improvement of the distribution of fertilizers
to bring down prices.
In connection with political cor-
ruption he points to the imperfect educational system as
one of t e principal causes, but advances no definite
2
reforms. He concludes with comments of gratification
respectin the imminent completion of the post-earthquake
reconstruction of Tokyo, whicn is to occur in Maroh of
this year.
3/.
Mr. Inouye, the Minister of Finance, reviews in
some detail the financial and economic steps already
taken by the present Government and their effects. He
describes the Government's intentions with respect to
the increase of the State subsidy for compulsory educa-
tion, revision of the land tax, and reduction of the
national debt.
As these subjects have already been
reported

speech did not raise any new issues.

can es in the Chinese tariff.
China on her part finds it ecually
important to secure a market in Japan for certain classes of
Chinese manufactures. These mutual desires have given rise to a
scheme of reciprocal undertakings in regard to customs tariffs.
The arrangement marks a step forvard torards a full realization
of the principle of solidarity betveen Japan and China and is
but the natural outcome of the specially close economic relations
existing betveen the tvo nations. Needless to say, it does not
in any ay infringe upon custons autonomy of eit er nation. On
the contrary, it is nothing more than the normal exercise of
state sovereinty:

made in his address of January 2lst of this year at the

London Naval Treaty, reported in my despatoh No. l18 of

the trust placed in her by the Powers. He also refers to
accusations appearing in the Chinese press, following the
reappearance of signs of political unrest in China, that
Japan has been an instigator. He described these accusa-
tions as "too preposterous to call for any denial".
The address of the Premier is unusually short and
touches briefly the London Naval Treaty, the draft budget
that has been submitted to the Diet, the Sino-Japanese
tariff agreement, and the bill providing for an increase
in the State subsidy for compulsory education paid from

whioh had come into Japan, before the ban was lifted, for

little is going out of the country. He expresses the be-
lief that conditions since the removal of the embargo have
been amooth and uneventful.
The addresses of these three Ministers of State re-
ceived no praise in press editorials, while those of the

criticism. The Tokyo NICHI NICHI finds in them nothing

of unemployment, this paper points out that the only

the appointment of an unemployment relief commission,
which it regards only as a makeshift. It urges the en-
forcement of new social policies to meet the situation.
The Tokyo Kokumin finds the Premier's address "monotonous",
but neutralizes this criticism by very high praise of the
Premier's character and personality.

states that Mr. Hamaguohi's address was nothing but a

The Japm Advertiser.
y
Tokyo, Saturday, April 26, 1930.
Text of Premier's Speech
A translation of the Premier's Speech
follows:
"I deem it a great honor to be able
to outline for you the Government's
policies on the occasion of the open-
ing of the 58th session of the Im-
perial Diet here today.
"As you all know, the Government's
budget for the Fifth Year of Showa
failed to receive the approval of the
House of Representatives, due to the
dissolution. In accordance with the
revisions of the Constitution, the
Government was to put into force the
budget for the last fiscal year. The
Government has compiled a working
budget within the limits of that for
the last fiscal year and at the same
time has compiled an additional
budget that is urgently needed, and
is submitting the latter at the pre-
sent session.
"In regard to the Government's
foreign policies, I wish first of all
to touch on the London naval con-
ference. The conference which open-
ed there on January 21 has resulted
in the London Naval Treaty, which
was signed on April 22, after three
months of arduous effort on the part
of the delegates of all the nations
concerned.
"The Imperial Government carried
on negotiations with the other Pow-
ers solely on the principle that it
wished to maintain naval strength
necessary to national defense—a
strength which does not menace any
other nation, yet which does not per-
mit Japan to be menaced by any
other. It is fortunate indeed that an
agreement was reached and a treaty
concluded. As a result the national
tax burden will be lightened at home
and at the same time the friendly
relations between the nations par-
ticipating will be enhanced, while an
important contribution will be made
toward internationa! peace.
Sino-Japanese Treaty
"Next I should like to mention the
revision of the commercial treaty be-
tween Japan and China. Negotiations
for this were conducted in 1927 but no
progress was made owing to a variety
of cause. The Government, recogniz-
ing the necessity of settling this prob-
I.lem as soon as possible, enteied into
negotations with the Nanking Govern-
ment last January, taking up the tariff
problem. These negotiations made
such satisfactory progress that on
March 11 representatives of both na-
tions were able to initial an agree-
ment, which the Government has sub-
n itted to the Privy Council for Im-
perial Ratification.
"The conclusion of this tariff treaty
i really a matter for congratulation
both to Japan and China, because it
n.cans the realization of China's long-
standing desire for customs autonomy
and also the establishment of accord
on the tariff. to which Japan has at-
tached the greatest importance.
"Among the various domestie meas-
ures which have been introduced into
the House of Representatives, I wish
esperially to call your attention to the
hill providing for an increase in the
State subsidy for compulsory educa-
tion paid from the national treasury.
In view of present taxation conditions
the Government considers this in-
crease most urgently needed. There-
tore the Government has decided to
increase by Y10,000,000 the amount to
be paid from the national treasury,
bringing the toal amount for compul-
sory education to Y85,000,000. It will
thus be possible to make a reduction
in local taxes due to the surplus re-
sulting from the increase.

the last half excess exports. As a

either be returned to Japan or be

DEPARTMENT OF X1A1
9, January 21, 6 p.m.
C.
The Foreign Office has sent me a copy of the
annual address which the Minister for Foreign Affairs
will deliver before the Diet tomorrow.
He will refer to the importance of the Naval
Treaty in stabilizing relations between the three
powers and in facilitating the work of the next con-
fernce in 1935.
The greater part of the address will be devoted
FILED
to a reiteration of the Minister's liberal policy to-
wards China, of references to the progress made by
the Nationalist Government in establishing itsclf
and bringing about reforms in China, and to the need
of patience and understanding in the adjustment of
relations with that country.
He will express the determination of Japan to
preserve its fishing rights in Russian waters and will
conolude

NET
3-#9, Tokio, January 21, 1931.
conolude with the following statement:
"On the question of the United States immigration
law which for the past seven years has been weighing
heavily on popular sentiment in this country there is
no longer any doubt that our position is now fully
understood and appreciated by a majority of the Amerioan
people. We shall watch further developments of the
question with unimpassioned but keen interest".

hara as Premier ad interim in both Houses
on the 22nd instant was as follows:—

FLTLE
The Honorable
The Seoretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
I have the honor to report that the Diet assembled
on Jamuary 22nd after the usual New Year recess. Since the
reading of the annual address of the Prime Minister ad
interim and the Minister for Foreign Affairs, copies
of which were transmitted with the Embassy's despatch
740214
No. 127, of January 27, 1931, and of the Minister of
Finanoe, the time of both Houses has been largely taken
up with listening to interpellations. Progress towards
the examination of the Budget and of other legal measures
has

=== Page 39 === (confidence 0.99)
-8-
has been blooked for several days by a deadleek between
the two major political parties over a matter of trifling
importance; and it is by no mans unfair to say that the
present session has thus far accomplished less than usual.
Immediately after the Lower House reassembled, the Op-
position direoted an attaek, from the constitutional point-
of-view, upon a Government represented in the Diet by an
acting Prime Minister who has no political afriliations.
The representative of the Opposition expressed the opinion
that the provision of law whioh permits the appointment
of a Prime Minister ad interim does not contemplate the
replacement of the Prime Minister for any substantial
period of time. He further emphasized the argument that
the leadership of the Government by a person who is not
a member of any party is disoordant with the parliamentary
system of government. A motion by the Opposition to debate
the right of Baron Shidehara to address the House as Prime
Minister ad interim was then presented by the Opposition;
and the motion was immediately put to a vote and defeated
by a substantial margin.
A few days later two members of the Seiyukai were
forbidden to appear on the floor of the House as a penalty
for the comparatively venal offense of having struck the
speaker's desk with their fists.
These two instanoes have been intensely resented by
the Opposition as evidences of the Minseito's determinati on
summarily to crush any opposition. An opportunity for the
Seiyukai to take its revenge presented itself on February
3rd during a meeting of the Budget Committee of the Lower
House. It appears from the records that a member of the
Seiyukai

=== Page 40 === (confidence 0.99)
-3-
Seiyukai had been askinga number of questions regarding the
adequaoy of the strength allotted to Japan by the London
Naval Treaty. These questions had been answered by Baron
Yabuki , the Parliamentary Vice Minister of Marine. The
interpellator commented somewhat ironioally on the unsatis-
factory nature of the replies which he had received, and
had indicated that he would put no further questions, when
Baron Shidehara arose and volunteered the following state-
ment:
" It is true that in the last session of the Diet,
both Mr. Hamaguchi, the Premier, and myself made
a statement to the effeot that we did not think
that the London Treaty endangered Japan's national
security. As a matter of fact, the Treaty has ben
ratified. The very fact that it has been ratified
is a clear testimony that it does not put national
defence in jeopardy".
The meeting at once fell into disorder, the Opposition
members demanding Baron Shidehara's immediate resignation,
as his statement could be construed to imply that the
Government had transferred its responsibility for the
Treaty to the Emperor. Baron Shidehara apparently saw
at once that he had made a serious slip, and declared
that the Government did not seek to avoid its oonstitutional
responsibility. The damage was done, however, and the
ohairman was obliged to adjourn the meeting. Several
attempts were subsequently made to reopen the meetings
of this Committee, but they were in each case defeated.
by scenes of unpreoedented disorder.
Elders of the two parties conferred upon the possi-
bility of finding a formula satisfactory to both sides,
and

=== Page 41 === (confidence 0.99)
and mediators have been working with much industry; but
the deadlock continues. I understand that Baron Shidehare
is anxious to withdraw the statement, but that the aders
of the Minseito take the view that his statement ipsissima
verba is not necessarily objeotionable, and that he should
therefore do nothing whioh would further embarrass the Party.
The Seiyukai, on the other hand, will not hear of a settlement
by a second declaration amplifying or explaining the first,
and insist upon nothing less than a complete retraction and
apology.
The press unites in condemning Baron Shidehara for
his statement as heartily as it denounces the disgraceful
scenes which have been taking place in the Diet. Baron
Shidehara's statement was obviously a slip of the tongue.
In addition to the duties of his proper portfolio and of
the duties of the Prime Minister, he had been spending
the greater part of each day attending bpth Houses and
answering interpellations for hours on end, during a period
of almost two weeks. He was obviously extremely fatigued,
and on this particular ocoasion he appears to have been
visibly annoyed by the interpellator, a former naval
officer. No one doubts that it was a piece of inadvertence;
nevertheless, it has brought about an impasse which may lead
to serious consequences unless the Budget can be passed
by the Lower House and placed in the hands of the House of
Peers by the l7th instant, as required by law.
Most observers are agreed that the present unfortunate
state of affairs is due entirely to the ineptness and in-
experience of the Minseito. The Minseito has thus far been
the minority Party for most of the time, and, as at present
oonstituted

=== Page 42 === (confidence 0.99)
-5
eeastituted, about two-thirds or its members have never
before been in Parliament . Enthused by the victory of the
last general eleotions, and somwhat unbalanced by the
pessibility of exeroising power, these new members seem de-
termined to override opposition with a high hand. The Minseito
might well have permitted a debate on the constitutionality
of the appointment of Baron Shidehara as Prime Minister ad
interim, as the arguments which it could have adduced to
support the appointment are quite as good as those advanced
against it, to say nothing of the confidence which it could
place in its substantial majority of votes. Similarly, it
might well have refrained from inflicting a relatively severe
penalty for a comparatitely trivial offense. Instead of erring
upon the side of liberality whioh it might well have afforded
to do by reason of its safe margin of power, and allowing the
Seiyukai to indulge in profitless argumentation, it acted
with a perverseness which appears to have brought its own reward.
However, the genius of the Japanese for compromise should not be
overlooked in a situation of this sort, and it is still premature
to attaoh serious importance to the present diffioulty.
The disoussions in both Chambers, on the foreign policies
of the Government, have not been of much interest. There have
been several references to the so-called American Exolusion
Question, the Naval Conference at London, and relations with
China and Russia. As soon as stenographio reports of the pro-
ceedings of the several committees can be collooted, reports
on these respective subjects will be prepared and forwarded
to the Department.
Respeotfully yours,
Eugone H. Dooman
Chargé d'Affaires ad interim.

=== Page 43 === (confidence 0.98)
May 1, 1931.
The session of the Japanese Diet,
ended March 27, was not notable for the
amount of work accomplished. Several
important sooial measures introduoed by
the Government,- to regulate trade and
labor unione, to ad just the position of
tenant farmers, and to revise the Eleo-
tion Law partially to enfranohise women,-
failed passage.
In addition to the budget 64 new
laws were passed,-inoluding a group of
bills for the revision of the land tax,
business profits tax, sugar consumption
tax and text ile consumption tax,and
also a group of laws intended to improve
social conditions,- such as the poor
relief law, relief of the unemployed and
other social legislation.
The revision of the land tax gave
rise to considerable disoussion. Únder
the former system land was divided for
purposes of tax into two classes; first,
farm and forest lands, two, residential
lands, whioh were assessed at prices
far below the ir market value. Under the
new system the distinction between farm
lands and residential lands has been re-
moved and the land tax will be assessed
at 3 8/10 per cent of the net rental
value of the property, thus tend ing to
equalize the inoidence of the tax on
lands irrespective of the use to which
they are put.
Rum.
(Desoatch No. 191, April 10, 1931,
from Tokyo)

Sir:
I nuve the honor to re:ort that the Fifty-Ninth
Session or the iet, which wus uxtended by tvo days,
ended on .arch z7th.
The amount of business transacted will not make
this one of the historic sessions. it is said that
it can claim one "record", unu t at is, of being the
most prolix. There were several important social
measures introuuceu by the Govermnent- bills to
regulate trade and labor unions, to adjust and re ulute
the

=== Page 45 === (confidence 0.98)
-2-
the position of tenant farmers, and to revise the Eleotion
Law partially to enfranchise women; but none of them was
enaoted. In audition to the budget, sixty-four new laws
were passed, but it is fairly safe to predict that the
session just ended will be remembered more for the inoident
- us the apanese see it-
arising out of the unfortunate manner in which the Minister
for Foreign Affairs brought the Emperor into the question
of the propriety of Japan's adherence to the Treaty of London,
than for the business actually transacted.
There are two groups of new laws, however, which me rit
some notioe.
As the Embassy reported on several occasions last year,
the Government had been c eating a reserve fund of. over 5o0
million yen to be expended over a period of five years be-
ginning with the current fiscal year, for the construction
of new warships and for the maintenance in other ways of the
naval forces. It will be recalled that the Government decided
eventually to devote about 37o million to the Navy and apply
the remainder to fulfilling one of the avowed purposes of
naval limitation- to decrease taxation. It, therefore, pre-
sented to the Diet bills for the revision or the land tax,
business prorits tax, sugar consumption tax, and textile
consumption tax. These bills ere passed, us was also a bill
authorizing the issuance of bonds to be applied to the
relief of laborers'discharged from navy yards on account of
the Lon.on Naval Ireaty".
The other important group of laws are intended prinoipally
to improve social conditions. .he first o. these is the Poor
Relief

=== Page 46 === (confidence 0.98)
-3-
Relief Law, whioh authorizes the Government to apply, through
the various local governments, not more than six million yen
annually to persons over sixty-five and less than thirteen
years of age, physical and mental defectives, and women with
child, who are without relations obligated by law to give
support. Thirty sen may be granted daily to each person as
a ration allowance, while in case of illness the cost of
medical care is to be borne by the State. The law further
permits the State to advance not more than loo yen to any
individual who desires to carry on a petty trade or to

=== Page 63 === (confidence 1.00)
The Address of Mr. Yoshizawa, Minister for Foreign
Affairs, at the 60th Session of the Imperial
Diet, January 21st, 1932.

=== Page 64 === (confidence 0.99)
P
It gives me much pleasure to lay before you my views on the
outstanding diplomatic questions that confront the Government today.
The most important of these questions, one which not only
affects this country profoundly, but has engaged the attention of the
entire world, is the Manchurian Aflair.
China, being our neighbor, always stands, both economically
and politically, in intimate relationship with this country. In regard
to Manchuria, in particular, through historical and geographical reasons,
political considerations must become of preponderant interest. It is of
course beyond argument that the welfare of Manchuria is of the greatest
consequence to Japan. There are more than a million Japanese subjects
residing in Manchuria, and we possess in that region, in connection
with leased territories, railways, mines, ete., numerons rights and inter-
ests of vital importance, secured either by treaty or by contract.
In recent years the Chinese anthorities, ignoring the historical
fact that the present development of Manchtria is entirely due to
Japanese efforts, and taking advantage of our complaisance, have pursued
a poliey of oppression towards our country men. and have again and
again set at nanght their treaty rights and interests. The protests and
warnings of our government, despatched from time to time, were of little
avail. Thens the situation becaume politically more and more disquicting.
and the menace to our position mereasingly grave. The patience of

=== Page 65 === (confidence 0.98)
一2一
our people was tried to breaking point, when there suddenly exploded
the Railway bombing incident of September 1sth, precipitating a
collision between Japanese and Chinese soldiers, the later developments
of which occurrence are well known to all.
Manchuria holds the key to the peace of the Far East. That
was true before the Russo-Japanese War; it is even more true at the
present time. In the past the Japanese Government have always done
everything in their power to prevent the civil commotions of China
from spreading into Manchuria, because we possess there important
rights and interests rendering the maintenance of peace and order in
Manchuria a matter of absolute necessity to Japan. Thanks to these
untiring efforts, Manchuria was kept free from the constant turmoil of
China Proper, and has been turned into a land of peace and prosperity.
But for the Chinese misconduct of late pears and the Chinese disregard
of treaty and contractual obligations. 1 believe that the incident of
September 1sth wonld never have resulted in the present complications.
At any rate, as we have been chielly aecountable in the past for the
maintenance of peace in Manchuria, so will our responsibilities in this
respect be enhanced rather than lessened in the fnture.
I desire to add a word in this connection to make it clear that
Japan harbors no territoriad desgns in Manchuria pand that she will
uphold the principles of the open door and cpmal opportnity as well
as all existing treaties relating to that territory. W hat Japan desires
is only to secure peace and order for Maunchuria. aond to make that

=== Page 66 === (confidence 0.99)
一3一
region safe both for Chinese and foreigners and open to all for economic
development.
P
Now let me turn to the conditions which prevail in China
Proper. There, for these many years past, anti-Japanese agitation has
been carried on withont intermission except for brief intervals of com-
parative calm, which were only followed by agitations of increased
violence and intensity. It should be noted that these agitations represent
not only an economic movement, as in the case of the boycott of
Japanese goods, but bear a psychological aspect, as may be inferred
from the insertion of anti-Japanese matter in various textbooks for the
use of publie schools. However, there exists unmistakable evidence
that this movement is being actively promoted by unollicial organiza-
tions such as the various anti-Japanese societies, under the direct or
indirect direction and encouragement of the government, but quite
against the free will of the Chinese merchants and people in general.
Besides, we know for a fact that there are no small number of
individual Chinese who actually make a profession of agitating against
Japan. Since the advent of the present situation in Manchuria the
anti-dapanese movement has begun to display umpleasant symptoms,
and a number of regrettable ontrages have been committed. Compared
with the complete protection which persons of Chinese nationality are
afforded within our borders, the indeseribable persecution to which our
fellow-countrymen are being subjected in China presents, indeed, a
glaring contrast. The truth is that the foreign relations of China

=== Page 67 === (confidence 0.99)
一4一
have often been made a sacrifice to the machinations of domestic
palitics, during these years of continual civil wars and factional strifes.
The anti-Japanese movement undoubtedly derives its origin from the
internal polities of China, and 1 believe that the hostile attitude of
the Manchurian authorities in the period immediately preceding the
recent incident may also be traced to the same source. In short it is
an undeniable fact, that the civil wars and domestic polities of China
have exerted a most harmful influence upon all her foreign relations,
and Japan, her nearest neighbor, has had to suffer from that more
than any other Power.
Thus, unfortunately, our relations with China have for the
moment come under the influence of the complex internal and external
relations of that country, and it is to be apprehended that it will be
some time before we shall be able to effect the necessary readjustments
and to bring back the relations between the two countries to a normal
footing. The Manchurian incident was caused by our being compelled to
resort to immediate measures of self-protection and the anti-Japanese
Movement is due to the misguided views entertained by the Chinese.
Solutions of the issues involved must therefore await a complete change
of heart and reversal of poliey on the part of China. However, I am
convinced that the Chinese people as a whole and their enlightened
leaders are not hostile but, on the contrary, are in their hearts amicably
disposed toward this country, and we need not be over-pessimistic as
to the prospects of the restoration of normal relations between Japan

=== Page 68 === (confidence 0.99)
一5一
and China. Undoubtedly it is to the best interests of the two coun-
tries that they should love and respect each other and that between
them friendliness should be the rule and any strained relationship
merely a passing phenomenon.
The Manchurian Incident at the ontset caused a considerable
shock to the Council and the Assembly of the League of Nations
then in session at Geneva. On September 21st the Chinese delegate
having requested the Council, in accordance with Article 11 of the
League Covenant, to take cognizance of the matter, the question was
officially made the subject of investigation by the League Council.
Since then, the Council, as is well known, held three sessions
and passed two resolutions. On the other hand, the United States,
though not a member of the League, acted generally in concert with
that body, evidently because, as a Signatory to the Anti-War Pact
and to the Nine Power Treaty, that Power felt deep concern in the
Far Eastern situation. Our government exercised the utmost efforts to
make their position clear both to the League of Nations and to the
United States. Although there have been occasions when the present
incident seemed to lead our country into more or less delicate relations
with the League and with the American Government, we have sue-
ceeded through a careful and complete presentation of our case in
bringing them both to a better appreciation of the position of Japan
and of the vital interests she possesses in Manchuria.
Towards the Government of the Soviet Republies, which has

=== Page 69 === (confidence 0.99)
一6一
steadily maintained an attitude of impartiality and non-interference
throughout the present affair, 1 desire to express the satisfaction of
the Japanese Government.
With regard to the disarmament conference to be opened on
the 2nd of February, 1 wish to say that this government have formed
definite plans, and have given instructions accordingly to the Japanese
Representatives. This most momentous conference being the first to
be held whose deliberations will cover the entire field of armament,
involving land, sea and air forces, the world is looking eagerly forward
to its outcome. As far as concerns this government, we will, of course,
strive to carry our points, but at all events we earnestly hope that
the coming Conference will bring forth tangible results which shall be
alike rational and equitable, and so contribute to the establishment of
permanent peace throughout the world.
The spirit of progress manifested when our country was first
thrown open governs as ever our national poliey. To " seek knowledge
in the wide world," as it is expressed in the oaths taken by the Great
Emperor Meiji, is the living creed of our people. The ideal of our
diplomacy is, while maintaining our just rights, to work in harmony
with all countries and to give our nation its share in the benefits of
universal culture and enlightenment. It is with that ideal that I hope
to work and help to promote the interests of the nation and the
happiness and welfare of our people.

I have the honor to report that the Sixty First
Session of the Imperiul Diet wus convened on arch 18,
and closed on march 24. ihe Session was extraordinary
and was called for tle purpose of pussing on emergency
finuncial .leusures necessituted by the expenses of the
military op rations in china.
The circumstances Which led to the calling of this
Session are interesting and sinificant. The Diet session
in January, See desputch No.471, January 29, 1902, was
dissolved

dissolved by an Imperial Ediot before it had taken
action on the Budget bills. To meet the extraordinary
expenses occasioned by the Shanghai and Manchurian
military actions, the Cabinet had obtained the sanction
of the Emperor, after approval by the Privy Council, of
two bond issues totaling Yen 54,910,000. These were
approved as emergency measures under Article 7o of the
Constitution which provides that in case of emergenoy the
Government can undertake finuncial measures by obtaining
Imperial consent. The third request for funds to be raised
in this manner amounting to Yen 22,000,000 encountered
opposition from the Privy council.
This body stated that
there was no need for treating the bond issue as an
energency measure, pointing out that the meusure could
and should be placed before the Diet for a proval in
accordance with the provisions of the constitution.
The
elections, following dissolution of the Diet, had been
held and the Diet could be convened at uny time. The

15,000,000 of the amount requested, which would cover
ex enditures incurred pr vious to the end of the fiscal
year ( March lst), but refused to a prove the remuinder.

The Address of Mr. Yoshizawa, Minister-for
Foreign Affairs, at the 6lst Sssion
538
of the Imperial Diet.
March 22nd, 1932.
At the last session of the Diet I had occasion to
discuss the more important of the foreign questions
confronting this country. To-day I have the pleasure
of reporting, and of stating my views, on the various
phases that have since developed.
As regards the Anti-Japanese movement in China Proper,
the Japanese Government seized every opportunity to urge
upon the Chinese authorities the thorough-going suppression
of the same. However. China failed to respond, and the
movement continued with increasing intensity and persistence.
In Shanghai. where the agitation was conducted with especial
vigor, the Minkuo Jihpao, a vernacular journal of that city.
published on J mary 9th an article highly abusive of our
a
Imperial House, and on the 18th of the same month Jepanese
his
pri est and thei companione were murdered or wounded by
a Chinese mob. The two incidents were sufficient to inflame
the Japanese residents. who had long endured the ceaseless
Anti-Ja panese movements and whose patience had been well-nigh
exhausted under th trving conditions of more recent months.
The situation thur came to assume a grave aspect.
The

=== Page 80 === (confidence 0.98)
-2 -
The Japanese Consul-General at Shanghai, acting under
the instructions of the Government, presented to the Mayor
of Shanghai strong protests regarding these two incidents,
and at the same time made various demands for the settlement
of the affairs. The Mayor complied without much demur with
our demands relating to the first incident, and after some
delay and much conversation, in the end acsepted our terms
regarding the second. On receipt of the Myor's reply, at
3 c'clock on the 28th, the Japanese authorities, anticipating
that the situstion would improve. adopted an attitude of
watchfully awaiting the fulfilment of their promise on the
part of the Chinese. In the meantime. the 19th Route Army.
then concentrated in the vicinity of Shanghai, having assumed
a disquieting attitude and making a display of warlike
preparations, the Shanghai Municipal Council had decided
to take due precautions against the possitility of
disturbances being created within the Settlement by
undisciplined Chinese soldiers, or by mobs and radical
elements. Accordingly, the Council, meeting on the 28th,
declared a state of siege, to commence tla" day at 4
o'clock in the afternoon. As a result, the garcisons
of the Poverr were assigred to duty at their respective
seetors in accordnce with a pre-arvanged plan of common
defense. It va while the Japanese marines were proceeding
to tuke up their poit in the Chapei district. their allotted
sector, that the Chinene fired upon our forces, and compelled
them

=== Page 81 === (confidence 0.98)
-3 -
them to take measures of defense. Reports are sometimes
circulated making it appear that it was the Japanese marines
who commenced the fighting, in spite of the Chinese acceptance
of our terms of settlement. But the facts of the case are
precisely as I have just stated.
Moreover, we exerted every effort to prevent any
aggravation of the situation, and upon two occasions we
entered into agreements with the Chinese authorities for a
truce. But each time the agreement vas rendered nugatory
by the action of the Chinese army. Our demand, presented
on the 19th of Febrmary, that the Chinese army should withdraw
to a certain distance, was rejected oy the Chinese. Thereupon,
on the 2oth, our troops which had been dispatched as a rein-
forcement, acting in concert with the navy, undertook to
compel the withdrawal of the Chineee forces outside the
prescribed area, and completed the task on March 3rd.
As soon as the Shanghai Incident occurred. the Chinese
Government proposed to the Council of the League of Nations
to make application of Article 15 of the League Covenant
to the Sino-Japanuge conflict, and later. upon the requent
of China, the case wae transferred to thy Assembly of the
League. The Tapanese Government took ti view that the
Shanghai Incicent was a morely local affair which was not
of a nature likely to lead to a rupture, while as regards the
Manchurian Incidont, nor only had there been no renewal
of

=== Page 82 === (confidence 0.98)
- 4 -
of hostilities, but the matter was elready pending in the
hands of the Council under Article 1l of the Covenant and,
moreover, the commission of inquiry had indeed left for the
purpose of conducting investigations on the spot into the
actual conditions prevailing in China; there was therefore
no legitimate ground for applying Article 15 to either of
these cases. It was with an unequivocal reservation based
on that view that our government participated in the delibera-
tions of the Council and the Assembly. However, the Assembly
passed a resolution on March 1lth, which was in more than
one respect unacceptable to our government. On account of
this unacceptability, our representatives under the instruc-
tions of the Government, expounded the Japenese position,
made a reservation
doolared a protost embodying our contention with regard to
the application of Article 15 of the Covenant, and abstained
from voting.
In Manchuria, by reason of the special position it
occupies in relation to China Proper, and the inveterate
antagonism of the Ma churian people towaras the militarist
rule to which they had been subjected, it appe rs that
subsequently to the downfall of the erstwhile Manchurian
authorities 1 st rutumn, the efforts of the local leaders
for the maintenance of peace and order in their respective
districts radually assumed the form of a movement for
autonomy. A decl ration of independence wa: maue
a
short
time

ADDRESS OF THE MINISTER FOR FOREIGN AFFAIRS OF JAPAN, VISCOUNT
MAKOTO SAITO, AT THE OPENING OF THE DIET, JUNE 3: 1932.
894.032/108
I have the privilege of reviewing, on this occasion,
the latest developments in the foreign relations of this
country.
We are all gratified to watch the rapid restoration
of peaceful conditions in the Shanghai area following the
conclusion on May 5th of the Sino-Japanese agreement for
the cessation of hostilities. I may say that in the course
of negotiations leading up to this agreement, we encountered
not a few difficulties and that the earnest labors of the
British Minister to China and the representatives of other
friendly powers on the spot as well as our own attitude of
fairness and rectitude largely contributed to the final
happy outcome. And I desire to avail myself of this oppor-
tunity to express my sincere gratitude to the of ficers and
men of our army and navy who fought in the Snanghai area and
rendered signal service to our fatherland. I also desire to
express my profound sympathy with the victims of the ronhing
incident of April 29th and, par icularly, my infinite grief
100
at the death of General Shirakawa, Commander-in-Chief of the
expeditionary force.
According to the terms of the agreement, Chinese troops
are to remain at a prescribed distance from Shanghai and
-1-

=== Page 102 === (confidence 0.97)
-2~
Chinese forces are to cease as far as it lies within their
power all hostile actions in Suenghai and its vicinity.
Should action of the Chinese army give occasion for apprehen-
sion, the reprosentatives of the four powers, Great Britain,
the United Suates of Aerica, France and Italy, are to ascer-
ta in the situation. As long as these terns of the agreement

=== Page 113 === (confidence 0.99)

5.
Bill relating to the calculation of the value of
national bonds. The bill provides for the creation of
a standard value for all national loan bonds. It en-
ables holders of government bonds to carry them at a
fixed value, irrespective of the market price, in con-
nection vith the appraisal of properties provided for
in the Comnercial Code. It is designed to protect the
interests of the oimers of such bonds, to facilitate
the future issue of ioans, and to sustain the market
price of bonds.
Bill providing for amendments to the regulations
relating to convertible bank notes. It provides for a
partial revision of the regulations governing the tax-
free fiduciary issue by the Bank of Japan with a view to
authorizing the Bank to issue convertible notes on the
security of Government loan bonds, exchequer notes,
other securities and conmercial bills of a reliable
nature to the maximum mount of Yen 1,000,000,000 in-
stead of to the maximum amount of Yen 120,000,000 as
in the past.
7.
Bill relating to the payment of royalty by the
Bank of Jap n.
The bill provides for the creation of
a contribution or royalty to be paid to the Government
by the Bank of Japan of a sum corres ponding to the
minimum amount of money to be set aside as the reserve
fund of the Bank according to the provisions of Article 10
of the Bank of Japan Law.
This contribution or royalty
is

=== Page 114 === (confidence 0.99)

is to be paid to the Gover ment in each business term.
With the enforcement of this law, the former system,
by which the Bank must pay a tax for excess issue of
convertible notes and interest on the Government current
deposit, is abolished.
8.
Bill relating to the creation of a Board of
Councilors of the Bank of Japan, its function being
to act as an advisory body to the Governor on important
business affairs.
9.
Bill for the prevention of the outflow of capital.
This bill authorizes the Minister of Finance to take

actions or depositing of foreign currencies or contracting
of loans in foreign currencies, sale or purchase or import
of loan bonds, securities and other certificates vith a
value indicated in foreign currency, giving credit to
persons residing in foreign countries, remittances to
foreign c untries, et cetera. his measure is on the
order of an exchange control act and is intendd to
check all transactions intended to expatriate capital .

Seldom if ever has a Diet session in Japan been
conducted with such decorum and despatch. The repre-
sentatives seem to have recognized that the parties
and the parliamentary system were being weighed in
the balance of public opinion. There vere no turbulent
scenes as have marked previous sessions. A dispute bet een
the two major parties at the opening of the session
over the appointment of parliamentary vice-ministers
and councilors called dowm such a storm of criticism
in the press that the matter vas hastily patched by
compromise. The ea erness of the parties to make a
good i pression end their obvious d sire to avoid
the usual partisan bickerings ere somewhat amusing
• to observers who have watched their truculence in the
past.
The only stron criticis of the Government cane from
a proletarian representative, r. Sugiyama, Who churac-
terized it as a re ctionary ministry eibodying the
olitical p rties, the Mitsui an Mitsubishi interests
and other capitalisitc elements. In words very strong
for a Japanese he openly criticized the Army for its
interference in politics, declar d t ai the Inukai
Cabinet was virtually a robot in the hands of the
Hilitary, and even insinuated that the War Minister
was in collusion  ith the young officers involved in
the May 15th outrages.

time and energies of the legislators.

There was some discussion of plans for inmigration

The ar Minister, General Araki, was criticized

Hay 15th affair. He was asked several times to explain

the Army.
Wis replies were evasive and he attempted to
explain that the whole affair was much exaggerated. The
affair itself was still being investigated, he stated, and he
could not as yet divulge the details. He did state, how-
ever, that he regretted that army officers had been involved
in the assassination of the Prime Minister, vhich he felt
reflected disrace on the Army.

Interpellations in the House of

Baron Ida:
" I assume that the present supplementary claim
for the Manchurian affair has for its ultimate
object Japan's support to and recognition of
Manchukuo. In his recent speech before the House,
Premier Saito stated t at the existence of a new
state in Manchuria was a matter of reality and as
such this country could by no means disregard its
existence. This utterance of the Premier is a sort
of political axiom, as I take it. Replying to Mr.
Matsuoka's interpellation in the Lower House the
other day, Premier Saito stated that he wished to
grant recognition to Manchukuo as speedily as ossible,
but since the matter is a uestion of grave significance
having bearings on political and various other phases,
the Government must deliberate carefully and make an
elaborate study on the problem before it to recognize
the new State. From this statement I presume that the
Premier already acknoledges the existence of the new
State and is ready to give it formal recognition.

supplcmentary budget has an important bearing on
extending support to Manchukuo and giving it full
recognition. Therefore, the approval of the present
budget by this House would mean such recognition.

=== Page 123 === (confidence 0.98)

to be more precise, approval of the present supple-

cular point".
(
Premier Saito replied as follows:
"As I stated in the other House the other day, the
Government is anxious to recognize Manchukuo speedily.
But the matter requires some preparetory arran ements
which cannot be expedited so soon.
Mere formal re-
cognition will not do. Until after all necessary
preparatory arrangements, for instance the assurance
of protection of the safety of the Japanese in Manchuria,
et cetera, are com leted, the Government deems it pre-
mature to comply with the request of the new State for
its recognition.
The Government is now pushing on the
work of expediting such preparatory arrangements or
negotiations. It cannot do anything at present unless
those necessary arrancements re completed.
As soon
as they are finished the Government will of course pro-
ceed to give recognition. As regards the sup lementary

"Commenting on the speech made by Count Uchida,
Japanese Foreign Minister, in the Imperial Diet concern-
ing Manchuria, the NORTH CHINA DAIPEN NEWS remarks that
it is not possible to endorse the view that Japan's in-
tervention last year and the subsequent creation of the
quasi-independent state of Manchukuo represented an ex-
pression of the wishes of the poople of Manchuria.
Nor can it be admitted declares the paper that
the course of events in Manchuria suggests the sturdiness
and health of the new state of which Count Uchida dilates
though the journal adds that this does not imply a belief
that Manchukuo cannot survive its infancy or a condemnation 
of present administration at Changchun merely because it
is mainly Japanese.
Of Japanese ability to bring peace to Manchukuo
in a military sense there can be little doubt but the
original views of the Japanesc General Staff in this re-

It brings the sword once more into full view
not as a final arbiter when all methods of persuasion
have failed but s an instrument for substitution of
those effcrts at the outset. It depreciates the
validity of international agreements by making them
entirely subordinate to material welfare of one party

Count Uchida's rejedion of 'sentimental

September 15, 1932.
Tokyo's despatoh No. 110 of August 27,
1932 (by Mr. Benninghoff) gives a resume of
the first five days of the recent session
of the Japanese Diet, during which period no
bills were passed.
The despatoh reports briefly Mr.
Kaku Mori's speech advocating the "Back to
Asia" polioy whioh has already been reported
in the press here. He said that the unfortu-
nate plight of Japan can be traced to the
surrender of Japan to oocidental civilization
during the last 60 years. If Japan would
sever its conneotion with the Oocident and
devote its energy toward establishing
permanent peaoe in the East, it also might
serve as a hint to the Western nations of
their degeneration. Recognition of "Manohoukuo"
would be the first step in this direction.
The Foreign Minister stated that Japan is
determined not to compromise in its polioy
toward Manohuria and Mongolia and that the
foreign powers must realize this attitude.
In response to one of several attacks
against the Government's emergenoy relief
measures the rinance Minister said that the
total loan obligations of the Government amount

- 2 -
to almost seven billion yen. He said that it
was desirable to buy gold abroad against fu-
ture lifting of the gold embargo but that
conditions were not ripe for suoh action.
There were also interpellations with re-
gard to the assassination of Prime Minister
Inukai and the Government answered that it
hoped to make a report on the assassination
during the present session.
Mr. Seigo Nakano, a member of former
Home Minister Adaohi's new party, said that
the speeoh of the Seoretary of State (of
August 8) was a ohallenge to Japan and that it
charaoterized Japan as the aggressor in the

speeoh in the Diet.

THE ADDRESS OF COUNT UCHIDA, MINISTER FOR FOREIGN
AFFAIRS, AT THE 63RD SESSION OF THE IMPERIAL DIET
- AUGUST 25. 1932.
At the June Session of the Diet my predecessor took
occasion to report upon and discuss the outstanding
foreign relations of this country.
Today, in view of
the importance of the developments in Manchuria that have
taken place since then, I desire to review the questions
of Manchuria and China proper, and to describe in some
detail the policy of the Japanese Governnent...
We are all ratificd to note that lanchuluo has
entered upon a cureer of sturdy and healthy progress.
The Japanese Gove ment are convinced that the reconition
of this new state is the only means of stabilizing con-
ditions in MManchuria and of establishing a condition of
permanent eace in the Fur ast.
nd it is with a view
to the early extension of formal recocnition to Manchukuo
that we are ma in various arrange ents, upon the expected
com lation of wich in the near future, our plan will be
carried throuch.
However, there are those in other countries who do
not fully comprehend the attitude of Japan toward China
nor the measures she has taken since the Incident of
September 18 of last year, and who, in default of a
correct conception of the &dvent of anchukuo, look upon
any recognition of Manchukuo by Japan as an illegitimate
prooedure. In view of such misunderstanding as still
persists, I avail myself of this opportunity to clarify
Japan's position and to expound to you the reasons why
ornment consider the recognition of Manohukuo to

=== Page 151 === (confidence 0.97)
一 8 
be the only means of solving the Manohurian problem, al-
though in doing so I may have to repeat what has already
been set forth in the successive declarations and state-
ments issued in the past by the Japanese Government.
It is an indisputable fact that the ohaotio condition
of China, and the so-called revolutionary policy carried
on by China under t.e enthralling influence of an extra-
vagant political dogna, have been principally responsible
for the unfortunate turn that international relations
have taken of recent yeurs in the lar ast.
Although
Japan has been the chief vieti: of the abnormal state of
affairs in China, other countries have also suffered in-
tolerable indignities and incalculuble matorial loss. At
the sale ti e it is ud itte by those conversant with the
actual conditions in Clina that no reledy can be effocted
by having recourse either to the Covenant of the League of
Nations or to any other organ of what may be terued the
"machinery of peace". In fact, it has beon the practice
of the Powers, as has been demonstrated on innunerable oc-
casions, to repair, or prevent, injuries to their important
rights and interests in China by the direct application of
force, without relying upon those instruments of peace.
For over twenty years Japan continued to exercise
the greatest patience and moderation, in the hope that
some day China might soberly undertake the task of re-
habilitating her fortunes and playing her proper role
in the maintenance of peace in the Far East.
China
failed, however, to show any sincere desire to reciprooate
our good will and kindly sentiments, but inoreased more
than ever in her arroganoe and intolerance. Our govern-
 tock pains time and again to point out to China the
dangor

=== Page 152 === (confidence 0.98)
denger she was running in trying too far the patienee of
Japanese people. But China did not heed our warnings.
The Inoident of September 18 ocourred in Manohuria, the
very region regarded as the first bulwark of Japan, at
the precise moment when the feeling of our people had been
wrought up to the highest pitch by repeated provooations.
We had no alternative other than to resort to measures of
self-defence.
There are those who argue as though the aotion of
Japan were a violation of the Kellogc-Eriand Anti-War Pact.
But such a contention has no founuation in fact. As I have
stated, Japan hes been forced to adopt the necessary
measures for the irevention of wanton attacks upon im-
portant richts and interests vital to her national existence.
The Anti-War Pact does not put a restraint upon the exer-
The
cise of the right of self-defence in such a case.
Pact does not prohibit a signatory Power from taking at
its owm discretion whatever steps it finds imperative in
order to renove innediate menaces to its territory and its
rights and interests of whatever kind. And obviously,
the exercise of the right of self-defense may extend
beyond the territory of the Power whioh exeroises that
right.
Japan's action is essentially identical with
the action that other Powers have taken elsewhere in
similar circumstances.
Following upon this action legitimately taken by
Japan in the face of the Chinese attaoks, the officials
of the régime presided over by Chang Hsueh-liang either
flod or resigned, as you know, with the result of the
mstieal extinotion of that régime. In the meantime,
leaaing people of Manehurie, who had long re-
sontoa

=== Page 153 === (confidence 0.99)
sonted the tyranny of the Changs and were opposed to
plunging their land into the civil turmoil of China proper,
a projeot for politioal reform was already under way.
It is in every way natural that these leaders should have
taken, as they did, advantage of the opportunity afforded
by the dowmfall of Chang to launoh out upon an aotive move-
ment.
A "Peace Maintenance Connittee" was accordingly
organized in lukden, in llarbin, and in other centres. In
view of our owm responsibility in respeot of the mainten-
ance of peace and order in Manchuria we extended the
necessary co-operation to these comittees. It was the
Manohurian leaders connecteu with these conmittees who de-
cided that the hour had struck for founding a new state.
The establishment of  anchukuo was an outcome of a separatist
movement having for its background the ceo raphic and
historical peculiarities of anchuria as well as the psycho-
logical characteristics of the Manchurian people.
Those who seek to place upon Japan the resporsibility
for the Wanchurian revolution, by tracing the independence
of anchukuo directly to our military operations, simply
labour under an ignorance of the facts, and their opinions
altocether miss the point.
Again, as regards those who
fancy they detect a secret connection of some sort on the
part of Japan with the foundation of the new state, basing
their suspicions on the fact that there are in fact a
number of Japanese in the employ of the Manohukuo Government,
I need only point to the existence of many precedents for
the enlistment by a young government or a newly founded
state of the services of foreigners. Our own government,
since the Meiji Restoration, have employed many foreigners
as advisers or as regular offioials; their number, for
instanoe,

=== Page 154 === (confidence 0.99)
noe, in the year 1875 or thereabout exoceded 500.
Those who misoonstrue the presence of Japanese in the
Manchukuo Government in the fashion alluded to, are
placing the responsibility where it does not belong.
Manchukuo has come into being, as I have already
remarked, as a result of separatist movements within
China herself. Consequently, the view expressed in
certain quarters that the recognition by Japan of the
new state, thus created, would constitute a violation
of the stipulations of the Nine-Power Treaty, is in my
opinion incomprehensible. The Nine-Power Treaty does
not forbid all separatist movements in China, or debar
the Chinese in any part of the country from setting up
of their free will an independent state. Hence, should
Japan extend recognition to the existing government of
Manchukuo, founded by the will of the people of Lanchuria,
she would not thereby, as a signatory Power to the Nine-
Power Treaty, violate in any way the stipulations of that
treaty. of course, it would be a different matter on
the assumption that Japan was seeking to annex Manchuria
or otherwise to satisfy a thirst for land. Only I hardly
need to waste words in once more disclaiming at this
juncture any territorial designs on our part in Manohuria
or anywhere else.
Thus far, I have elucidated the theses that the
attitude maintained by Japan toward China, and especially
the measures we have taken since the Inoident of September
18, have been just and preper, that the independence of
been eehieved through the spontaneous will

=== Page 155 === (confidence 0.99)
the stipulations of the Nine-Power Treaty. Now let me

Government consider the recognition of Manohukuo as the

With regard to the question of finding a solution
for the Manchurian problem, the Japanese Government attaoh
the createst importance to the following two points:
First, that in seeking a satisfactory solution we should
aim at the fulfilment of the legitimate aspirations of
the Manchurian people, - at adequate guarantees for the
rights and interests of Japan, - at the prevention, in
order to make Manchuria a safe Place to live in alike for
Lanchurians and foreigners, of any recrudescence of the
erstwhile anti-foreign policy and movements, and finally
at bringing not only stability to lanchuria but permanent
peace to the Far East; secondly, that such a solution
should be effected by rejecting all ser timental propositions
and abstract theories and arrived at upon the solid basis
of the realities of the situation. In view of the ciroum-
stances that led to the outbreak of last September and the
1mmense sacrifices our country has been compelled to make
in the past, the Japanese Government feel the imp rious
need of arriving at a fundamental solution of the Manchurian
problem on the lines of these two propositions, and of
eliminating for all time the causes of the secular Sino-
Japanese oonflict.
On the other hand, it appears that in certain quarters
a plan is being considered to reaoh a solution by patching

creed enunciated in the Declaration of Independence and

The iposition upon
Manchuria of what is not wanted by anchurians would be

The

Manchukuo has set out upon an honest programme that
is open and above-board.
It has declared its intention
to pursue a policy of justice, peace and friendship toward

As lone as Manchukuo Putuin the storline spirit in

Whereas in most of such cases it

the world. It is my hope that the healthy development

osides, as the anti-

EMBASSY OF THE

Tokyo, September 7,1932.
No. 115.
SUBJECT:
Day-by-day Résumé of the 63rd Diet.
2.2
F/HS

DEPARTMENT OF BIATE

8
FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
SEP 26 1932
The Honorable
PJT
The Secretary of State,
Washington.
Sir:
Continuing my despatch: No. 110 of August 27, 1932, on
the sa e subject as the present, there follows a day-by-day
résumé of the 63rd Session of the Imperial Diet from Satur-
day August 27, 1932, through its last meeting on Sunday
Sept mber 2, 1932.
Saturday August 27.
In the House of Peers Ir. Kazue Shoda, a former Finance
Minister, violently attacked the Government's financial pro-
gram, and denounced the relief measures as mere surface-
scratching. He demanded fundamental reforms in taxation,
control

=== Page 163 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
control of the exchange situation, official loans and debt
readjustment. His speech lasted two and one-half hours.
In reply, the Premier said that he could not answer questions
relating to the Bank of Japan without investigation, but that
the relief plans already presented formed the Government's
fundamental policies in that regard. Further plans concerning
rice and silk would be presented to the Diet in December, he
said. The Finance Minister then said that exchange control was
not deemed advis able at the present time, and intimated that the
Government had more than actual financial measures to worry
about. When asked as to the policy with regard to the repayment
of national loans, he answered in general terms that the Govern-
ment is not without plans, but as they are "in the nature of a
stratagem", he thought it unwise to make them public at present.
In the House of Representatires the Law of Mediation of
Debts was discussed first, (see Embassy's despatch No. llo of
August 27, 1932). The Seiyukai and Adachi's party were against
it for various reasons, while the Minseito supported it. It was
put before a committee of 18. Other measures dis cussed were the
bill for the readjustment of debts of agricultural guilds, and
the compulsory education loan bill, which after argument pro and
con were put in the hands of committees, together with opposition
bills of like character..
In the evening, the Cabinet decided to prolong the Session
of the Diet by three days, or through September 2nd.
Sunday August 28.
The House of Peers met in secret session in the morning to
hear a report on the Inukai Assassination. Little is known as
to what transpired, but it is believed that there was no parti-
cular opposition to what was said. In the afternoon the finan-
cial

=== Page 164 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
oial polioy of the Government was criticized, but no action
taken.
In the House of Representatives there was no plenary
session, the budget committee spending all day in discussion
of the relief measures. The Finance Minister withstood a
barrage of questions. In answer to which he indicated that the
Government had no intention of flooding the country with cur-
rency. He also defended the official banks, said that the De-
posits Bureau has no intention of selling its bonds to make
more money, and refused to promise any further reduction in
the Bank of Japan's interest rate. He asserted that artificial
control of the yen would be harmful, and said that loss of con-
fidence within Japan brought about its decline. Most of the op-
position came from the Seiyukai, and the Finance Minister, once
a Seiyukai Premier, was harsh toward his quondam associates.
The rice control law revision also came in for attack, the
Minister for Agriculture and Forestry answering.
Monday August 29.
The House of Peers did not meet in plenary session, a meet-
ing of the committee on petitions being the only activity for
the day.
The House of Representatives met in the morning, and various
members' bills were presented, but nothing of importance trans-
pired. Various committee meetings took place in the afternoon.
However, a sharp conflict broke out between the Government
and the Seiyukai over the Rice and Cereal Law. As at present
constituted

=== Page 165 === (confidence 1.00)
constituted, this law provides a high and low price for the
year, the Government buying and selling just enough to keep
the price levels within a predetermined range. Although the
Government for a time advocated a change in this system, it
changed its mind, thereby rousing the ire of the Seiyukai,
which proposes that the Government buy as much as possible and
store it, which practice would keep the price of rice at a
high level.
Though it was rumored that the violent opposition thus
engendered might lead to a possible resignation of the Cabinet
or dissolution of the Diet, competent observers thought that
public opinion would not allow the Seiyukai to bring about any-
thing which would postpone the emergency relief legislation so
seriously needed by the country. It was possible that, in order
to save the face of the Seiyukai, the Minister of Agriculture
and Forestry, Mr. Fumio Goto, might resign and be replaced by a
Seiyukai man.
Tuesday August 30.
The House of Peers met only in various comnittees; nothing
of importance appears to have transpired.
The House of Representatives attempted to meet in the morn-
ing, but could not do so due to a Seiyukai obstruction creating
a lack of a quorum. It succeeded in meeting, however, in the
afternoon and listened to several private bills, all being re-
ferred to committees.
The most important meeting was that of the budget committee.
The Foreign Minister, in answer to an inquiry, said that he was
not in a position to announce the future moves of the Government,
but that steps were being taken with regard to a possible defeat
of Japan at the Assembly of the League; he thought that fore ign
reactions

=== Page 166 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
reactions to his speech were showing that other countries were
beginning to understand better the position of Japan. When
asked what Japan would do if opposed by all the world on its
"Manchukuo" recognition, he said that adequate steps would be
taken, and that the people of Japan were prepared to meet any
eventuality.
After further discussion regarding Manchuria and China, it
was asked whether bandit depredations in South Manchuria, pre-
01
sumably at the instigation of Chang Hsueh-liang, necessitated
an advance on Jehol. The Minister of War answered that "it was
not easy to settle matters of that kind because of the inter-
national questions involved", and that "relations with China
will have to be considered in making any decision in the matter".
Following the noon recess various que stions concerning the
alleged insincerity of the Government regarding relief, and de-
fects in the proposed program were put, which were answered more
or less evasively and in general terms. The meeting again turned
its attention to Manchuria, and the Minister of War again said;
"There is no knowing when a crisis will occur in relations with
other countries. So the military authorities are pushing on with
preparations to meet any emergency. On the other hand, we are
working in concert with the authorities of the Foreign Office
for the prevention of a situation which might involve us with
foreign countries. We are acting on the conviction that it is
best to be fully prepared against any emergency."(JAPAN ADVERTISER
translation).
The meeting then again discussed the relief measures, and
the several Ministers were interpellated regarding the working of
the various sections of the Government's proposals.
Wednesday

=== Page 167 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
Wednesday August 31.
The House of Peers did little, as the Government's bills
were still being debated in the Lower House. The budget com-
mittee, because of the importance of the subject, was increased
from nine to fifteen members. The revision of the Natural Dis-
aster Relief Fund Law was passed, it being introduced here first
to save time. It was sent to the Lower House for consideration.
The House of Representatives finally succeeded in passing
a number of bills. The four budget and extra-budgetary finan-
cial bills were passed unamended, but with a resolution calling
onthe government to exercise great care in their administration,
and advising that the three-year plan be changed to a two year
plan. Details of these bills are not yet available.
The Lower House also passed the extraordinary State subsi-
dy to primary schools without amendment; then it passed the much
moot ed revision of the special account law relating to Rice.
There had been more discussion regarding this proposal of the
Government than on any other, and the bill as finally passed was
amended in such a form that the Government, through the Minister
of Agriculture and Forestry, could not agrec to it. The Seiyukai,
however, had a majority, and the anended bill was passed without
undue trouble. It was e xpected by the evening papers that some
sort of compromise would be patched up, and that a further re-
vision would be made in the House of Peers following a compro-
mise of a joint committee.
Thursday September 1.
The House of Peers opened in the morning with a speech on
finances by the Finance Minister. He said nothing new. Several
bills which had passed the Lower House the previous day were de-
bated on for a short time, and then referred to committees.
The

=== Page 168 === (confidence 0.99)
- 7 -
The House of Representatives,meeting in the afternoon,
passed a law for the control of the silk-reeling industry,
amended; a Bill relating to special monetary accommodation
by the Central Bank of Cooperative Societies, and a Government
guarantee for the losses incurred by said bank through such
special accommodation, not amended; a Bill for the partial re-
vision of the Law for the control of Cooperative Societies,
slightly amended; a Bill partially revising the law controling
the Central Bank for Cooperative Societies, unamended; a Bill
concerning monetary accommodation on immovable property, the
Government to guarantee a certain per cent of the loss; a Bill
revising a law of the previous session regarding the flotation
of national loans, necessary for the supplementary budget; a
Bill for additional control of commercial guilds or associations;
a Bill controling the issue of presentation tickets, presumably
for taxation purposes; a Bill providing for the mediation of the
Law Courts for small amount debts.
Frid ay September 2.
In the House of Peers the four Government bills relating to
the budget were passed after being approved by a committee ap-
pointed to examine them. They thus became law, and were trans-
mitted to the Emperor for Imperial sanction. Other bills which
had been ent up from the Lower House were referred to committees.
During the course of the discussion it was pointed out that the
relief plans were inadequate. The Government bills relating to
agrarian debt adjastment and mediation of debts by the law courts,
which had been passed by the Lower House greatly anended, were
discussed at great length, the Minister of Agriculture and
Forestry stating that the Government could in no way accept the
amendments

=== Page 169 === (confidence 0.99)
- 8 -
amendments which had been made.
The House of Representatives did nothing important, and
little interest was shown in the session. Petitions were handled.
Late in the day it was announced that the Session of the
Diet had been prolonged for an other day.
Saturday September 3.
The day was spent by both houses in many sessions and
recesses, interpersed with joint committee meetings to try to
discover com romise measures on the rice and the debt adjust-
ment bills which had passed the Lower House. The Upper House
during the day passed its own amendments to those bills, but
they were defeated in the Lower House. Sessions continued in-
termittently during the day, until at ll p.m. the Speaker of
the Lower House anounced that the Session had been extended
another day, September 4.
All bills other than those in dispute were passed early
in the morning by the Upper House as brought from the Lower
House. The only bills left, therefore, to be considered during
the next day were the debt mediation bill and the debt adjust-
ment bill, which hinge on each other, and the rice bill. Whether
or not some sort of compromise could be effected was doubted.
Sunday September 4.
The Sessions of both houses again lasted almost until mid-
night, with frequent recesses for joint conferences. It finally
turned out that the Government's rice bill, with but slight
amendments, passed both houses and became law, while the debt
adjustment bill was rejected by the Upper House in the farm
passed by the joint committee, and thus was shelved. The debt
mediation

=== Page 170 === (confidence 0.99)
-9 -
mediation bill, in an amended form, was finally passed, but
its agrarian debt provisions, which hinged on the working of
the rejected bill, were naturally rejected.
As had been anticipated, the Diet session was not a vio-
lent one. There was only one case of a member being attacked
by opposition members, a rather remarkable record. The chief
feature of the Session was the opposition of the Seiyukai to
two of the Government's proposals. At first the Rice Control
Bill was attacked, and virtually a new bill was passed in the
Lower House by the Seiyukai majority. This party, as is well
known, has its strength in the rural districts, and the effect
of its bill would have been to force the price of rice as high
as possible, thus benefiting the farmers. This bill, however,
had in it more than a hint of speculation, and even involved
a system of lotteries. It was accordingly bitterly opposed in
the more conservative Upper House, while the vernacular press
editorially excoriated the Seiyukai for obstructing relief
measures by playing party politics and even, through the specu-
lation provisions, endangering the public moneys. The Rice
Control Bill which finally passed the Upper House was practically
the same as that which was originally introduced by the Govern-
ment, and the Lower House was forced, by public opinion more
than anything else, to back down and agree.
However, during the later days of the Session, when it be-
came more and more apparent that the Government's Rice Control
Bill would eventually be passed, the Seiyukai attacked the
Agrarian Debt Adjustment Bill. A compromise on this was never
reached, and it was finally shelved by the Upper House, which
could not agree to the amendments placed therein by the Lower
House

House.

as follows:
l. A Bill for the temporary increase of the state subsidy

incurred by the bank.

along all lines of her constructive work.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
February 2ê, 1934.
RCM:
Tokyo's despatoh No. 673 of February 10
1934, comments on the proceedings of the
Diet since it reconvened on January 23 after
the New Year holidays. The Embassy promises!
to send separate reports on the various
matters which have been engaging the attentin
of the Diet, but I believe the despatoh would
have been far more helpful if it had pro-
vided a narrative of events.
The despatch is nevertheless an
important one and should be read in full.
The attack in the Diet on the military -
element has apparently been more vigorous and
'extensive than press despatches from Tokyo
have indicated. Mr. Grew states that the
younger officcrs of the Army and Navy "are
indignant over the attitude of the political
parties because of their merciless bombard-
ment of questions dealing with military
matters". (Page 4).
The despatch does not appear to call
for comment or explanation.
That there has been more opposition to
the military than is displayed in the Diet
is

=== Page 207 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
is confirmed by the first enclosure, an
editorial from the JAPAN ADVERTISER.
A particularly interesting clipping is
the editorial of the AsAHI (No. 3). which.
while it believes complete confidence is
placed by the people in the Army and Navy.
deplores the efforts of those who "clamor
about the 1935-36 crisis".
One of the most important clippings is
an account of the proceedings in the Diet of
January 26 (No. 6), which nepas a
discussion on naval matters. The Navy
Minister indicated that no deoision had been
reached with regard to the serving of notice
of withdrawal from the Washington and London
Naval Treaties. Sec aloo, No. 32
A clipping from the JAPAN ADVERTISER of
January 31 contains the text of a bill
designed to afford protection against dumping
of foreign wheat in Japan. The statement is
made that the Japanese Government"is watching
with some trepidation the operations of the
wheat export indemnification law recently
passed in the United States".
The account given in Clipping No. 14
of Mr. Yoshizawa's discussion of diplomatic
affairs will repay reading in full. Mr.
Yoshizawa advocates, in view of the naval
policies of the principal countries, that the
forthcoming

=== Page 208 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 3 -
forthgoming naval conference be abandoned.
After referring to the relations between Japar
and Russia, which he described as giving much
concern in a "third country", Mr. Yoshizawa
expresses the opinion that there are no
serious problems pending between the two
countries. The reply of Mr. Hirota is not
without interest. It indicates that the
Japanese Government will oppose the
disoussion of Far Eastern matters at the
forthcoming naval conference, if held.
Clipping No. 20 disousses the foreign re-
action to Mr. Hirota's annual editorial on
foreign relations. It points out that
Count Uchida's policy was to risk the
national existence rather than abandon
Japan's vital interests in China; that Mr.
Hirota has adopted a new policy designed to
meet existing conditions; and that the
inability of foreign countries to understand
Mr. Hirota's policy may be explained by the
fact that the change has been too abrupt.
The editorial advocates that Japan prooeed
slowly about cooperation with other
countries.

THE UNDER SECRETARY
