The Honorable
COPIES SENT TO
The Secretary of State,
O.N.IIP
Washington.
S i r:
I have the honor to inform the Department that the
65th session of the Diet reconvened on January 23rd. The
Embassy plans, at the close of the present session, to
review in separate despatches various matters which ae
FILED
receiving the Diet's attention. In the meantime the
3
ceedings up to the present writing may be of interest
and are set forth as follows.
As soon as the Diet reconvened after the New Year
holidays

=== Page 210 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
holidays it became apparent that this session would
not be a repetition of previous sessions in which the
legislators followed the dictates of the military. On
the contrary, the Government came in for a general at-
tack from individual members of both the Minseito and
Seiyukai, the two major political parties, although
there was no definite attack on the Government by the
parties acting as such.
This attack was directed chiefly against the mili-
tary, and the Government was thereby immediately placed
on the defensive by interpellations in connection with
the huge budget for military purposes, and in general for
the reduction in the budget for farm relief in favor of
the military. The attempt has been made by the speakers
to force the Government to admit that the military has
monopolized the budget to the exclusion of appropriations
for farm relief; so far the Government has avoided a
direct reply to these interpellations, but the implication
of the speeches and re lies indicates a tacit admission
that the military has unduly benefitted. The farm prob-
lem is important partly because of the actual distress
in rural communities, and partly because a large number
of parliamentary members represent the country districts.
Foreign Minister Hirota has won much admiration for
his straightforward and moderate replies to questions con-
cerning foreign relations. It is obvious that his is a
policy of peace.and, as far as possible, conciliation.
In the military budget discussions the question of
the

=== Page 211 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
the so-called "crisis" in Japan's foreign relations in
1935-36 has been severely criticized, the opposition
contending that the constant cry of "crisis" is liable
to cause a feeling of unrest among the people, affect
the economic world and bring about disadvantages to
Japan in its diplomatic relations. Mr. Hirota, the
Minister for Foreign Affairs, in replying to these
statements said that the talk of a "crisis" in 1935- 36
is regrettable as it invites the contempt of foreign
countries and that he believes Japan should be calm and
take steps to bring about smooth and satisfactory inter-
national relations. This statement is in line with Mr.
Hirota's announced policy to improve Japan's relations
with foreign countries through diplomatic channels.
The attitude of Japan at the forthcoming Naval Con-
ference in 1935-36 was also the subject of questions. In
the course of interpellations on this subject, the Govern-
ment stated that Japan's policy toward this conference
is still in the process of formation. The Foreign Minis-
ter, however, stated that he regards the present naval
ratios as being unsatisfactory and Admiral Osumi, the
Navy Minister, was induced by interpellation to reiterate
the Navy's intention of demanding a change in the present
naval ratios.
These attacks on the military apparently caught them
unaware, because it took them several days to prepare a
general counter-attack. This counter-attack was led by
Admiral Osumi, the Navy Minister, who in a statement in
the

=== Page 212 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
the Diet said that the Navy was guiltless of any disad-
vantages Japan may suffer under the 5-5-3 ratio of the
London Naval Treaty, implying under interrogation that
the Minseito was responsible for the pact. He likewise
said that continued inquisitiveness in the Diet on
naval matters would bring disclosures from the military
side concerning the charge that a movement was under way
to alienate the civilian and military populations, and
that such disclosures would make matters extremely un-
comfortable for the Minseito leaders. Regardless of
this threat the Minseito and Seiyukai continued their in-
terpellations on military matters.
The younger elements of the Army and Navy are in-
dignant over the attitude of the political parties be-
cause of their merciless bombardment of questions dealing
with military matters. They have held several meetings
urging that a firm stand be taken by the higher authori-
ties. Whether they will take further action, and in what
form, remains to be seen.
In regard to the finances of Japan, Mr. Takahashi,
the Finance Minister, stated in the Diet that Japan is in
no hurry to revalue the yen, but that Japan intends to
await the practical stabilization of both the pound and
the dollar before action is taken. He stated that the
Government does not intend to move hastily in the matter
because the United States or Great Britain might modify
its attitude on the currency and this would leave Japan
with

=== Page 213 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
with a currency too strong to compete in foreign fields.
On the question of taxation Minister Takahashi stated
that the time is not ripe for increasing taxes. It is
generally admitted that the farmers, who are the largest
tax-payers in Japan, are now being taxed to the limit
and that to further increase their taxes at the present
time would only invite trouble from the rural districts.
The interpellations on the Government's bill for
amalgamating the steel industry of Japan into one Govern-
ment-controlled concern is believed to have caused the
resignation of Baron Nakajima, the Minister of Commerce
and Industry, it being alleged that he is closely connect-
ed with the steel interests.
Not since September 193l have members of the Diet
dared to attack the military as is being done in the pre-
sent session, and this attack is characterized by the
close cooperation between the two major political parties
as their interpellations have followed along the same
general lines as if by concerted action. At the same time
the Ministers of State have made much less evasive replies
to the interpellations than they have in any other recent
session.
The foregoing report is not intended to convey the
impression that the present Government is in danger or
that the budget as prepared will not be voted. It merely
indicates that members of the Diet have seized this op-
portunity to air their views and grievances with a frank-
ness and freedom which they have not dared or seen fit
to

=== Page 214 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
to exerise since the Manchurian incident of 1931.
11.
There are transmitted herewith clippings selected
from local English language newspapers in regard to the
proceedings in the Diet now in session. Clippings in
connection with the Diet have been omitted from the usual
clippings submitted bi-weekly.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew

DIET HEARS CHARGE
PLEDGE OF ECONOMY
IN ARMS IS VIOLATED
Saito Accused on Floor of Lower
House of Breaking Promise
on Navy Budget
MILITARY IS UNDER FIRE
Premier Replies that Interna-
tional Relations Have Changed
Since Last Diet
SECRET SESSION IS HELD
Insistent in his questions as to why
the Government's previous assurances
against increased military expenses have
not been realized for the coming fiscal
year. Mr. Shinya Uchida of the Seiyu-
kai yesterday in the Diet launched a
keen interpellation on national defense
matters, eliciting numerous replies from
Premier Saito and Navy Minister Mi-
neo Osumi. and finally bringing about
a half-hour session in camera to enable
Admiral Osumi to answer queries in-
volving naval secrets.
Premier Saito. according to Nippon
Dempo. was accused by Mr. Uchida of
"deceiving the people at the last Diet
session by telling them that military
expenses would not increase, while he
was aware of the navy's intention to
formulate the second replenishment
program." The Premier replied that
the world situation had changed.
During the course of an extremely
lively day of question and answer, For-
eign Minister Koki Hirota divulged that
the Soviet Government had expressed
regret over the recent "disclosure" of
"secret documents" tending to reveal a
Japanese plan to seize the Chinese East-
ern Railway by force.
Osumi Can't Reply
The session went into camera after
an especially brisk exchange between
Mr. Uchida and Navy Minister Osumi.
Quoting the Navy Minister to the ef-
fect that appropriations allowed in the
new draft budget for the second re-
plenishment program are not sufficient.
the interpellator asked just how much
would be needed. The Navy Minister
replied that the navy. of course, had
its estirnates, but that he was unable
to reveal them pending the conclusion
of present negotiations between the
Navy Office and the Finance Office.
Dissatisfied with the reply. Mr. Uchi-
da reauested the session iu camera

During the course of an extremely

the Soviet Government had expressed

Osumi Can't Reply
The session went into camera after
an especially brisk exchange between
Mr. Uchida and Navy Minister Osumi.
Quoting the Navy Minister to the ef-
fect that appropriations allowed in the
new draft budget for the second re-
plenishment program are not sufficient,
the interpallator asked just how much
would be needed. The Navy Minister
replied that the navy, of course, had
its estimatos, but that he was unable
to reveal them pending the oonclusion
of present negdtiations between the
Navy Office and the Finance Office.
Dissatisfied with the reply, Mr. Uchi-
da requested the session in camera.
It was the second day on which the
Government has been roundly interro-
gated on military matters by members
of the Seiyukai and the Minseito, a
circumstance which is said to have
aroused the quick resentment of the
military element, causing preparations
for a counter-attack in the Diet if the
civilians continue their line of ques-
tioning.
The House of Peers was adjourned
yesterday so that its members might
attend the Lower House's first session
on the budget. Government bills for
general and special appropriations for
the 1934-35 fiscal year and the measure
providing for expenses not included in
the Cabinet draft budget being pre-
sented. Mr. Masataka Ota of the Sei-
yukai, Mr. Mitsugu Tanaka of the
Minseito and Mr. Hikosaburo Kurihara
of the Kokumin-Domei made formal
requests that the Government submit
data for reference, then Mr. Uchida be-
gan the naval interrogation that was to
consume most of the session.
Conditions Change
"At the previous session of the
Diet." the interpellator began, "the
Premier declared that expenditures for
military preparations would not be in-
creased in the future, but the budget
for the next fiscal year shows that
expenditures of the army and navy are
to be increased by 90.000.000. What
is the reason?"
"The developing situation forced a
change in plans," Premier Saito re-
plied. "Military expenditures must be
increased to cope with new interna-
tional situation. The change in the
situation could not be foreseen at the
time of the previous Diet session."
Thereafter question and answer, ac-
cording to Rengo's translation. ran as
follows:
Mr. Uchida: The Premier stated at
the opening of this plenary session of
the Diet that Japan's friendly rela-
tions with the Powers had increased.
and was continuing to increase. I
would like comment now on that
statement.
The Premier: Our relations with
the Powers are improving. but nation-
al defense is not based entirely on
discord with the Powers. It should
be carried out after taking the de-
fense programs of the various Powers
into consideration.
Mr. Uchida: Was the second naval
replenishment plan anticipated when
the first replenishment plan was
drafted?
Navy Minister Osumi: The second
replenishment plan is almost the same
as the program considered when the
first plan was formulated, but in some
respects it differs.
Mr. Uchida: According to the Navy
Minister. the second supplementary
plan was considered together with the
first. In that case. the second plan
should have been decided when the
budget for the previous fiseal year was
formed.
The Premier: It might have been
considered by the navy. but the Gov-
ernment had no knowledge of it.
Mr. Uchida: The fact that military
expenditures increased in spite of the

The Hochi
Translated by the Japan Advertiser
Tokyo, Wednesday, January 31, 1y34.
Diet Politics Reviving
HOCHI
Diet politics has revived. It has parti-
ally revived, but not wholly, as may
be seen from the progress of the situa-
tion in the Diet. Attacks have been
directed against military discipline,
but the answers of the War and Navy
Ministers have not been sufficiently
thorough to satisfy the people at large.
They simply referred to the military
criminal law. which prohibits officers
on the active list from participating
in politics. When officers break the
law, they said, they are punished. The
Ministers seem to attach importance
to legal matters, but the spiritual signi-
ficance of the question is far greater.
They quoted the Imperial Rescript
awarded to the soldiers and sailors by
the late Emperor Meiji in connec-
tion with participation in politics. The
article which says that "soldiers and
sailors should refrain from politics" is
much dcbated. Its application depends
upon its interpretation, and there is
wide variance of interpretation, depend-
ing on who is doing the interpreting. At
any rate the article is being discussed
seriously. The Diet and the military
must settle the matter. In our opin-
ion. however, the late Emperor Meiji
must have had but one view and ex-
pressed it simply in the Rescript. There
is no reason for the meaning to differ
according to time and circumstances.
Its true meaning must be reffected as
from a clear mirror on the minds of
all soldiers and sailors from the Field-
Marshals and Fleet-Admirals down.
What do the War Minister and the
Navy Minister think of this? It is im-
portant that the Diet have more de-
finite replies from these responsible
Ministers. The military must keep in
touch with active polities. but it must
do so through official channels. There
must be a clear line of distinction be-
tween the military as a whole and offli-
cialdon.
Another seriots issue on the floor of
the Diet is corruption of oflicial dis-
cipline. in which one Cabinet Minister
i, alleged to be involved. It is a
matter of special regret that a mem-
ber of the present Cabinet is suspect-
ed. for the Cabinet stands for purifica-
tien of the political atmosphere. As
it has been exposed in the Diet, the
matter must be discussed thoroughly
and settled. Party Cabinets used to
face this sort of problem with com-
parative frequency. but it is surpris-
ing to see the Saito Cabinet, one of
whose important missions is political
purification. besmirched in the same
manner. The present scandal may be
somewhat different from past seandals.
In the past. divergence of political
opinion would probably have given the
discussion serious proportions. At any
rate. the discussion must be thorough
in order to lift suspicion from the
Cabinet Minister involved.
We also hope that the Diet will take
up corruption of public morals. which
has of late been increasing. Though
the matter may have no diréct bearing
on politics. it affects public discipline.
It is entirely too important to be ignor-
ed by the Diet.

sonous substances flowing into a riv-
er from the Ashio copper mine. He
has brought up this subject at every
session .or years.
Mr. Kurihara:
"The cor per mine
gives off poisonous mud and sand.
containing arsenic acid. to the quanti-
ty of 700.000 tons annually. When it
rains this poisonous River, causing
flows into the Watarase River, causing
considerable damage in Gumma, To-
chigi and other Prefectures through
which the river flows. Is the Govern-
ment holding the mine operators re-
sponsible for this. or is the Govern-
ment responsible?"
Mr. Takao Saito. Parliamentary Vice.
Minister of Home Affairs, replied: "The
Government and the operators are
spending more than ¥300,000 annually
on measures to prevent poisonous sub-
stances from being introduced into
the stream.

before the congress of the Communist
Party, in which he stressed the need
of strengthening defenses in the Far

The Mishi-Michi
Trammlated by the Japan Aavertiser
Tokyo, Saturday, February 3, 19:4.
Yoshizawa Encourages Hirota
NICHI NICHI
The questions put by Mr. Kenkichi
Yoshizawa, former Foreign Minister
and leading member of the Seiyukai.
to Foreign Minister Koki Hirota on
questions of foreign policy in the
House of Peers the other day and the
Ministers replies were very informing.
They served to diminish the anxiety
which is felt by the pople about the
future 1osition of their country in in-
ternational politics.
Mr. Yoshizawa dwelt at length on
the international position of Japan.
Regarding the coming Washington naval
conference, he said that Japan is en-
titled to a higher naval ratio with
America and gave the reasons for
demanding upward revision of the ratio
granted in the London and Washing-
ton naval treaties. He urged on the
Government the necessity of making
diplomatic efforts toward the conclu-
sion of agreements with foreign coun-
tries in this matter.
The Japanese-Soviet situation recelv-
ed a large share of the attention of the
former Foreign Minister. He deplored
the alarmist rumors about future re-
lations between Japan and the Soviet
Union and declared that he could see
nothing about which the two must
fight. He said in effect that the best
interests of Japan demand that every.
thing should be avoided by the Gov-
erninent and people which would caus
misunderstanding in foreign countries.
Mr. Yoshizawa favored stregthening
of national defense. But in the same
breath insisted that diplomatic efforts
should not be neglected.
Mr. Yoshizawa's speech was in per-
feet accord with the statements of
I olicy which have been repeatedly made
by Foreign Minister Hirota. Thus the
speech may be taken as an attempt
to encourage the Foreign Minister in
his policy.
It is easy to see the impression Mr.
Yoshizawa wanted to convey. It is
that Japan must try to improve its
international position by diplomatic
means. It was in the nature of things
that the Foreign Minister expressed
complete agreement with the viws of
his interpellator. In the course of his
reply. he warned the people against
worrying too much about the future
of their country. The Minister said
that worries harm nations as to in-
dividuals. He repeated in different
language what he had said earlier in
deprecating the tendency for a section
of the public to talk about the 1935-36
crisis.
The guiding principle of Mr. Hirota's
policy is that Japan must act in ac-
cordance with the the dictates of justice
and that it must keep within bounds,
Guided by this principle. Japan may
hope to surmount the obstacles that
lie in its way.

The Hochi
Translated by the Japan Advertiser
Tokyo, Sunday, Febryary 4, 1934.
Diet Politics Picking Up
HOCHI
The Diet is regaining its former pre-
stige. Even those who used to abhor
Diet politics are beginning to see it
in a new light and to admire the
stand of the Diet against the various
weaknesses of the Government. This
fair attitude is increasing. So far in
the present session there has been only
one unpleasant incident, the interpel-
lation by a Seiyukai member about
the London naval treaty and the
naval replenishment program. which
.was unpleasant because the aim
seemed to be to put the Minseito
in a bad light. It was like the speech
of Dr. Kisaburo Suzuki. president of
the Seiyukai, on the London naval
treaty last fall, which made much
trouble for the party. An attitude of
this nature only affords a good (x-
cuse for outsiders to attack the politi-
cal parties. The parties need to com-
bine against their common enemy in-
stead of quarreling among themselves.
The Minseito have shown themselves
wise by avoiding entanglement in the
interpellation of the Seiyukai member.
It is to be hoped that the favorable
atmosphere of the Diet will continue
throughout the session.
The best thing that the Diet has done
so far is its correction of the general
notion that there is an emergency that
will reach its crisis in 1935 and 1936.
The Cabinet Ministers and Diet mem-
bers have agreed that Japan's inter-
naticnal difficulties must be :olved by
peaceful means. This is a very good
policy. and it should favorably im-
press the Powers. No one in the pres-
ent session has advocated the precipi-
ate policy of Count Yasuya Uchida.
the former Foreign Minister. Sound
commonsense is taking the place of the
extreme ideas that carried everything
before them a ycar ago.
Credit is also due to the Diet for
the manner in which the iron merger
has been discussed. This does not
mean, however, that the Minister of
Commerce and Industry, Baron Kuma-
kichi Nakajima. has been reasonable.
That is something else again. What
we mean is that the actions of a limit-
ed few were criticized. It has been
charged that the interpellation by Mr.
Mannoshin Kamiyama in the House of
Peers lacked substance. It would not
be fair to expect him to deliver an
impeachment speech. however, for the
facts of the merger issue have not yet
been clarified. There is no need for
impeachment at this nioment. Wha*
we want is clarification of what has
taken place. Only as a last resort
should judgment be passed on Baron
Nakajima as a State Minister. For
the Diet to launch reckless attacks
merely for the sake of attacking
would invite loss of credit.
We are satisfied with the atitudes of
Foreign Minister Koki Hirota and War
Minister General Senjuro Hayashi. Mr.
Hirota has done well to express his
views without the least sign of cater-
ing to the public. General Hayashi
has answered questions simply but
i clearly.

Tokyo, Sunday, February 4, 1934.
MINISTRY CRITICIZED
BY KOKUMIN DOMEI
Adachi's Party Issues Statement
Deploring Delay in Introduc-
ing Important Bills
THIRD OF SESSION ELAPSED
Government Warned Its Attitude
May Cause People to Doubt
Loyalty to Parliamentarism
Criticism of the Government and the
other political partics in the Diet on
the score that no bills of any impor-
tance have yet been taken up in the
present session. was voired by leaders
of the Kokumin Domei. or National
League, Mr. Kenzo Adachi's political
party. at a meeting held Friday aftcr-
noon. The meeting took place in the
Diet building and was a private one.
The Asahi reported that the principa!
subject discussed was the attitude c.
the Government and of the two major
political parties. the Seiyukai and the
Minseito. All the Kokumin Domei
eaders. it was reported. agrecd in
criticizing the manner in which the
Government. with the assistance of the
Seiyukai and Minseito. is handling af-
tairs in the Diet.
Following the meeting a statement
was issued in the name of the party.
It began by pointing out that about
one-third of the present session of the
Diet already has elapsed. Yet. it adds.
no bills relating to farm relief. to re-
vision of the election law or to other
inportant matters have been laid be-
fore the Diet, despite a previous pro-
mise by the Government that such
measures would be introduced during
the present session.
"There is no excuse for the Govern.
ment's delay in introducing important
measures." the statement contirued.
"As a result of this neglect of its
duty by the Government. the Lower
House has been left with no important
bills to discuss. How are we to ex-
1 lain this situation except on the
theory that the Government is indif-
ferent to the wishes of the people.
being concerned only with temporiz-
ing?
"We hereby call the attention of the
Government and of the two major
political parties which are supporting
it to the fact that their attitude in
the Diet is contrary to their profes-
sions of loyalty to the cause of rarlia-
mentary government. We also warn
them that this attitude is giving cause
to the people to doubt whether the
Diet is performing its functions pro-
perly."
The Kokumin Domei leaders during
the meeting also discussed the attitude
of the party toward the bill revising
the peace preservation law. which was
sheduled to be introduced into the
Diet yesterday. It was decided that
Mr. Yojiro Matsutani should speak for
the party when debate on the mea-
ure was held.

COMMENT STRESSES
Said Mr. Naoyoshi Tsukazaki, one of
the defense attorneys in the naval
court-martial at Yokosuka:
"These defendants, who had planned
SEVERITY OF TERMS
such a great incident, were surely
prepared to meet death in carrying
out their deed. Therefore I believe
that the defendants themselves will
not raise the question of the severity
of their sentences. Such a view can
Difference Cited in Punishment
be gathered from their testimony and
Imposed on Civilians Com-

as delivered, however, I am unable to

was disappointed to see that the court

great attitude of the defendants.

One of the first scenes of public
comment on the sentences was the
Lower House of the Diet. There Mr.
Kazuo Nagai, Seiyukai member. took
oce sion to point out that the civil of-
fenders in the May 15 outrages seemed
to have been subjected to heavier
punishment than the service men. He
expressed fear that such a difference
in punishment would have an "un-
desired effect" upon public thought.
Premier Saito and Justice Minister
Matsukichi Koyama replied vaguely
that close attention would be given to
the matter. The War Minister. Gen-
cral Senjuro Hayashi. replied that it
was necessary for the army to have
military administrative powers, and the
Navy Minister, Admiral Mineo Osumi,
retorted that he could not coment on
the decision of a court-martial holding
independent authority.
Mr. Hanzo Yamada, an attorney who
defended the army conspirators at the
1st Division court-matial, was quoted
by Rengo as commenting:
"As I did not attend the trial of the
civilian defendants I am not in a posi-
tion to comment in any detail. How-
ever. I have a general idea of the case
because the incident centered more or
less around the army defcndants. The
severity of the sentences imposed by
the civil court can be commented upon
from various angles. I personally think
the sentences were too heavy. This
was especially true in the case of the
defendant Takeshi Ikematsu. He was
involved to an extent similar to the
army cadets who were tried by court-
martial. Yet, Ikematsu's sentence is
15 years. compared with four years
imposed on the army cadets. This is
too heavy a sentence and is to be reg-
retted."

ADMIRAL'S MENTION
OF LINDBERGH VISIT
DRAWS FIRE IN DIET
Osumi Says Reference to Lind-
bergh as Spy, Credited to
Suetsugu, Went too Far
NEWS 'LEAK' IS CHARGED
Member Claims Amnesty Publi-
shed Without Announcement
from Official Quarters
INDIAN
TARIFF
DEBATED
Published charges attributed to Vice-
Admiral Nobumasa Suetsugu that Co-
lonel Charles A. Lindbergh may have
made his flight to Japan as an Ameri-
can spy "seem to have gone too far."
Navy Minister Mineo Osumi declared
in the Lower House of the Diet yes-
terday afternoon under a keen inter-
pellation from Mr. Kikuo Kiyose, Sei-
yukai.
The statement appeared in an in-
terview with Vice-Admiral Suctsugu.
Commander-in-Chief of the Combined
Fleet. in the magazine Gendai, and
created exeitement both here and in
the United States. coupled as it was
with conjectures as to eventualities in
a possible war between Japan and
America.
"This article, attributed to Vice-Ad-
miral Suetsugu. has caused a great
sensation in America and other parts."
Mr. Kivose stated. "What step has
been taken by the Navy Minister?"
"The article was not written by
Vice-Admiral Suetsugu himself." said
the Navy Minister. "However. the
s
matter is recciving attention. as it has
n
had effects unexpected by Vice-Admi-
h
ral Suetsugu."
"The article said Colonel Lindbergh
.s
acted as a spy when he flew over the
h
Chishima Ialands." persisted the inter-
pellator. "What is the view of the
authorities on that matter."
e
Rengo said Admiral Osumi replied.
y
"The writing seems to have gone too
far." and that. "An explanation has
been made through American news-
papermen and correspondents in order
that possible misunderstanding may be
removed."
Treaty Costs Discussed
Then Mr. Kiyose turned to naval

=== Page 251 === (confidence 0.99)
America.
"This article, attributed to Vice-Ad-
miral Suetsugu, has caused a great
sensation in America and other parts,"
Mr. Kiyose stated. "What step has
been taken by the Navy Minister?"
"The article was not written by
Vice-Admiral Suetsugu himself," said
the Navy Minister. "However, the
matter is receiving attention, as it has
had effects unexpected by Vice-Admi-
h
ral Suetsugu."
"The article said Colonel Lindbergh
acted as a spy when he flew over the
Chishima Ialands," persisted the inter-
pellator. "What is the view of the
authorities on that matter."
Rengo said Admiral Osumi replied,
"The writing seems to have gone too
far," and that, "An explanation has
been made through American news-
papermen and correspondents in order
that possible misunderstanding may be
removed."
Treaty Costs Discuseed
Then Mr. Kiyose turned to naval
disarmament.
"There are many." said he, "who
consider that the navy treaty should
not be revised. because such treaties
serve to increase expenditures rather
than lessen them. What is the opinion
of the navy on that?"
"It depends on the contents of the
treaty," replied the Navy Minister.
"Do the naval authorities consider
that military expenditures have been
decreased as a result of the London
Treaty?"
"The demand for military expendi-
tures must change in accordance with
various conditions other than disarma-
ment treaties."
"It is considered," Mr. Kiyose con-
tinued, "that the continued existence
of the London Treaty encourages ship
building competition between Japan
and America."
"Efforts will be made to prevent the
occurrence of such a state of affairs."
the Navy Minister said.
"Beyond naval competition," asked
Mr. Kiyose bluntly, "is there fear of
a serious state between the two coun-
tries?"
"I must refrain from commenting on
the future." Admiral Osumi replicd.
Mr. Kiyose next wanted to know the
number and kini of vessels in the first
replenishment plan. construction of
which has not yet been undertaken.
"The vessels not yet constructed."
said the Navy Minister. "are one cruis-
er, one destroyer, two submarine boats
and two mine sweepers."
Vessels Are Listed
"What is the allotment of ship con-
struetion to the naval yards and civil
yards?"
"The allotments have hitherto been
half-and-half. but as the naval arse-
nals have been engaging recently in
the reconstruction of capital ships. the
allotments of new ship construction at
present are two-thirds to civil yards
and one-third to non-civil yards."
"What are the items and prices of
main artieles to be imported with the
naval appropriations of 1934," asked
Mr. Kiyose.
Paymaster Rear-Admiral Haruichi
Murakami, chief of the accountant and
supply bureau. listed them as fuel oil.
arms. nickel. Chilean nitrate and
others, the total expenditure being es-
timated at Y23,000,000.
Hope for Solution
Answers by Cabinet Ministers under
interpellation at other periods of the
day indicated that the Government still
has hope of inducing the Indian Gov-
ernment to modify its recent tariff in-
crease on sundry goods. and indicated
that protests of mail pilots will bring
about a change in the location of the
Osaka airport.
In the budget session of the Lower
House Mr. Teikichi Ichimatsu. Minsei-
to. complained that a "news leak" had
taken place Sunday in announcement
of Government decision to petition the
Emperor to proclaim an amnesty in
celebration of the birth of the Crown
Prince. Mr. Ichimatsu said that no
formal announcement had been made
by the authorities. yet the decision re-
ceived general publicity.
Replying to questions by Mr. Tsuguo
Nakamura of the Kokumin-Domei on
the iron merger question, in which it

=== Page 253 === (confidence 0.99)
The Japan Advertiser,
3
Tokyo, Wednesday, February 7, 1934.
Powers Ignore Hirota
KOKUMIN
There is heated discussion of Japan's
foreign policy in both Houses of the
Diet. It seems that the people are de-
termined that diplomacy should be
taken from the ground in which it has
lain for years and given a new start.
Much credit goes to Foreign Minister
Hirota for the revival of the interest
of the people in foreign policy. He has
successfully resisted the elements which
would keep diplomacy subservient or
in the background.
Mr. Hirota's speech before the Diet
was for both domestic and foreign con-
sumption. The effect was unfortunate.
It tampered with the spirit of the peo-
ple at home. Abroad it had the op-
posite result from that desired by him.
He deprecated the talk about the crisis
of 1935 and 1936, but he failed to make
clear what he proposes to do to tide
over the crisis. The people are feeling
the gravity of the situation which will
confront the country when the naval
treaties expire. The uncertainty felt
by them about the future is too great
to be dispelled by the statement that
diplomacy may be counted on to cope
successfully with the anticipated crisis.
As for the effect oi Mr. Hirota's Diet
speech in foreign countries. it has been
disconcerting. The speech did not
evoke serious reactions. The foreign
press was interested more in the re-
signation of General Araki as War Min-
ister than in the Hirota speech. Mr.
Hirota had much to say regarding
America, the Soviet Union and China.
The effect of his speech in these coun-
tries was discouraging. China did not
respond to what the Minister said
about it. In the United States, the
specch was the occasion for discussion
of relations between Japan and the
U.S.S.R. Some commentators went the
length of declaring that a war between
them was inevitable. In Moscow. the
speech was most unpopular. High
Soviet officials concluded that there was
no intention on the part of Japan to
alter its policy toward the Soviet Union.
which they held was responsible for
the tension in relations between them.
Mr. Hirota would promote friendly re-
lations with America, the Soviet Union
and China. But these countries refuse
to listen to him. The task before Mr.
Hirota is difficult.

=== Page 257 === (confidence 0.99)
35
The Kokumin,
Translsted by the Japan Advertiser.
Toryo, Thuredey, Pebrwory 8. 19?4.
Takauji Ashikaga
KOKUMIN
Baron Kumakichi Nakajima, the Min-
ister of Commerce and Industry, has
again exposed himself to widespread
criticism, this time, over a magazine
article he has written on the life of
Takauji Ashikaga. sometimes known as
the most disloyal figure in Japanes
history. This developmen: follows im-
mediately the violent Diet bradside
let loose on the person of Baron Na-
kajima over his alleged participation in
the iron merger scandal. It is most de-
plorable that a Minister of the National
Cabinet should have written in praise
of a historic figure remembered for his
disloyalty to the Emperor. It is quite
as much unfortunate that a group of
minor politicians have taken this op-
portunity to stir up an incident and
lead a movement toward a rupture of
the Cabinet.
Japanese history tells us that Takauji
Ashikaga was a traitor. There is no
need now for any to come forward and
tell us this was not so. An indiscre-
tion committed by the Commerce Min-
ister is much to be regretted; but at
the same time the attitude of the Diet
clique of making political capital of
this is even harder to forgive. It is
less courageous, more selfish.
In any case, the Diet session as it is,
being he!d today has no room for dle
talk about the character. traitor or no
traitor, of an ancient historical hero.
Military reform and improvement of
our foreign relations are incomparably
more important. The questions of
passing the budget and of the possi-
bility of inflation in Japan are waiting
for immediate solution, and, besides
these, there is the burning question of
agricultural relief. Due to the prolong-
ed economic depression, the farmers of
Japan have almost lost hope in the
future. Meantime the rice crop of 1934
is likely to be another bumper yield.
causing further despair and suffering.
Last year Korean and Formosan rice
also were harvc:ted in record quanti-
ties. thereby complicating the already
exasperating situation. While the far-
mer's debt is mounting and large relic!
funds are needed. there are no signs
that the military budget may be re-
duccd. Import restriction may be ne-
cessary for colonial rice.
All these vital questions are con-
fronting the Diet. The minute sooner
they are solved the greater will be
the extent of relief and benefit. And
yet the members have engaged them-
selves in a debate on the moral in-
tegrity of Takauji Ashikaga-the man
long dead and gone.
In conclusion. we wish to make it
clear that we are in no way obliged
to defend the conduct of Baron Naka-
jima. in whatever private action he
may take. We believe in common
sense in political honesty and, above
all. in loyalty to His Majesty the Em-
peror.

LDS:
Tokyo's despatch No. 1088, dated
December 10, 1934:
This despatch discusses the proceedings
of an estraordinary session of the-Diet
convened on November 28 to enact relief
measures for districts which are suffering
from depression and from natural calamities.
Two supplementary budget bills were passed
providing for a total appropriation of
Yen 81,202,513. Other relief measures were
enacted.
The Government was not able to confine
discussion to relief measures. Interpella-
tions were addressed to the Government
regarding the preponderance of military
appropriations in the national budget;
increased expenditures in Manchuria; con-
tinued issuance of deficiency bonds, etc.
Aside from the measures finally adopted,
the following developments were important:
the defeat of the Seiyukai by the Government
and a consequent loss of prestige by that
Party, the failure of efforts to restore the
functioning of constitutional Government, and
further manifestations of the seemingly un-
assailable position of the military in the
political life of the country.

Sir:
As forecasted by the press,* the Government was
not able to confine the discussions of the Extraordinary
Session of the Diet to relief measures, the enactment of
which was the reason for calling a special session. Such
troublesome subjects were brought up for iscussion as the
preponderance of Army and Navy appropriations in the 1935-
JAN 8 11995
36 national budget, increased expenditures in Manchuria,
the continued issuance of red ink bonds, the pamphlet re-
cently issued by the Army, the dependence of national se-
curity
*See Section III, (f) The Diet, of the Monthly Report for
October, 1934.
** Embassy's despatch No. 1031, November 1, 1934.

=== Page 271 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
curity upon sound financial, industrial, and commercial
conditions, dissolution of the Diet with consequent
general elections, and the failure of the Government to
convene the special session at an earlier date. Never-
theless, the Government accomplished its main aims, which
were success in withstanding the attack of the majority
party in the Lower House and the approval of a tolerably
small supplementary budget for relief.* Aside from the
five measures finally enacted, the following were the
most important developments and results of the Diet pro-
ceedings: the defeat of the Seiyukai by the Government
and a consequent loss of prestige by that Party; the fail-
ure at present of efforts to restore the functioning of
constitutional Government in Japan;** a renewed manifesta-
tion of the seemingly unassailable position of the mili-
tary in the political life of the country; and a further
demonstration of the political astuteness and skill of Mr.
Takahashi, who was taken into the Cabinet one day prior
to the formal opening of the Diet.
The extraordinary session of the Diet was formally
convened by the Emperor on November 28 in the House of
Peers with the reading of an Imperial Rescript. The fol-
lowing day Premier Okada delivered an ad ress before a
plenary session of the House of Peers in which he reas. ert-
ed Japan's naval policy in general terms, touched on the
urgent need for the relief of those rural districts which
were suffering from the economic depression or had been
visited with natural calamities, stated that the funds
reguired
*See previous Monthly Reports.
**Embassy's despatches No. 308,July 31, 1934, and No.
1068, November 30, 1934.

=== Page 272 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
required for relief of the distressed districts would
be raised solely by the issuance of Government Bonds,
emphasized the need for bringing about a solution of
the rice problem, and expressed regret that there had
been trouble over the project for reorganization in
Manchuria, at the same time remarking that this diffi-
culty had been settled amicably and that the reorgani-
zation plan was operating with success. On November
30 the Minister for Foreign Affairs read a speech on
Japan's foreign policy before the Upper House and later
before the Lower House. In his speech Mr. Hirota remark-
ed on the improvement in the relations with Soviet Rus-
sia, expressed gratification over the satisfactory trade
relations with India following the conclusion of the
Trade Convention, gave voice to the hope that the trade
conference at Batavia would end in an agreement, and
stated th t Japan's naval desires were a naval force
adequate for national security and at the same time the
establishment of the principle of non-menace and non-ag-
gression. This speech contained no specific discussion
of the London naval conversations, Sino-Japanese relations,
and Japan's difficulties in the matter of foreign commerce.
Interpellations conmenced on Dec mber 1 and severe! days
later the Lower House vegan to discuss the Government's
supplementary relief budget. On December 5 these budget
discussions were delayed by a Seiyukai motion that a sum
of ¥180,000,000 be ad ed to the relief budget. As a result
of this delay the deliberations of the Diet were extended
twice by Imperial decree. The Diet was formally adjourned
on

=== Page 273 === (confidence 0.97)
-4 -.
on December 10, after it had passed the relief budget
as submitted by the Government and after 12 days of re-
portedly intense discussion, although the duration of the
session had originally been fixed at seven days.
Interpellations.
A
Among the many outstanding interpellations and Cabinet
answers in the House of Represenatives were the following:
Mr. Yamamoto, the leading interpellator of the Seiyukai,
asserted that the present Government was bureaucratic and
accused Premier Okada of desiring a dictatorship and of
ignoring the political Parties, to which Premier Okada re-
plied that he favored constitutional Government an had not
disregarded the political Parties; Mr. Nakashima, of the
Minseito, asked if the Army intended to carry out its ideas
concerning the economic reform of Japan, and in reply
General Hayashi declared that the Army had no intention of
putting its economic theories into practice at once, thus
leaving the Lower House in ignorance as to whether such
an intention existed with regard to the future; and another
Minseito member stated in an interpellation that national
security could not be achieved, n matter how large the
military appropriation might be, if the farmers remained
in their istressed condition, to which Admiral Okada
responded that he was in agreement with the view that the
farmers should be afforded relief; and, upon being ques-
tioned concerning the Government's recently announced
emergency tax plan, the Premier saic that the Government
did not consider the present time favorable for a general
tax

=== Page 274 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
tax increase.
Legislation Enacted.
Two supplementary budget bills were passed providing
for a total appropriation of ¥81,202,513 (about $24,360,754)
to be spent over a period of 16 months: ¥80,954,814 for
relief in the districts affected by natural disasters,
¥187,536 for sending delegates to the trade conference
at Batavia, and ¥60,163 for the creation of the proposed
Manchurian Affairs Bureau in the Cabinet(the last named
sum is to be approriated only if the Diet in its regular
1935 session approves the Government's bill for the re-
organization of Japanese administration and representation
in Manchuria). Another bill was passed providing for a
reduction of taxes for the benefit of persons wh suffered
from the storm and flood disasters of July 1l and September
21, respectively. A fourth bill enacted provides for the
distribution of Government-owned rice to the inhabitants
of those northeastern prefectures which are suffering
from a small rice crop. A further bill was passed providing
that Law No. 5 of 1934, which authorizes the Government
to issue a national loan to the amount of ¥606,900,000 for
financing the general account expenditures for the 1935-36
fiscal year, shall be amended to the end that the Govern-
ment may increase this national 1oan to ¥676,100,000.
The two budget bills caused a great deal of discussion
in the House of Representatives but met with relatively
little difficulty in the House of Peers. Viscount Maeda,
however, st ted to the Peers before their approval of the
bill had been given, that compared with other expenditures
in the 1935-36 budget the relief budget was too small,
that the Government had neglected its duty by not conven-
ing

=== Page 275 === (confidence 0.99)
-6-
ing the session at an earlier date, and that he doubted
the sincerity of a statement made by the Government to
the effect that additional relief measures would be taken
in the future, if necessary.
Loss of Prestige by the Seiyukai as a Result of its
Actions in the Course of the Diet Proceedings.
According to newspaper reports, the Seiyukai employ-
ed political tactics that caused resentment on the part
of the Government and wide-spread derrogatory criticism
of the Party. In the first place, this Party took up
a considerable amount of time in attacking the Minister
of Justice for the employment of alleged third degree
methods in the examination by the judicial authorities of
persons suspected of bribery. Several Seiyukai members
called attention to the discrepancies between military
appropriations and non-military appropriations in the 1935-
36 budget and then roceeded to change the subject in de-
feren. to or in fear of the military. The final fatal
step t: en by the Seiyukai was the presentation of a motion
in the h  se of Representatives Budgetary Committee which
demanded une addition of ¥130,000,000 to the relief funds
under oiscussion, with the provision that if the Government
did not accede to this demand the Seiyukai Party "would
not permit" the continuance of celiber&tions on th relief
budget. This sum seemed abnormally high to the Government
in view of the fact th t it would have been difficult, if
not impossible, to find the mechanical means of handling
such an amount. The principal feature of the demand that
incensed the Cabinet, however, was that the motion came
as

=== Page 276 === (confidence 0.98)
- 7 -
as a complete surprise and was obviously an attempt at
filibustering. The Cabinet unanimously decided to reject
the Seiyukai motion and informed the Seiyukai that it would
dissolve the Diet and call for new elections rather than
agree to the demand in question, whereupon the Seiyukai
yielded and agreed that the relief budget deliberations
should proceed. Apparently the majority party was appre-
hensive concerning its ability to maintain its majority
in the Lower House if new elections sno ld be held.
The action of the Seiyukai was criticised by the Koku-
min Domei. Since the motion had been presented without
the knowledge of the Minseito, that Party was, according
to reports, indignant and it even threatened to abandon
the Seiyukai-Minseito "coalition". It will be remembered
that when the cooperation plan was announced on November
26, the so-called coalition was pregnant of little prorise
of concerted action between th to purtics.*
The Failure at Present of Efforts on the Part of the
Political Parties to Restore the Functioning of Consti-
tutional Government in Japan.
When the Government issued its above-mentione ultima-
tum to the Seiyukai, it ap arently fclt sure of its position,
and the f ct that that Party promptly capitul ted strengthened
tie entrenchment of th present nat oral C: binct and lessen-
ed thc ch nces of : returr t constitut:onal party govorn-
ment iu the near future. The G vernment's st: tus was also
enhnced by the dissaffection of the Minseito from the Sei-
yukai. The bulk of editorial comnent vigorously attacked
the Seiyukai for its attempt at obstruction an: for its
other
*Embassy's despatch No. 10c8, November 70, 1974.

=== Page 277 === (confidence 0.98)
- 8 -
other ill-chosen maneuvers, one newspaper branding the
party's meekness as "abject", another describing it as a
threat not backed up by action. RENGO of December 7 re-
marked that for the first time since its organization,
the Okada Cabinet ha: enjoyed the solid support of the
press in its parliamentary struggle with the Seiyukai.
A Renewed Manifestation of the Supromacy of the Mili-
tary in Political Affairs.
Although several interpellations in the Diet contain-
ed criticisms of the large appropriations for the Army and
the Navy in the 1935-36 budget, the attacks were made half-
heartedly and the Government seems to have successfully
withstood them.
General Hayashi stated in answer to an interpellation
concerning the latest Army pamphlet that it had been is-
sued chiefly to persuade the population to sink domestic
conflicts and give attention to the general problem of
defense. The Army's success in crcating among the people
a feeling that Japan is threat ned by other Powers was ta-
citly admitted by Mr. Takahashi, wh  is reportec to have
state in the Diet proceedings that the propaganda about
the 1955-36 crisis hac crcated an atmosphere of uneasi-
ness which in itself constituted a crisis.* In reply to
an interpellation concerning th preponderance of mili-
tary appropri tions over other appropriations in the next
budget, the Finance Minister is alleged to have said that
the Okada Cabinet inherited the policies of the Saito
Cahinet, one of then being the principle that Japan should
maintain
*In this connection refercnce is made to the Embas. y's
despatc). No. 1079, Dec(mber 11, 1934.

=== Page 278 === (confidence 0.99)
- 9 -
maintain a strong foreign policy, the backbone of which
was national defense.
Mr. Nakashima of the Minseito asserted that General
Araki, at the time he was War Minister, had promised that
by 1934 the military expenditures in "Manchukuo" would
have been reduced to a normal figure, whereupon General
Hayashi remarked that "circumstances had changed". Both
the War and Navy Ministers, in answer to questions con-
cerning the large 1935-36 military expenditures, stated
emphatically that all the reductions had been made that
could possibly be made in their respective budgets.
An instance of the weak manner in which the extra-
vagance of the military was queried by Diet members may be
found in statements attributed to Mr. Seigo Nakano of the
Kokumin Domei by the JAPAN CHRONICLE. Mr. Nakano is re-
ported to have asked whether the urgent questions of farm
relief and the food of the people were to be sacrificed
on the altar of military expenditure, and to have followed
this interrogation immediately with a lengthy defense of
the Army pamphlet. Accor ing to this newspaper, the Kokumin
Domei member steted that the Army had a right to express
itself pub icly as it had done and that neither General
Araki nor General Hayashi could have prevented its doing so.
As further evidence of the alleged timorousness with which
the Diet took up the cuestion of the relatively large mili-
tary appropriations, the following comment: ry from the KOKUMIN
SHIMBUN of December 1 may be citcd, as it appeared in trans-
lation:

=== Page 279 === (confidence 0.99)
- 10 -
lation: "When he(Mr. Yamamoto, chief interpellator of
the Seiyukai) referred to the huge military budgets in
comparison with relief measures, Mr. Yamamoto was doing
it while carefully looking at the faces of the military
and naval officers to see if they were not angry with him."
There is quoted below an excerpt from an editorial
on the Diet session which appeared in the JAPAN CHRONICLE
of December 5:
" The propaganda for the army and the "nation-
al crisis" is so intense and voluminous that
there are few indeed who can retain a complete
detachment of thought on the subject or apply
to it the usual standards that have to be ap-
plied when deciding on questions of expendi-
ture. In comparison with the magnitude of the
subject - the contrast between the expenditure
on defence in a time of undisturbed peace and
the paucity  funds available for the financial
and even the alimentary relief of the most im-
portant community in the nat:onal economy - the
remonstrances in the Diet werc few and feeble
indeed."
A Further Demonstration of Takahashi's Political As=
tuteness ard Skill.
Mr. Takahashi's adroit replies to inte pellations
in the Diet tended to increase his long standing prestige.
For example, Mr. Horikiri, a Se yukai member, severely
criticised the Finance Ministry for the manner in which
the budget had been fixed and Mr. Takahashi replied with
the remark that the interpellator had once becn Parlia-
mentary Vice Minister in the Finance Ministry and should
have the fullest understanding of budget making. After the
relief budget had been decided upon, the Finance Minister
stated that official efforts to make relief measures ef-
fective would be useles? unless the people in the afflicted
districts

=== Page 280 === (confidence 0.99)
-11-
districts exerted themselves. The success with which the
new Minister for Finance parried questions put to the
Cabinet would appear to lend weight to the opinion express-
ed at the time of his appointment to the effect that his
induction into the Cabinet would definitely strengthen
the Government's position.*
Inasmuch as there was not sufficient time after ad-
journment to translate the Diet proceedings as announced in
the OFFICTAL GAZETTE of the Japanese Government, the Embassy
had to rely on the newspapers for reports concerning the
various interpellations. The provisions of the bills enact-
ed, however, were obtained directly from the OFFICIAL
GAZETTE.

JAPANESE ROLE IN
ASIA
FOREIGN MINISTER'S
SPEECH
RELATIONS WITH CHINA
FROM OUR OWN CORRESPONDENT
TOKYO, JAN. 22
Press predictions that 1935 will see a
new China policy launched gave special
interest to Mr. Hirota's references to
China to-day in his annual speech to the
Diet on foreign policy. Both warning and
invitation may be read into his carefully
chosen expressions. China was told that
her condition still gives Japan cause for
anxiety. She was invited to share with
Japan responsibility for peace and order
in the Far East. The hope was expressed
that she would awake to the realities of
the situation and realize Japan's power to
·assist her.
Those rounded phrases can hardly be
regarded as a contradiction of recent
rumours that China may be asked to free
herself from Western trammels and rely
on Japan instead. The speech seemed to
be inspired by the guiding principle of
"Eastern Asia first " and " Japan first in
Eastern Asia." It assumed that the power
of Japan as well as her interests give her
primacy in the Far East. With the rest
of the world she will cultivate friendship.
but Mr. Hirota's silences even more than
his words implied that she regards the Far
East as her sphere.
BRITAIN AND AMERICA
Great Britain and America came in for
special mention, each in the friendliest
manner, but with a significant difference
of tone. Conflict with the United States
was unimaginable, said the Minister, but
when he added that cooperation between
Japan and Great Britain would be a
really important contribution to world
peace, he seemed to be thinking of more
than the mere continuance of peaceful
relations.
The hope Mr. Hirota expressed once
before that demilitarized frontiers would
be established between Russia and Japan
reappeared in his suggestion that the
Moscow Government should consider the
military works it has erected near the
Manchurian border. The Soviet Govern-
ment will easily perceive that fortifications
along the Amur are less important than
submarine and air bases at Vladivostok.
Mr. Hirota repeated that Japan did not
propose to launch out in naval construc-
tion, but hoped to reach agreement based
on a new formula. The speech concluded
with an admonition addressed to Mr.
Hirota's countrymen that if Japan was to
consolidate her position and realize her
purposes extreme caution would be
necessary as well as strong determination.
A report of Mr. Hirota's speecht appears
on the preceding page.

JEHOL BORDER DISPUTE
FROM OUR CORRESPONDENT
PEKING, JAN. 21
The Jehol Deader incident, which both
sides have beer reating as a local issue,
has been virtually settled without resort
to force, but the general problem of the
western border of Jehol north of the
Great Wall remains. The Japanese are
not pressing the matter at present, how-
ever, in view of Chinese reluctance to
discuss the question.
The Chinese complained that Japanese and
Manchukuo troops were concentrating in
Western Jehol for the purpose of expelling
Chinese militia from a disputed area of the
adjoining province of Chahar.

WASHINGTON TREATY
it will not be difficult, I am sure, to surmount
Coming to the question of naval disarm-
any obstacles we may encounter. In face of the
ament, Mr. Hirota said the fundamental
gravity of the present international situation
I wish to appeal specially for the united support
policy of the Japanese Government in the
of the whole nation.
present negotiation was to carry out in full
a sweeping reduction of armaments and
to effect the total abolition or drastic limi-
tation of offensive armaments so as to free
each Power from the menace of other
Powers, and, in fact, to make it impossible
for any Power to menace another.
The Washington Treaty of Naval Limitation
of 1922 having now become incompatible with
our basic policy (he continued), our Govern-
ment notified the Government of the United
States on December 29 last year of Japan's in-
tention to terminate that Treaty in accordance
with its provisions. In consequence the Wash-
ington Treaty ceases to be in force as from the
last day of December, 1936. This step must
not be taken to mean that we intend to launch
out on any expansion of our armaments, which
is far from our thoughts. On the contrary, we
look forward to the conclusion of another pact
to supersede the Washington Treaty, a pact basej
on a new formula which shall be truly consonant
with the spirit of disarmament.
We sincerely hope—and are devoting our best
efforts—to bring about an early resumption of
the negotiations for the achievement of a new
and equitable agreement which will embody the
principles of non-aggression and non-menace and
effect an actual and sweeping reduction of arma-
ments, and advance thereby the cause of world
peace.
Japan and the United States have been bound
not only by the vital economic relationship of a
mutual interdependence unparalleled elsewhere,
but also by the historical bond of friendship
ever since the commencement of their inter-
course.
There exists no question between
the two countries which it is intrinsically
difficult to solve amicably. Certainly it is un-
imaginable that there should be any cause of
conflict between two countries separated by the
vast expanse of the Pacific.
RELATIONS WITH BRITAIN
As for our former ally Great Britain, while it
is certain that in order to safeguard our over-
seas trade we have yet to discuss various matters
with that country, I cannot think of any part
of the globe where an adjustment of interest
between us might be considered in any way un-
attainable. It is needless to say that good un-
derstanding and cooperation between Japan and
Great Britain constitutes a really important con-
tribution to the peace of the world.
It is in consideration of these relationships
that the Japanese Government have carried on
pesotiations with America and Great Britain in

"We have been guided wholly by the
same spirit in all our negotiations with the
Soviet Union," Mr. Hirota went on.
The present negotiations on the transfer of the
North Manchurian Railway have made further
progress since I last spoke, and it will not perhaps
be long before agreement is reached. If the
transfer serves to eliminate a source of such dis-
putes as have occurred hitherto so frequently
along that railway, and to strengthen the friend-
ship between Japan, Manchukuo, and the Soviet
Union, the prime objective of the present negotia-
tions will have been attained.
Our Government are planning to accelerate the
peaceful development of Soviet-Japanese rela-
tions by redoubling their efforts for a solution of
other pending problems. To that end hearty
cooperation on the part of the Soviet Union is, of
course, essential. In this connexion I cannot but
hope that the Soviet Government will give special
consideration to 'the question of military works
in the Far East, especially along the Soviet-
Manchukuo frontiers, with a view to promoting
mutual trust and assurance.
GOOD WILL TO CHINA
Mr. Hirota said that in the Chinese
political situation many serious possi-
bilities were still latent.
While fortunately the main strength of the Red
armies in the region of Kiangsi and Fukien
appears to have been driven away from those
territories by the expeditionary forces sent by the
Government (he said) remaining units of these
armies are said to be establishing contact with
their allies in Kweichow and Szechwan and there-
abouts and moving westward into remoter
regions. In view of this fact, coupled with
reports of the Sovietization of Sinkiang, the
Japanese Government will be obliged to continue
to watch with concern the activities of the Com-
munist Party and armies in China.
Again, it is to be regretted that even now in
certain localities of China anti-Japanese agitation
has not yet been completely brought to an end.
Japan attaches great importance to her friend-
ship with other States in Eastern Asia, and counts
on their participation in bearing the serious
responsibilities for the maintenance of peace and
order in this part of the world.
I fervently hope therefore not only that China
will soon recover her stability but that she will
awaken to a realization of the whole situation
of Eastern Asia and undertake to meet the
genuine aspirations of our country. In view of
our position as China's neighbour and the stabiliz-
ing force in Eastern Asia it is our policy to try
to assist China in the attainment of this goal.
I may add that the Japanese Government are
glad to acknowledge the fact that, as has been
pending questions, there is to-day a growing
trend among the Chinese people to appreciate
the true motive of Japan.
While we will do everything to encourage that
us further in that direction.
FOREIGN TRADE
Turning to Japan's trade relations
Mr. Hirota said that the policy of trade
restriction exemplified in the promulga-
tion of high Customs tariffs, import
restrictions, exchange control, and ex-
change compensation duties, &c., was as
prevalent as ever among foreign countries.
Some of them have even gone so far as to
abrogate their commercial treaties with our
country (he continued). Such a situation is
extremely regrettable not only for the sake of
Japan, but also from the broader point of view
of the world's economic rehabilitation. Our
Government are endeavouring to come to a fair
agreement with each one of the Powers con-
cerned in order to have such measures of trade
restriction either modified or removed as far as
possible, and to promote the mutual interests of
both.
For a country like Japan, with a vast popula-
tion on the one、hand and meagre natural
resources on the other, foreign trade constitutes
an important peaceful means of livelihood. The
advance of Japanese commodities on the markets
of the world to-day is only the fruit of our
tion's united and indefatigable labours for

FAR EASTERNRS

FECEIVED
FEB 71935
January 22, 1935.
DIVISION OF
1n the Diet on January 22, 1935.
894,032
The speech reviewing Japan's foreign relations
delivered in the Diet today by the Minister for Foreign
Affairs gives marked indication of having been addressed
more to the world at large than to the Japanese people
894.032/1
and having been formulated with an eye primarily on
creating a certain desired effect on overseas opinion.
It contains at the same time a firm restatement of Japanese
foreign policy ("The road for Japan to take has long been
33
chosen.") and a plea for the restitution to Japan of the
8
good-will of other nations. It may thus in effect be
regarded as a declaration of desire to work toward the re-
sumption of Japan's former relations with its neighbors,
but on a basis acceptable to Japan.
The speech may be analyzed as follows:
1. At the forefront, there is the effusive
reference to the forthcoming visit to Japan of the "Emperor
of Manchukuo". In times not marked by stress, it would be
F
1
usual for the Minister for Foreign Affairs to make some
G
felicitous reference to the contemplated visit to Japan of
a foreign monarch; but, in view of the existing situation
in the Far East, the reference is obviously intended to
have a definite significance. It may be surmised that the
portion

=== Page 292 === (confidence 0.98)
227
- 2 -
portion of the speech dealing with "Manchukuo" had a
two-fold purpose: one, to allay any suspicion likely to
arise in Japanese nationalistic quarters of the existence
of any divergence of opinion between the military element
and the civil government, and, two, to reaffirm to foreign
governments Japan's determination to maintain the situation
which it has brought about in Manchuria.
2. The portion devoted to naval limitation is a
restatement of previous Japanese declarations and contains
nothing new; but even as a restatement it usefully serves
Japan's purposes, for the reason that it operates to keep
alive the Japanese thesis in regard to "security",
"non-aggression" and"reduction of offensive categories"
which has undeniably made an impression upon the unthinking
both in this country and in Great Britain.
3. The reference to relations with the United States
1s brief, but it is of distinct interest to note that
Mr. Hirota has, by stating that "there exists no question
between the two countries which is intrinsically difficult
of amicable solution", sought to express assent to the
view set forth in the Secretary's reply to Mr. Hirota's
personal message of February last, that "there are in fact
no questions lying between our two countries which if they
be viewed in proper perspective in both countries can with
any warrant be regarded as not readily susceptible to
adjustment by pacific processes".
4.As

=== Page 293 === (confidence 0.98)
358
- 3. -
4. As predicted in press despatches from Tokyo,
Mr. Hirota's references to relations with the Soviet Union
are couched in substantially more friendly language than
was the case in his last two addresses to the Diet. It
will be noticed that Mr. Hirota concludes an apparently
optimistic forecast of developments in the relations between
the two countries with a request which is not of a reassur-
ing character -- that the construction by the Soviets of
fortifications along the Manchurian frontier cease.
5. Considerable space was devoted by Mr. Hirota to
Japanese relations with China. The language which he
used in urging China to collaborate with Japan in main-
taining peace in the Far East is unobjectionable, but
there is nothing in the statement which rules out the
plausibility of the prediction made by the Japanese press
that he would suggest to China cooperation with a view to
eliminating western political influence from the Far East.
Conclusion:
The speech does not offer any basis to suppose that
there has occurred any substantial change in Japan's
foreign policy. It contains material which may suggest de-
velopment of an unfavorable character (Sino-Japanese
relations) as well as of a favorable character (Soviet-
Japanese relations). The speech does not appear to warrant
any relaxation in the attitude of caution and reserve which
has been assumed by this Government in the conduct of
relations with the Japanese Government.

Sir:
I have the honor to report that the speech of
the Japanese Foreign Minister, iirota, received in
general conciliatory comment in the Soviet press.
His allusions to the desire of the Japanese Government
to develop peaceful relations with the Soviet Union
and his remarks regarding the successful trend of the
negotiations for the sale of the Chinese Eastern Rail-
way were greeted with approval. However, the hope
which Mr. Hirota expressed thet the Soviet Government
would find it possible to examine the question of
Soviet
F
G

=== Page 298 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
Soviet fortifications in the Far Bast was criticized
as contradicting the expressed desire for friendly
relations.
PRAVDA on January 24th, after pointing out this
alleged contradiction, wrote: "We understand that
Japanese imperialistic circles, whose aims are to
prepare war on the U.S.S.R., are discontented with
the measures taken by the Soviet Government to protect
its frontiers against elements which have made 'easy
conquests' their specialty during the last years. It
is, however, difficult to understand how statesmen in
favor of 'acceleration of the development of peaceful
relations' can share this discontent."
Mr. Hirota's allusion to the "Sovietization" of
Sinkiang and to the presence of troops of the Red Army
there were denied with indignation by the Soviet press,
which ex, essed "astonishment in hearing the Japanese
Minister f- Foreign Affairs making use of these inven-
tions, whose cource is mostly Japanese."
The occasion
was used to restate Soviet policy in opposing any im-
perialistic conquest, especially in regard to China.
On January 29th PRAVDA printed a Reuter dispatch to
the effect that the military and civil governors of
Sinkiang had telegraphed Nanking denying the insinua-
tions contained in Mr. Hirota's speech and reaffirming
their loyalty to the Nanking Government.
The govern-
ors were reported as having stated that while foreign
advisers were employed in Sinkiang, their duties were
of a purely commercial and technical character.
They
admitted

=== Page 299 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
admitted, however, that a loan from the Soviet Govern-
ment was under consideration. The Soviet sensitiveness
to Mr. Hirota's allusions in regard to Sinkiang and the
speedy denial of Soviet influence is in all probability
based on fear of arousing British apprehensions of
Soviet penetration in the proximity of India.
The Sowiet press has given publicity to unfavor-
able foreign comment on Mr. Hirota's speech and in par-
ticular to the observation of the London TIMES of January
22nd that Mr. Hirota's remarks regarding Soviet military
preparations in the Far East were open to the same criti-
cism from a realistic point of view as the Japanese
proposals regarding naval arms.
The conciliatory tone
of Mr. Hirota's speech in respect of relations with the
Soviet Union and the comparatively favorable comment of
the Soviet press are indicative, however of the recent
relaxation in tension which has lately characterized
Soviet-Japanese relations.
Indications are not lacking
that the Kremlin is tending to regard the West rather
than the East as the most dangerous so urce of war
against the Soviet Union.
Mr. Molotov's speech at
the opening of the VII All-Union Congress of Soviets
in respect of Japan reflected this tendency.
His
concluding remarks. however, on January 3l, were of
a distinctly more hostile tone.
Replying to Mr.
Hirota's speech to the Japanese Diet on January 25th
on the demilitarization of the Soviet-Manchurian fron-
tier in the spirit of the Treaty of Portsmouth, Mr.
Molotov cited numerous instances of Japanese failure
to

=== Page 300 === (confidence 0.95)
to observe the provisions of this treaty, eapecially
concerning the return of Manchuria to China and the
removal of Japanese troops from the province. while
Mr. Molotov's remarks were critical in tone, it is not
expected that they will have much bearing on the present
course of Soviet-Japanese relations.
Respectfully yours,
Shne.nil
John C. Wley
Chargé d'Affaires ad interim.

The Honorable
