MAR 13 1935
Sir:
FILED
I have the honor to observe that for the first time
since 1930 a For ign Minister of Japan has been able to
deliver a speech* on the country's foreign relations, as
Mr. Hirota did on January 2, 1935, at the opening of
the regular session of the Diet, and point with gratifi-
cation to a real improvement - at least in one direction.
F/FG.
The recent conclusion of the negotiations for the sale of
the North Manchuria Railway has made this possible with
respect to Sovict Russia.
*Embassy's telegram No. 12, January 22, 3 p.m.

=== Page 302 === (confidence 0.99)
331
- 2 -
In form Mr. Hirota's speech was orthodox; in tone
perhaps more than ordinarily optimistic; and in substance
a model of carefully chosen expression cloaking at the same
time a purposeful policy of undisputed leadership in the
Far East. This policy, which is described by the Japanese
as "the preservation of peace in East Asia", has been enun-
ciated so often that it hardly seems necessary to repeat it.
Nevertheless, inasmuch as it is the basic factor with which
all nations have to contend in dealing with Japan, whether
it be in the field of naval disarmament, political questions
or commercial and economic problems, it may be found of in-
terest to review several of the public announcements made
in this connection during the past year.
In his speech to the Diet on January 23, 1934, Mr.
Hirota said: "We should not forget for a moment that Japan,
serving as the only cornerstone for the edifice of peace
of East Asia, bears the entire burden of responsibilities".
While this statement did not pass unnoticed, it was not
generally taken up until repeated in more direct form by
Mr. Amau in his now famous statement of April 17, 1934. He
said: "...at the same time we consider it only natural that,
to keep peace and order in East Asia, we must even act alone
on our own responsibility..." Nothing could be clearer than
these two announcements, following one another as they do.
The next reference to this policy is found in Mr. Hi-
rota's statement in his specch before the extraordinary ses-
sion of the Diet on November 30, 1934: "I am very pleased
to say that on the whole our relations with the various
countries of Asia, Europe and America have grown increasing-
ly cordial because better understanding now prevails among
the

=== Page 303 === (confidence 0.99)
232
- 3 -
the Powers regarding Japan's position in East Asia". This
is of particular interest in that it carries the implica-
tion of acceptance by the rest of the world of Japan's
policy and this is supported by Mr. Hirota's remarks before
the Diet two weeksago in which he said: "Japan attaches
great importance to her friendship with other states in
East Asia, and counts on their participation in bearing the
serious responsibilities for thc maintenance of peace in
this part of the world".
Bearing in mind, then, the nature of this announced
policy in the pursuit of which Japan has not hesitated to
resort to force of arms and presumably will not hesitate
to do so in the future, the repeatcd expressions by Mr.
Hirota in the speech under consideration of good will,
fraternal sentiments, non-aggression pects, friendship,
collaboration, cooperation anc peaceful purposes may be
viewed with perhaps a keener perception of the realities
involved.
Paragraph by paragraph Mr. Hirota in his speech takes
up Japan's relations with the various countries important
to her and with thc outstanding question of naval isarma-
ment. In most cases he deals with generalities and em-
phasizes Japan's peaceful puroo. es; the same time, however,
it is often possible to extract a kernel of fact from which
a conclusion may be dravm as to the Government's real in-
tentions regarding the various problems.
In dealing with "Manchukuo" Mr. Hirota avoids any di-
rect or indirect suggestion of recognition by other powers
and contents himself with the simple statcment that "the
work of laying th national foundations of Manchukuo has
been

=== Page 304 === (confidence 0.99)
233
4 -
been completed." By implication the question of recogni-
tion is for the time being at least a matter of indifference
to Japan. He emphasizes the economic importance of the
new state to Japan as well as the ties of friendship which
bind the two countries together.
Touching the question of the suspension of the preli-
minary naval conversations he says: "We sincerely hope for...
the achievement of a new equitable agreement which will
embody the principles of non-aggression and non-menace and
effect an actual and sweeping armament reduction..."
With the United States "there exists no question....
which is intrinsically difficult of an amicable solution",
and the "good understanding and cooperation betvween Japan
and Great Britain constitute really an important contribu-
tion to the peace of the world".
For the first time since his incumbency Mr. Hirota has
been able to speak vith a measure of guarded optimism about
the relations with the Soviet Union. Referring to the im-
pending settlement of the North Manchuria Railway question
he says that the Japanese Government is planning "to acce-
lerate the peaceful develorment of Soviet-Japanese relat:ons
by redoubling their efforts for the solution of other pend-
ing pro lems!, and he indicates thet the first problem to
be undertaken will be that of the establishment of a demili-
tar zed zone along the "Manchukuo"-Soviet frontier. That
this question is uppermost in the minds of the Japanese will
be found by reference to Mr. Hirota's speech before the Diet
in January of last year in which he said that no difficult
questions were encountered in Soviet-Japanese relations
fron the conclusion of the Peking Basic Treaty of 1925
even

=== Page 305 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
234
even through the Manchurian Incident but that "recently
the attitude of Soviet Russia toward Japan seems to have
undergone a change... Japan is certainly setting up no
new military establishments along the Manchukuo-Soviet
frontiers". And recently, in reply to an interpellation
in the Diet on January 25, 1935, Mr. Hirota said: "I con-
template calling the attention of the Soviet Government
to the status of Soviet defense in the Far East, particu-
larly on the Soviet-Manchukuo frontier".
At this point it is highly significant to note that
the remarkable progress which Mr. Hirota has been able to
achieve in the improvement of relations with Soviet Russia
during the past year has come about only after the energetic
militarization and defense of the Eastern Provinces by the
Soviet Government and tlis fact may prove an object lesson
from which other nations may well profit in dealing with
Japan.
In describing Japan's problems in relation to China,
the Foreign Minister emphasizes the communist dangers
abroad in that country, possibly wit the intention of ap-
pe:ling to the othcr foreign countries. He also makes an
indirect appeal for a terminat on of the anti-Japanese boy-
cott in China. In connection with recent tele rams from
Peiping regar ing the runor thut Chiang Kai Shek has de-
cided to adopt a policy of "coorcination" with Japan, there
may be a int in this direction in Mr. Hirota's statement:
"I may ad? that the Japanese Government are glad to acknow-
ledge the fact that....there is today a growing trend among
the Chinese people to appreciate the true otive of Japan.
While we will do everything to encourage this tendency,
I

=== Page 306 === (confidence 0.99)
-6 -
235
I hope that China will collaborate with us further in
that direction".
He finds the foreign trade situation, in which some
countries have abrogated their commercial treaties with
Japan to be "extremely regrettable" and he makes the fol-
lowing statement which in some ways may be considered as
the most important statement in his speech: "For a country
like Japan with a vast population on the one hand and meagre
natural resources on the other, foreign trade constitutes
an important peaceful means of livelihood". The word "peace-
ful" gives this sentence its significance for it brings
to mind the only possible alternative, namely that Japan
would otherwise be driven to seck means of livelihood by
force.
There follows a reference to the failure of the Batavia
Trade parley and a conventional expression to the effect that
it removed misunderstandings and clarified the respective
positions of Japan and the Netherlands.
In conclusion Mr. Hirota mentions thc foct that Japan's
withdrawal from the League of Nations will become effective
on March 27, next, and exhorts the nation to exert itself
in the pursuance of its p licy and purpose.
Careful reading of the Foreign Minister's address will
reveal that however important may be the varied and widely
diffused problems with which Japan has to deal, the all-
import nt, the vital, inescapable f ctor in eac! one is China.
It is Japan's relationship to China and her intense pre-occupa-
tion vith the chaotic political con it ons thcre which basi-
cally govern her relations with other nations; and it is the
policy of those nations in dealing with China which affects
their

=== Page 307 === (confidence 0.99)
236
- 7 -
their relationship with Japan. It is this question which
is at the root of Japan's "preservation of peace in East
Asia" policy; it is this question which has determined the
course of the naval conversations at London; it is this
question which all but brought Soviet Russia into open
conflict with Japan and which, indeed, has done so in the
past. The politico-economic struggle with which both the
United States and Great Britain are engaged with Japan has
its basis fundamentally in the question of China. Remove
that guestion and Japan's relations with the rest of the
world would be as normal as those of other nations with
each other.
1/
In this connection I enclose an article by Baron Yoshiro
Sakatani, member of the House of Peers, as it appeared in
the JAPAN ADVERTISER of January 9, 1935. He develops this
thesis in an interesting manner and makes several statements
which assume considerable importance, especially when read
in conjunction with the Foreign Minister's speech. Baron
Sakatani says that no close student of the preliminary
naval conversations at London car fail to note "a dark un-
dercurrent which poisons the minds of the delegations; and
that current has its fountain-head in the discrepancies of
opinions regarding the policy toward China".
He claims
that the United States places over-emphasis upon the "so-
called Open Door" in China an maintains that those British
and American stat smen who fear that to give Japan complete
freedom of cction in the East would jeopardize their in-
terests in China are under a grave misapprehension.
To those who may feel that Mr. Hirota's avowal of
peaceful purposes and concilictory methods are to be taken
at

=== Page 308 === (confidence 0.99)
237
-8-
at their face value, the following passages in Baron
Sakatani's article should be studied: "If England and
America aim to form their own policies toward China in-
dependently of Japan, the two nations would be attempt-
ing a thing imprudent and unwise". And, again: "One
can imagine no greater obstacle to the peace of the world
than that England and America, backed by their powerful
navies, should assume the leadership in the Far East".
Thinly veiled threats, these, and clothed in the
language of diplo acy, but not to be in any sense ignored.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew

=== Page 311 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
February 27, 1935.
S
Tokyo's 1150 of February 8, 1935,
reviews the proceedings of the Diet from
January 22, when the Diet resumed session
after the New Year holidays, to February 7.
The despatch unfortunately contains
considerable material which might have been
used to better advantage in separate
despatches. It not only describes the
tactics of the Seiyukai in attempting to
weaken the position of the Government and
the methods of the government in meeting
attacks, but it outlines and discusses
debates and statements in the Diet in
regard to Japan's foreign relations. Con-
sequently, the despatch does not present
an adequate and clear account of the
domestic political situation nor does it
give a clear picture of the attitude of the
political parties toward the foreign
policies of the Government.
The most important portion of the
despatch is its last paragraph, in which
it is stated that there are no indications
of any material change taking place in
the near future in the domestic political
situation.

Thus far there have been few important developments,
other than constant political maneuvering by the Government
MAR·12 1935
and by the Seiyukai, during the course of the 67th regular
FILED
Diet session, which was convoked on December 26, adjourned
on the following day, and resumed on January 22. A resumé
of the Diet proceedings during the past two weeks may,
F/FG.
however, be of interest to the Department in showing the
trend of parliamentary government, semi-defunct at present,
and also the anomalous relationship which continues to
exist

=== Page 313 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
exist between the Government and the political parties.
As in the ertraordinary session of the Diet held from
November 28 to December 10, 1934,* in the present session
the interpellations have not, for the most part, been
energetically followed up by Diet members, and Cabinet
Ministers have in most cases answered potentially
embarrassing interpellations in an evasive or equivocal
manner or with assumed righteous acquiescence.
Perhaps the most striking example of the curious
functioning of so-called parliamentary government in
Japan that has come to light in the present Diet pro-
ceedings is the manner in which the Government has
handled the "bomb-shell" motion of the Seiyukai for
the appropriation of an additional 180,000,000 for
relief purposes. This motion was originally presented
in November at the extraordinary session in a manner
that gave rise to widespread doubt as to whether the
Seiyukai was really concerned over rural economic con-
ditions or was merely attempting to make political capital
out of the motion. On the day of resumption of the
current Diet session, the Seiyukai resuscitated its
motion for an increased relief fund of ¥180,000,000,
launching an attack against the Government for failure
to provide adequate relief for agricultural regions and
those districts that suffered from natural calamities.
Premier Okada, according to press reports, retorted that
the appropriation sanctioned at the extraordinary session
was adequate for relief purposes. The Minister for Finance
stated that he had no idea at present of asking the Diet
for an additional relief appropriation and that he would
recommend
* Embassy's despatch No. 1088 of December 10, 1934.

=== Page 314 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
recommend such an inorease only if there should exist
an actual need for it, not merely for the political
purpose of assuaging the Seiyukai. Notwithstanding
these declarations, the Government subsequently stated
its willingness to grant further relief funds amounting
to ¥30,000,000. The newspapers state that the Cabinet's
proposal to provide such increased relief is primarily
a move to conciliate the Seiyukai and that the plan
only awaits the formal meeting of the Cabinet on February
9 for final approval. The vernacular press has been
vigorous in denouncing the Government's efforts at a
compromise with the Seiyukai. The TOKYO ASAHI states
that the Government ought to dissolve the Diet rather
than temporize with the stagnant political situation,
the NICHI NICHI believes that such a settlement of the
controversy would not be a compromise but a defeat for
the Government, the OSAKA MAINICHI says that the pro-
posed compromise is a Government attempt to prolong its
tenure of office and a scheme to save the Seiyukai's
face, the KOkUMIN remarks that compromises must be
rejected for the sake of parliamentary government, and
the OSAKA ASAHI concludes that Premier Okada is opposed
to dissolution of the Diet because it might be inter-
preted as lack of "unanimous cooperation" in the nation.
Several of the interpellations directed against the
Army in the regular session of the Diet have been more
resolutely framed than those put forth at the extra-
ordinary session. The War and Navy Ministers, however,
have shown no sign that they are willing to compromise
in

=== Page 315 === (confidence 0.99)
in any way. Mr. Takao Saito, of the Minseito (temporarily
a pro-Government party) censured the War Minister for the
publication of the mmch-disoussed Army pamphlet* in
November, 1934, asserting that the pamphlet had been issued
apparently for the purpose of implanting jingoiam in the
minds of the nation and had caused misgivings abroad with
respect to Japan's real intentions. According to the
JAPAN TIMES of January 25, General Hayashi did not reply
to this interpellation. Mr. Saito also attacked the
Government for laying too much emphasis on national defense,
too little on peaceful diplomaoy. A Kokumin Domei inter-
pellator assailed the Cabinet for approving large military
funds to the detriment of industrial appropriations. Mr.
Ashida, a Seiyukai leader, referring to a statement made
by Mr. Takahashi in the extraordinary session of the Diet
to the effect that a "diplomacy-first and defense-second"
polioy should be the guiding principle of the Okada Govern-
ment, remarked that, if one judged from the draft budget
for 1935-36, the Government had been totally indifferent
to the avowed policy. In answer to an interpellation as
to what Japan's naval policy would be after the expiration
of the Washington and London Naval Treaties if these
treaties were not replaced, Admiral Osumi said that he
did not in the least expect a naval race to develop even
if no new naval limitation agreement should be conoluded
and that the present building program of the Japanese
Navy would not have to be materially increased in the
event of a non-treaty situation. A member of the Seiyukai
warned
* Embassy's despatch No. 1031 of November 1, 1934.

=== Page 316 === (confidence 0.98)
一5-
warned the Army against too much interference in politics,
whereupon the War Minister stated that the Army should not
interfere in politics.
Some of the interpellations were apparently made with
a view to currying favor with the military. For example,
a member of the House of Peers raised the question of the
adequacy or inadequacy of Japan's air defense and both the
War and Navy Ministers admitted that Japan's air force was
inadequate, particularly in comparison with the aviation
equipments of the other Powers. Again, Mr. Yasumi of the
Seiyukai asked the Premier if he had adopted the slogan
of the Saito Government "diplomacy first and defense
measures second". Upon being answered in the affirmative,
the interpellator went on to say that in his opinion defense
was at least equal in importance to diplomacy. Premier
Okada then explained that the order of the words in the
slogan was intended not to indicate the relative importance
of the two matters but to signify that defense was to be
invoked only in case diplomacy had failed. According to
the JAPAN ADVERTISER of February 1, the Navy Minister
did not consider the Premier's answer sufficiently clear
and therefore supplemented it with the assertion that
diplomatic negotiations could not be effective unless
supported by the power of armaments. The War Minister
endorsed the Navy Minister's statement. In this conneotion,
it may be remarked that Admiral Osumi's opinion is almost
a national credo.

Mr. Hirota's address on Japan's foreign policy
delivered before the Diet on January 22* attracted con-
siderable attention in the newspapers, both in Japan and
abroad
* Embassy's despatoh No. 1l62 of February 7, 1935.

=== Page 317 === (confidence 0.99)
- 6-
A abroad, and brought forth a certain amount of comment
in the Diet. His statements concerning American-Japanese
and Anglo-Japanese relations contained nothing new, only
reiterating that the relationship in each case was satis-
factorily friendly. There seemed to be a note of optimism
regarding China in the somewhat equivocal statement that
there was a growing tendenoy among the Chinese to appreciate
Japan's intentions in East Asia and that while Japan would
do everything to encourage this tendency, he hoped that
China would collaborate with Japan further in that direotion.
With regard to Soviet Russia, the Minister for Foreign
Affairs stated that Japan had been guided by a spirit of
goodwill in its relations with that country and expressed
the hope that the Soviet Government would "give special
consideration to the creation of military works in the
Far East, especially along the Soviet-Manchukuo frontiers,
with a view to promoting mutual trust and confidence".
A member of the Kokumin Domei, Mr. Yamanichi, ques-
tioned the assurances contained in Mr. Hirota's address
to the effeot that Japan's foreign relations were favorable,
particularly in the case of China, of the Soviet Union,
and of Great Britain. Mr. Ashida of the Seiyukai inter-
pellated the Minister for Foreign Affairs on Japan's future
foreign policy, asking specifically if Mr. Hirota had any
concrete plans regarding Japan's policy toward Russia and
Japan's future course of action after the expiration of
the Washington Naval Treaty. According to the ASAHI SHIMBUN
of January 26, Mr. Hirota's reply, alleged to have been
taken from the stenographic records, was to the effect
that he intended to propose to the Soviet Government a
limitation

=== Page 318 === (confidence 0.99)
-7 -
limitation of defense preparations on the Soviet-
Manehukuo border and, with respeot to Japan's relation
to the international situation, that he was confident
that there would never be war during his tenure of office.
The broad promise of the Minister for Foreign Affairs
about war received considerable favorable comment in
the Japanese newspapers.
The political tactics of the Seiyukai are amply
illustrated by the party's efforts to disoredit the
Okada Government through an impugnment of the patriotism
of Mr. Tokonami, Minister for Communioations. Seizing
upon an isolated and unfounded report that had apparently
rested in oblivion for six months, on January 23 a Sei-
yukai interpellator accused the Communications Minister
of having accepted a bribe of 500,000 yuan from Marshal
Chang Hsueh-liang in 1928. It was alleged that a pam-
phlet emanating from the Japanese military authorities
in Auguat, 1934, had stated that Chang Hsueh-liang had
issued a receipt for the amount of 500,000 yuan to Messrs.
Akatsuka and Tsuruoka as the agents of Mr. Tokonami. At
first the military authorities indignantly denied that
they had had anything to do with the pamphlet in question
and accused the Seiyukai interpellator of trying to injure
the Army's prestige. Subsequently, however, the War Office
was reported to have instituted a search for the receipt
said to have been signed by Chang Hsueh-liang and believed
to be at present in a warehouse of the Kwantung Army at
Hsinking. According to press reports, the charge against
the Communications Minister is still under investigation.
There have been no indications that any material
change

=== Page 319 === (confidence 0.96)
- 8 -
change in the internal politioal situation is likely to
take place in the immediate future or that the desuetude
into whioh party government has fallen in Japan will be
altered, despite the frantic efforts of the Seiyukai to
enfeeble the present Government without going to the
length of demanding a dissolution of the Diet. Nor has
the Cabinet manifested any weakening of its position,
unless one construes as weakness the proposal now under
advisement for the granting of slightly inoreased relief
funds in the 1935-36 budget.

Proceedings of the Diet.
The 67th regular session of the Diet, which had been
adjourned on December 27, was re-opened on January 22.
The salient features of the proceedings up to the close
of the month were the following: Mr. Hirota's speech on
foreign

Tokyo's No. 1217 of March 22, 1935.
This despatch outlines briefly the
proceedings of the Imperial Diet up to
March 21st.
At the time this despatch was written
the Diet had failed to act on important
bills, with the exception of the budget bill.
Two controversles have occupled much
attention recently in the Diet and press.
The first concerns theories regarding the
position of the Emperor -- an extremely
touchy subject in Japan -- expressed in a
book by Dr. Minobe, who is an eminent
scholar and Imperial appointee to the Ilouse
of Peers. A separate despatch deals with
this subject.
Another controversy has raged over
official leniency extended toward reactionary
violence. It was charged in the Diet that
the peril of radicalism had been replaced
by the peril of violent reaction, and that
if any group of ruffians call themselves
a patriotic organization, the Government
lifts the hand of vigilance from them.

Opinion on this subject seems to be divided.
A number of rather significant statements have been
made in the Diet since the Embassy's last despatch on
this subject was written. Several of these statements
are given herewith in some detail because of their peculiar
bearing on certain phases of the present political situation.
On March 5 Mr. Seijun Ando of the Seiyukai issued a scathing
denunciation of reactionary violence in Japan and of the
Government for not only peraitting such violence but also
for using its exponents for Government ends. According
to the JAPAN ADVERTISER of March 6, he said that the
peril of radicalism had been replaced by the peril of
violent reactionism; that unlawful acts and the use of
violence had occurred with remarkable frequency during
the past two years; that the public could hardly continue
to go about its business with ease of mind under such
conditions; and that the recurrence of such events was a
disgraceful blot upon the Showa era. The interpellator
recounted a number of incidents of reectionary violence
that had occurred since the May 15 incident of 1932,
asked the Government's views on crimes that involve
so-called patriots, and enquired as to what steps the
Premier and the Home, Justice, War, and Navy Ministers
intended to take toward the prevention of such happenings
in

=== Page 332 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
in future. Mr. Ando stated that of late the press had
failed to publish the information on diplomatic and
military subjects in which the people were most interested
for fear of violence from reactionary terrorists who go
unmolested. He also remarked that if any member of the
House of Representatives spoke in the Diet against the
wishes of the military, his name was written down on the
list of persons against whom the hand of violence was ever
ready to act. He summed up his assertions as follows:
"If a group of vagabonds gives itself some such name as
Aikoku ("Patriotic") or Kokka ("National Family"), the
Government lifts the hand of vigilance and even goes to
the extreme of utilizing such a group for its own purposes.
I resent this neglect to exercise proper vigilance against
such groups of ruffians". The replies received by the
interpellator from the Premier and the Ministers of Justice,
Home Affairs, and the Navy, as reported by the JAPAN
ADVERTISER of the 6th, were routine in nature and extremely
vague.
 Mr. Ando expressed his dissatisfaction with all
these replies and added that the Navy Minister's answer
sounded like a passage from a school text book.
Mr. Yukio Ozaki, doyen of the House of Representatives
and a veteran exponent of parliamentary government in
Japan, made a speech on March 16 in response to the
Lower House's action in voting a special expression of
gratitude and recognition to him and to five other
Representatives for their services over many years in
the cause of parliamentary administration. He deplored
the death of .r. Tsuyoshi Inukai who had striven with
him for the cause of constitutional administration from
the time the Diet was first organized in 1890. He said
that

=== Page 333 === (confidence 0.99)
- 5 -
that he felt humiliated to be honored at this time, when
party politics had failed so miserably to meet the ex-
pectations of the people, and that the chief reasons for
the loss of confidence in the political parties were their
abuse of political power and the influence of money in the
elections. Furthermore, he stated that it was vain to hope
that any confidence in the political parties could be
restored as long as the custom existed of giving prepon-
derance to official power, which placed the Government
above the people. He went on to remark that members of
parliamentary bodies in foreign countries, specifically
England, were accorded high esteem and he apologized
ironically for citing a foreign country as an example,
since he knew that the trend of the time in Japan was to
reject examples from abroad.
Vith reference to criticisms directed against Dr.
Minobe in the House of Peers for the theory expressed in
his book that the Emperor is an institution,* the Premier
stated on March 4 in the Upper House, according to the
NICHI MICHI of March 5, that the dignity and sanctity of
the Japanese national cheracter could not be expressed in
words and would not be disturbed by academic theory, and
thet he disapproved of the theory that the Emperor is an
institution. On March 20, the House of Peers passed a
resolution, aimed obviously at r. inobe, which deprecates
the tendency in Japan to imitate Western civilization and
thus to disregard the "national spirit" of Japan, und
reconmends that the Government take drastic steps tgainst
persons who enunciate theories that are detrimental to
the national structure of the country. It is interesting
to

No action was taken in a number of
important bills introduced by the Govern-
ment, such as a voluntary rice control
bill, a bill to control reactionary organ-
izations, a bill to control fisheries in
northern waters, a bill to control the
fertilizer industry, a bill to reduce the
tariff on pig-iron, and a bill to
stabilize cocoon production.
Much of the session seems to have been
devoted to debating Dr. Minobe's constitu-
tional theories. Both Houses passed
resolutions urging the Government to take
steps against ideas calculated to damage
the national structure.
The internal political situation
remains unchanged after the close of the
session. The political parties are
largely discredited, and the Government
seems disinclined to force any issues
as

=== Page 336 === (confidence 0.97)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS

as long as its budgets are approved. The
Cabinet apparently continues to enjoy the
backing of the military and is not expected
to fall.

Sir:
With reference to the Embassy's despatches No. 1088
114.
of Decomber 10, 1934, No. 1154 of February 8, 1935, and
142
No. 1217/of March 22, 1935, I have the honor to report
that the 67th session of the Imperial Diet was adjourned
on March 26, nothing having been accomplished in the way
of legislation other than the passage of the Government's
regular and supplementary budget bills for the fiscal year
1935-1936, the extra budget for 1934-1935, and the Govern-
F
ment's Special Business Profits Tax Bill* as amended by
both
*See Monthly Report for November,1934, Section V (1).

=== Page 338 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
both Houses.
It is true that the last-mentioned bill was one of
the Government's major measures, and one that had been
opposed by the political parties since its drafting in
November, 1934. It is likewise true that the supple-
mentary budget bill for 1935-36 includes a provision
which authorizes an appropriation for the organization
of a National Policy Investigation Comnission, another
of the Government's principal projects in the recent
Diet session. However, the Seiyukai majority party have
announced their unwillingness to participate in the mem-
bership of the proposed National Policy Investigation
Commission, although one of the Government's aims in
desiring its establishment is reportedly to create an
impression, at least, of wholehearted national support
of the Cabinet by granting wide representation in the
Comnission.* Furthermore, a nunber of bills which were
introduced by the Government and which the Government
especially desired to have passed were not approved by
the Diet, inclu ing the folloving: the Voluntary Rice
Control Bill providing for State encouragement of co-
operative marketing by farmers; a bill revising the Peace
Preservation Law in order to establish stricter control
over reactionary organizations; a bill for the Control
of fisheries in northern waters; the Fertilizer Industry
Control Bill, designed to regulate prices in the interests
of farmers; a bill for the reduction of the import tariff
on pig-iron in view of the existing shortage of that commo-
dity; and a bill providing for the stabilization of silk
cocoon
*See Monthly Report for January, 1935, Section III (b).

=== Page 339 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
cocoon production. Of these unpassed bills, perhaps the
one that came in for the most heated debate was the Volun-
tary Rice Control Bill, since discussion of it brought out
the divergencies in the interests of the rice dealers and
of the farmers' cooperatives. The bill was finally passed
with slight amendments by the Lower House but the House of
Peers shelved it, alleging that there was not sufficient
time for discussion of the measure.
It will be recalled that on March 20 the Upper House
passed a resolution which recommended that the Government
take drastic steps against enunciators of theories detri-
mental to the national structure of Japan. This resolution
was aimed at Dr. Minobe, who ha? stated in his books on the
Constitution that the Emperor was an institution.* Four
days later the House of Representatives passed a similar
resolution urging the Government to take "drastic steps"
against any speeches or theories that may be deemed incom-
patible with the Empire's national structure. The news-
papers report that the Cabinet will ask Dr. Minobe to re-
vise or delete the passages in his books that arc considered
obnoxious. However, the Cabinet has apparently been unable
to reach a decision on this matter and it is possible that
the whole thing may be dropped.
Although the Cabinet was reported to have considered
prolonging the Diet session for the passage of necessary
legislation, this step was not taken, and despite news-
paper predictions** neither was the Diet dissolved nor
has
*Embassy's despatches No. 1208 of March 29 and No. 1217 of
March 2, 1935.
**Embassy's despatch No. 1217 of March 22, 1935.

=== Page 340 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
has the Cabinet resigned. Thus, the internal political
situation as regards the Government in its relation to the
political parties remains essentially the same as reported
in the Embassy's despatches during the past several months,
namely, a situation characterized by the weakness of the
parties and by the unwillingness of the Government to take
any clear-cut and possibly dangerous action as long as its
budgets are voted, particularly its military appropriations.
It would not appear likely that the Government will fall
through lack of support from the political parties, since
it apparently continues to enjoy the backing of the mili-
tary, in whom the public still has more confidence than it
has in the politicl parties.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew

February 12, 1936
JOMTT
SKH.
Tokyo's No. 1649 of January 22,
1936, gives an account of the dissolution
of the Diet and describes the political
situation.
The reasons for the decision of the
Cabinet to dissolve the Diet were the
obstructive tactics of the Seiyukai party,
which had successfully blocked the Cabinets
program of legislation in the two previous
sessions of the Diet, and the desire of the
Government to avoid a vote on a non-con-
fidence motion, which the Seiyukai was
prepared to present, censoring the
Government for its action with regard to
the Minobe affair.
In any event general elections would
have been called for shortly after the
Diet session, probably in March.
February 20 is set as the date for
elections. The Embassy considers that
the Seiyukai will maintain if not improve
its position in the Diet as the result
of the impending elections.
In any case there appears to be no
expectation of any material change in
Japan's nationalistic foreign policy.

years previously, and a loss for the Minseito, which
party has generally supported the Okada Cabinet. However,
as has happened in the past, the Okada Cabinet may make
such compromise overtures to the Se iyukai or such changes
in its own personnel as to avert a crisis in the next
Diet session, which the Constitution provides shall be
opened within five months of the date of dissolution or
before June 21, 1936.
There would appear to be no cause to look for any
material change in Japan's present nationalistic foreign
policy in the event of a victory for the Seiyukai at
the forthcoming elections and a subseguent overthrow
of the Government. The only criticisn by the Seiyukai
of the Okada Government's forein policy has heretofore
been that it has not been positive enough, especially
wi: rerd to China.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew.

IV. COURSE F LEGISLATION.
According to press reports, the following bills have
been drafted by the Government for presentation in the
Diet after its reconvening on January 2lst:
A bill to place the automobile manufacturing industry
f Janan under a system of control by means of licenses
for the protection of the Japanese industry and for the
prevention of expansion of the foreign automobile com-
panies in Japan.*
A bill providing for a national subsidy to im-
poverished towns and villages of ¥20,00,000 a year.
A bill providing for a revision of the Staple
Industries Control Law to the end that there shall be
more effective control of industries by means of a
permit system.
A bill for the organization of rice control guilds
which shall carry out autonomous control f surolus rice
in Japan, Korea, aná Formosa.
A bill to empower the Government to fix maximum
and minimum rice prices once a month instead of once
a year as formerly.

∴) TEXT OF FOREIGN MINISTER ARITA'S SPEECH BEFORE THE
DIET , MAY 6, 1936. RELEASABLE FOR  EDNESDAY MORNING PAPERS.
TEXT FOLLOWS:
"To insure stability in East Asia contributing thereby
to the cause of world peace and at the same time to promote the
happiness and welfare of mankind by upholding firmly interna-
tional justice has always been our rational policy.
In execution of this policy it should be, I believe,
the guiding spirit of our diplomacy to seek security and develop-
M
ment of our national life as well as to work for concord and co-
operation among all nations aná especially to maintain upon the
894.0
basis of our special and inseperable relationship with Manchoukuo
our position as stabilizing force in East Asia.
We shall of course be independent and positive in dealing
with international questions but we must take care not to impair
the honor and dignity of Japan as a great power and to strive
assiduously to conform to the august wishes of our Sovereign
2
by cultivating as set forth in the Inperial Reserint issued at
the time of Japan's withdrawal from tp Lea ue of Nations mutual
confidence between our mpire und ail other Powers and to make
known the justice of its cause throughout tie world.
Nowadays there prevuils all over the vorld un atmosphere
of aissatisfuction und unrest und tend ncies ure rife every-
where that are threatenine to the preservuation of peace. e
must first of all liminate the causes that u derlie tais
atmosphere of unrest und dissatisf etion if  ure to insure
the peace of the world.
Thile it is only natural that all nations should try to
-1-

=== Page 375 === (confidence 0.98)
42
safeguard their own interests, excess ardor in that attempt of-
ten leads a country to disregard the position of others. This
is one of the prime causes of universal unrest ma today.
One
of
cannot but hope that statesmen/the world will give serious
thought to this point as they address themselves to the task
of readjusting international relations political and economic.
We desire the maintenance of normal and peaceful rela-
tions between Japan and Manchoukuo on the one hund and the
Union of the Soviet Socialist Republics on the other. It is
unnecessary to say that ncither Jupun nor Manchoukuo harbors
any aggressive designs. That there should have occurred
nevertheless ineidents so frequently along frontiers both
between Manchoukuo and the Soviet Union and between lancnoukuo
and Outer Mongolia is most rogrettab!e.
Our Government has already called the attention of the
Soviet authorities to the fact that wunton resort to arms on
the dogmatic ussumption thut trespass has been committed upon
their territories, where there exists no clcar border demarea-
tion, cun serve no useful purpose und only injure friendly
relations between the netions concerned. I umphasize this
point aguin rost stronrly.
The Jupunese Gove rnment after consultetion with the
Governnent of Manehoukuo proposed in the midd le of Mareh
i plun
for tukin: prbper und ffective me sures for tlhe clarifieation

of all torder disputes. We proposed to put this plun into
-2-

execution beginning with the Eastern frontier where disputes
have been most frequent. Now that the Soviet Union has indicat-
ed her concurrence with our views I hope that discussions on
concrete questions will begin in due course.
Direct negotiations are now in progress between Man-

negotiations it is, I am told, the intention of the Government
of Manchoukuo to solve amicably various pending questions and to

hoped that these direct negotiations will result in an eurly
solution of all questions and particularly in the exchange of
representatives between Manchoukuo and Outer Mongolia.
The present relations between Japan and the Soviet Union
cannot be said to be altogether felicitous. Its fundamental
cause lies in the lack of comprehension on the part of Soviet
statesmen of Jupan's position in East Asiu coupled with their
baseless fears und suspicion.
The fact that the Soviet Union maintains excessive arma-
ments at remote outposts in the Far East constitutes a real
menace to th peace in this part of the world. I desire to
state definitely on this occasion that Japan in her solicitude
for peace in East Asia cunnot remuin indifferont to that fuot.

June 9, 1936.
Tokyo's No. 1830 of May 14 discusses
the proceedings of the extraordinary
session of the Diet opened on May 1.
The Emperor's message, usually purely
formal and perfunctory, was of unusual
significance in that it specifically
mentioned the Emperor's regret over the
February 26 incident.
The strength of the various parties
in the Lower House was as follows:
Minseito, 205; Se1yuka1, 171; Showaki, 25;
Shokai Taishuto, 18; Kokumin Dome1, 15;
others 32. A Minseito member, Mr. Kojiro
Tomita, was elected Speaker.
If you have not already read the
speeches of the Premier and the Foreign
Minister you may wish to read the trans-
lations in the clippings appended to the
despatch.
Interpellations were characterized
by unusual frankness and boldness. Much
of the interpellations were directed
against the army and its responsibility
for the February 26 incident. Other
important interpellations criticised the
vagueness of the Government's program for
sound reform and economic renovation. The
Government's replies to questions were
generally

=== Page 381 === (confidence 0.97)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
generally evasive and equivocal.
The three largest political parties
vigorously assailed the Government, but
have shown no disposition to attempt to
overthrow the Cabinet. Their aim seemed
to be to make the Cabinet live up to its
responsibilities.
The Embassy comments, in conclusion,
that the bills introduced by the Government,
even if passed, will not go very far toward
allaying public unrest or effecting the
desired reformation in economic and social
matters.

The extraordinary session of the Diet was opened
onMay 1 and the Emperor's Message was read before both
Houses on May 4. This Message, which had probably been
suggested by Premier Hirota, was of unusual significance
39
in that it specifically mentioned the Emperor's regret
over the February 26 Incident. The Emperor's concern
over political and military affairs had already been
expressed in a statement issued by Mr. Matsudaira, the
7
Imperial Household Minister, to the effect that His
Majesty had spent sleepless nights in receiving officials
and hearing reports on the development of the Incident.
The JAPAN ADVERTISER of April 28 reported that the Govern-
ment planned to allow free debate in the forthcoming Diet
session,

=== Page 383 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
session, with the primary object of allaying public
unrest.
THE POLITICAL PARTIES
Before the convening of the Diet the Minseito
Party, which had supported the Okada Government and
which has the greatest numerical strength in the pre-
sent session, announced the following platform: (1)
an
establishment ofaindependent foreign polioy; (2) formu-
lation of a national defense program for the maintenance
of peace in the Far East; (3) stabilization of the
"living of the masses"; (4) reformation of financial
and taxation policies; (5) establishment of a new par-
liamentary system to meet all national needs; (6) funda-
mental reorganization of central and local administrative
systems; (7) reformation of the judicial system; and
(8) a reorganization of the system of education to make
it more practical. This statement of policies represents
an orientation to the changed situation after the in-
surrection of February 26 and would appear to be a re-
vision of ideas to meet the platform of the new Government.
The Minseito and the Seiyukai (the next party in
the Diet in nunerical strength) were reported to have
reached an agreement to comply with the Imperial Wish,
as expressed in the Emperor's Message of May 4, for the
cooperation of the Government and the people to effeot
national progress. On the other hand, both the Kokumin
Domei and the Shakai Taishuto (Social Mass Party) were
said to be in opposition to the Government on the ground
of the continuance of Martial Law. Declaring itself the
sole opposition party in the Diet, the Shakei Taishuto
1ssued

=== Page 384 === (confidence 0.98)
一 3-
issued a statement on May 1 charging the Govermment
with failure to recognize the fact that the present
social unrest is due to "the conflicting elements of
capitalism in Japan" and to neglect of the masses. This
party also reiterated its February electoral platform,
namely, the establishment of labor parlimmentary poli-
tics, opposition to increased tares on the masses and
advocacy of increased taxes on capitalists, establishment
of a national pension system, State management of major in-
dustries, and state compensation for agrioultural losses.*
According to press reports, the strength of the
various Parties in the House of Representatives at the
opening of the Diet session was as follows: Minseito....205;
Seiyukai....171; Showakai....25; Shakai Taishuto....18;
Kokumin Domei....15; others....32. Mr. Kojiro Tomita,
as a representative of the Minseito, the party with the
largest representation in the Lower House, was elected
Speaker of that body on the day of the Die t's opening.
BILLS INTRODUCED
The most important bills thus far introduced in the
Diet are the following: a bill for the control of seditious
publications harmful to public peace and order providing
for a three years' prison term for those persons guilty
of putting forth documents and illustrations for the pur-
pose of disturbing peace and order, such as stirring up
the public mind, unsettling military discipline, and dis-
turbing financial circles" and likewise for persons guilty
of
*Imbassy's despatch No. 1706 of March 5, 1936.

=== Page 385 === (confidence 0.99)
一4 -
of circulating *rumors or false reports for the purpose
of disturbing peace and order, regardless of the methods
used"; a bill requiring that industrialists establish
retirement fund allowances for their employees; a bill
for the eatablishment of monetary organs for the benefit
of small merohants and industrialists; a bill for the
control of staple industries; a bill for the control of
the fertilizer industry; a bill to extend the life of the
Trade Protection Law, which provides for retaliatory mea-
sures against countries that restrict Japanese imports,
and as a counterbalance three bills providing for the
voluntary control of Japanese exports; a bill for the
control of the automobile industry in Japan by the es-
tablishment of a license system under which foreign auto-
mobile firms shall not be allowed to operate in Japan un-
less Japanese are admitted to ownership of a majority of
the stock;* a bill for the control of shipping routes; and
a bill revising the Iron Industry Protection Law.
The JAPAN ADVERTISER of April 8 reported that the
Cabinet mould introduce a bill providing for a broader
and more rigid protection of military secrets with stricter
provisions for the restriction on surveying, photographing,
and sketching of certain areas on land and sea to be de-
signated by the Government. The text of those bills enacted
into law will be reported to the Department as soon as
they have been promulgated in the OFFICIAL GAZETTE.
The seditious publications bill mas attacked in the
House of Represen tatives by the Minseito, the Seiyukai,
and the Shakai Taishuto on the ground that it would be
arbitrarily
*Embassy's telegram No. 165 of August 10,1935 and Embassy's
despatohes No. 1431 of August 10,1935 and No. 1440 of
August 22, 1935.

=== Page 386 === (confidence 0.98)
一 5 -
arbitrarily used for the restraining of freedom of
speech. This bill was also opposed in the House of
Peers. Members of the House of Representatives assailed
the three rice control bills, asserting that they were
antiquated in character.
CABINET MRMBERS' SPEECHES
The initial addresses delivered before the Diet on
May 6 by the Premier and the Ministers for Foreign Af-
fairs, War, and Finance were along the same lines as
the public utterances made by these officials since
the formation of the Hirota Cabinet on March 9, i.e.,
in consonance with the demands issued by the Army as a
condition for the formation of a new Cabinet.* Premier
Hirota deplored the February 26 Incident on account of the
anxiety caused thereby to the Throne as well as the re-
grettable deaths of high officials of State, attributed the
affair to subversive activities on the part of unruly ele-
ments inside and outside the Army, and expressed the
opinion that the Incident mould not cause the foundation
of the Army to be affected in the slightest way. He went
on to state that the Army mas enforcing stricter discipline
and that at the same time the Cabinet was determined to
eliminate the "deep-rooted evils of the situation" to
bring about a renovation of the national administration;
that the Government and Diet must avoid extremes and follow
the parliamentary system of politics created by the Consti-
tution which the Rmperor Meiji granted; that a faulty appli-
cation of constitutional politics had induced some people
to
*Rmbassy's despatches No. 1736 of March 18,1936, No. 1733 of
March 19, 1936 and No. 1803 of May 1,1936.

=== Page 387 === (confidence 0.99)
to resort to violence; that the Government was deter-
mined to devise effective measures to clarify the na-
tional policy; that Western culture had contributed
greatly to Japan's advancement but had given rise to
various evils that must now be remedied through edu-
cational changes; that as the stabilizing force in
East Asia Japan must strive for the welfare of humanity
on the basis of co-existence and co-prosperity dth
various nations in the Orient, and especially on the
basis of the inseparable relations between Japan and
"Manchukuo"; that Japan could not understand the real
intentions of the Soviet Union with respect to border
disputes and the excessive armaments of that country in
the Far East; that it was gratifying to note the peaceful
political situation in China but that one could not ignore
that this situation was fraught with difficulties; that
the Government had to replenish national defense in view
of the actions of foreign pomers; that Japan must be pre-
pared for inoreased national expendi tures and that a new
revenue policy would have to be established with this in
view; that Japan's trade with foreign countries continued
to be favorable but that the Government would have to take
proper steps to combat the obstacles confronting Japan's
trade; that the enforcement of stricter official discipline
and the renovation of administrative affairs were urgently
necessary from the far-sighted point of view; and that the
existing situation was "grave indeed". A copy of the JAPAN
1/
ADVERTISER's translation of Mr. Hirota's speech is enclosed.
The speech of the Minister for Foreign Affairs, a
translation

=== Page 388 === (confidence 0.98)

z/ translation of which is enclosed as published by the
JAPAN TIMES of May 6, stressed the necessity of main-
taining normal and peaceful relations between Japan and
"Manchukuo" on the one hand and the Soviet Union on the
other, relations which could not be called felicitous
at present; that Japen would expedite negotiations with
China on the basis of the three principles expounded at
the last session of the Diet by the then Foreign Minister,
Mr. Hirota; that Japan was anxiously watching the influx
of communist influence into east Asia; that the adjustment
of the respective interests of Japan and Great Britain
should not prove difficult if the two countries kept in
mind their old friendship and their common responsibilities
toward morld peace; and that the "recent unfortunate In-
cident in Tokyo" might have caused untoward consequences
in Japan's foreign relations but for the august virtue
of the Emperor and the spirit of loyalty and solidarity
manifested by the Japanese people. With regard to the
United States, Mr. Arita avomed that he would exert his

amity and understandi ng; asserted that in Japanese-American
friendship lay the very key to the maintenance of peace
in the Pacific; and remarked that not only mere the economic
relations of the two countries complementary but that also
mutual understanding between the two countries was becom-
ing deeper.
The substance of the War Minister's speech was that
discipline in the Army remained entirely unaffected by the
February 26 Incident; that the military authorities were
taking

=== Page 389 === (confidence 0.99)
-8-
taking every precaution to control any aftermath which
might result from the uprising; that the soldiers involved
in the affair were conscious of the gravity of their
actions and were showing signs of penitence; and that the
Army mould make known the particulars of the Inoident
at the proper time.
Mr. Baba, the Finance Minister, said that national
expenditures were bound to increase; that some of this
increase could be met by the issuance of deficit bonds;
but that more revenue must be sought through a reforma-
tion of the tax system.
INTERPELLATIONS AND GOVERNMENT REPLIES
Interpellations during the present session of the
Diet have been characterized by a frankness and a boldness
which were lacking in those made during previous sessions.
As was to have been expected, provided the Government car-
ried out the promise mentioned above to allo free debate,
a great part of the interpellations have been directed
against the Army in general and the responsibility of the
military for the February 26 Incident in particular.
Other important interpellations have been critical of the
vagueness of the Government's program and the lack of con-
crete measures for social reform. The most striking speech
yet delivered in the Diet was that made on May 7 by Mr.
Takao Saito of the Minseito, who spoke courageously for
liberaliam and roundly berated the Army for its responsi-
bility for the February 26 assassinations.* In an inter-
view with the JAPAN ADVERTISER on May 13 Mr. Saito was
guoted
*Embassy's despatch No. 1826 of May 13, 1936.

=== Page 390 === (confidence 0.99)
- 9 -
quoted by this newspaper as having said that he would
even give his life in the name of constitutional govern-
ment in Japan; that he was fundamentally a liberal; that
he had studied in the United States at Yale University
and that in his opinion merican liberalism had had
some effect on him, al though he had been a liberal since
hia student days in Japan; that in his 25 years as a
member of the Diet he had never seen a time when consti-
tutional government in Japan needed to be championed
more than it did today; and that parliamentarianimm and
political and economic freedom must be preserved if Japan
was to raise the standard of national life and to become
a real force in the advance of human civilization.
Mr. Hisashi Aso of the Shakai Taishuto endorsed Mr.
Saito's opinions on the day following the latter's speech
and added that social unrest mas one of the contributory
causes of the Incident and that the Government had no
concrete plans for social and economio renovation. On
March 12 Mr. Chokichi Miyamak: of the Seiyukai blamed the
military authorities for not l..ving watched the actions
of the rebellious officers, sinc- the latter were known
to hold subversive ideas; censured the Army for discussing
such a wide range of subjects as political, economic, agri-
cultural and other questions, which are under the juris-
diotion of various Departments; asserted that the public
was laughing at the Army because such questions could not
be so easily understood; and severely criticized the Army
for

=== Page 391 === (confidence 0.99)
-10-
for having supplied food to the rebels during the
February 26 Inoident and for not having suppressed
the inmurrectionary movement at once by armed force.
The reply of the War Minister to these criticisms was
that he would do his best. On the same day Mr. Takeo
Sugiura, Independent, asked the War Minister if the
recent outbreak had not been due to the lenient atti-
tude taken by the authorities concerned toward the men
involved in the May 15 Incident, to which General Tera-
uchi replied that this may have been one of the causes
but that it had not been the single cause of the Incident.
Mr. Kojiro Iwasaki of the Seiyukai charged that
since the beginning of the Showe era the military schools
had been inculcating extreme ideas in the students and
the War Minister emphatically denied this allegation. Mr.
Kojiro Tsutsumi of the Minseito took strong exception to
the Friday conferences of the Ministers of War, Navy, and
Foreign Affairs decided upon on April 24* on the score that
such conferences would have an unfavorable infl uence on
the national mind for the reason that they would give the
impression that diplomacy was controlled by the War and
Navy Ministers. Dr. Etsujiro Uehara of the Seiyukai cri-
ticized the present system of appointing a War Minister
only upon the recommendation of the Army, saying that
such a procedure was contrary to the interests of parlia-
mentary government. Furthermore, he told the Minister for
Foreign Affairs that it was the latter's duty to make it
clear to various countries that Japan's commercial advance-
ment abroad mas not for the purpose of territorial
ambition
*See Monthly Report for April,1936, Section III (a)

=== Page 392 === (confidence 0.99)

ambition.
Secret sessions were held in both Houses at which
the War Minister replied to pointed questions concern-
ing the February 26 Incident. Nothing definite is
known concerning General Terauchi's replies in the Lower
House, other than the report that he stated that it was
not a fundamental defect in the Army's system of train-
ing that had been responsible for the Incident. However,
according to DOMEI of May 7, when questioned on inter-
ferenoe in political affairs by the military the War
Minister replied that the political views of thm Army men
should be expressed through the War Office. According
to the same news agenoy, Viscount Yukimitsu of the
House of Peers put the following questions to the War
Minister: what are the prospects for the trial of the
guilty officers?; about when was the rebellious nature
of the Incident recognized?; why mas a plot of such an
extensive nature not detected beforehand? General Tere-
uchi's replies were reported to have been the following:
that he could say nothing as to the date of the trial;
that he felt deep regret over the Army's failure to de-
tect the plot; and that the rebellious nature of the In-
cident had been recognized by the authorities "from the
time the barracks were left".
On May 12 the War Minister refused to disclose more
details of the causes of the February 26 Incident, after
having been asked to do so by Mr. Chokichi Miyawaki of
the Minseito, whereupon the interpellator issued the
warning that refusal to divulge facts concerning the
Incident

=== Page 393 === (confidence 0.99)
-12-
Incident was likely to estrange the Army from the people.
Premier Hirota was questioned by several Diet mem-
bers concerning the actual steps being taken by him to
settle Japanese-Soviet Russian difficulties peacefully
and his replies were evasive. On May 13 Mr. Tomesaburo
Shimizu of the Minseito asked Admiral Nagano, the Navy
Minister, whether there would be competition in naval
building as a result of the non-treaty status and what
the ship buildi ng capacities of Japan and the United
States were. The Navy Minister's reply mas that he was
not in a position to answer this question.
There have been a number of frank interpellations
on the Government's economic and social policies. For
example, on May 8 Mr. Hisayoshi Muramatsu of the Minseito
stated that any controlled economy not used to stabilize
the people's living would fail and issued a marning
against an increase in taxes on the masses. Similar in-
terpellations elicited from Mr. Baba, the Finance Minister,
the statement that he mouid avoid as much as possible the
imposition of taxes on the masses in effecting a revision
of the existing taxation system. In interpellating the
War Minister on the unsatisfactory state of Japan's in-
dustries from the viempoint of national defense, Mr.
Kanichiro Kamei of the Social Mass Party asserted that
the present capitalistic system of profit-seeking was
inadequate for rectifying this condition and inquired if
General Terauchi did not consider it necessary to resort
to planned economic measures backed by national control.
The War Minister replied vaguely that he agreed with the
interpellator

=== Page 394 === (confidence 0.98)
-13-
interpellator on some points but that he mished to pro-
ceed only after conferring with various circles.
A member of the Seiyukai accused the police of
having violated the Consti tution in their treatment of
persons arrested for alleged violation of the election
laws, adding that many persons had been arrested and
even punished for crimes which they had not committed
and that for this reason people had resolved to have
nothing to do with the eleotions in the future. Accord-
ing to the JAPAN ADVERTISER of May 9, the Home Minister
began his reply as follows: "It may be that many police-
men, out of too much zeal....", whereupon he was inter-
rupted by shouts of disapproval from a large part of
the members of the House of Representatives present.
It will be observed from the interpellations reported
above that they covered a wide variety of subjects; that
they dealt with the most pertinent and fundamental issues
of the moment; that they applied especially to the after-
math of the February 26 Incident; that the hitherto
dreaded military circles were freely criticized; that the
Government's replies to clearly-morded queries were
generally evasive and equivocal; and that three political
parties (the Minseito, the Seiyukmi, and the Shakai Tai-
shuto) vigorously assailed the Government.
Editorial comment in general concerning the Diet pro-
ceedings was critical of the Army and skeptical of the
new Cabinet's announced intention of bringing about the
necessary reforms. This fact may be taken as at least
二

=== Page 395 === (confidence 0.99)
-14-
a temporary indication of a more liberal atmosphere
in Japanese politics.
CONCLUSION
The conclusions to be drawn from the Diet pro-
ceedings up to date may be summarized as folloms: the
Government's bills, even if passed, will not go very
far toward allaying public unrest or the suspicions
of the Army and another "Incident* of some sort is a
possibility,in view of the new Government's apparent
unwillingness or inability to carry out reforms hoped
for by a large section of the Army and of the people.*
In this connection it is interesting to note that, ac-
cording to the NICHI NICHI of May 13, dissatisfaction
is becoming vocal in the Army over what is termed lack
of proper zeal among members of the Diet and of the
Government to cooperate with the military in dealing
with the emergency situation, as evidenced by the in-
terpellations of members of the Lower House and the
Government's replies thereto, a situation considered
by the Army to be very regrettable in viem of the "fact
that disoipline in the Army has now been restored to
normal".
While the political parties have criticized the
Government wholeheartedly they have so far not done this
with the idea of ousting the Cabinet, as did the Seiyukai
in the February session of the Diet. There has as yet
been no intimation of a motion of non-confidence in the
Government. The parties have attacked the Cabinet es-
sentially with a view to making it live up to its res-
ponsibilities, ance it has been accepted, willy-nilly,
a8
*Embassy's despatch No. 1833 of May 14, 1936.

=== Page 396 === (confidence 0.97)
-15-
as an emergenoy Government forced upon the nation to
tide over the situation created by the recent Incident.
It is true that the War Minister has to some extent correct-
ly voiced the intent of the present military authorities
to wipe out subversive elements in the Army, as illustrated
by the trial and death sentence of Lieutenant-Colonel Ai-
zawa.* Nevertheless, the evasiveness of General Terauchi's
replies to interpellations and his refusal to give speci-
fic details on the causesof the February 26 Incident and on
the trial of the officers involved therein give rise in
some quarters to the belier that the belligerently chau-
vinist element in the Army has not been definitely dis-
covered or eliminated. The Embassy will continue to ob-
serve and report on the Diet proceedings.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew

=== Page 403 === (confidence 0.98)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
July 6, 1936.
The Tokyo Embassy's despatch No. 1869,
May 29, 1936, outlines the proceedings of
the 69th Diet session. This session was
characterized by a freedom of speech, a
seriousness of purpose, and a lack of
political manoeuvering that were foreign
to other sessions in the immediate past.
There were many sharp criticisms voiced
of the lack of discipline in the army
and of the failure of the government to
carry out its pledges of administrative
and economic reform.
Forty-five bills were passed in the
session. The Embassy has stated that it
will submit further reports on the
legislation enacted after publication
in the OFFICIAL GAZETTE. Most of the
bills passed have little political, economic,
or social significance. From the American
standpoint the bill for the control of
the automobile industry is most important.
As the despatch is largely an account
of the "high lights" of the Diet session
you probably will not care to read it.

I have the honor to submit a report on the last
two weeks of the Diet session which opened on May 1
and was formally closed on the morning of May 27.*
The session was scheduled to end on May 25 but was
extended for two days by Imperial Ordinances to per-

Interpellations and Resolutions
As reported in the Embassy's last despatch on the
cr
political parties were unusually frank and unhampered
in their criticisms of both the Army and the Government.
In the latter half of the session there were further
F/FG
interpellations
*Embassy's despatch No. 1830 of May 14, 1936.

=== Page 405 === (confidence 0.99)
-2
interpellations concerning the lack of discipline in
the Army and the failure of the Government to provide
concrete measures for the reform of social and economic
conditions and for the clarification of the national
polity. On May 15 the Kokumin Domei, the Shakai Taishuto
(the Social Mass Party), and the Independents introduced
a resolution in the House of Representatives asking the
Government to call another special session of the Diet
for the reason that the bills thus far introduced by the
Government, including the budget bill, failed to meet the
need for the "renovation of Japan's administration in
all branches".
A few of the more interesting interpellations, which
illustrate the atmosphere of the Diet session just passed,
aregiven below as of possible interest to the Departaent.
On May 18 Mr. Uchida of the Koyukai asked the Premier if
his present advocacy of administrative reforms was not
due to the fact that he shared the views of the men in-
volved in the February 26 Incident, which question Mr.
Hirota was reported by the press to have ignored. Several
interpellations were made concerning the police precautions
that were being taken to prevent the occurrence of similar
outbreaks. Mr. Kanju Kato of the Shakai Taishuto demanded
of the Government that the Army remove the cause of possi-
ble scandals in connection with the allotment of orders
to munitions firms and at the same time made the charge
that the profits of munitions manufacturers were increasi ng
while the wages of laborers were decreasing. The Sei yukai
attacked

=== Page 406 === (confidence 0.98)
一 3 -
attacked the bill for the control of seditious publica-
tions on the ground that it would be used arbitrarily
by the Government against the civilian population when
all the so-called seditious literature in question came
from the ranks of the Army. Mr. Riyo Koyama, Independent,
refused to support the resolution of the House of Repre-
sentatives which pledged faithful observance of the Im-
perial wishes set forth in the Rescript for the Diet
opening. After this act of negation, Mr. Koyama was
reported to have stated to the press that neither the
Government nor the political parties had given sufficient
evidence of their sincerity of purpose, parti cularly d th
regard to the welfare of Japanese laborers and farmers.
One of the interpellations that created a furor in
1
the House of Peers was that made by Mr. Jusha Tsumura,
who questioned the courage of Japanese Army and Navy offi-
cers and remarked that the men in the ranks seemed to be
more loyal than their superiors. Mr. Tsumura stated fur-
ther that Lieutenant Colonel Aizawa had murdered Ma jor
General Nagata and had afterward believed that he would
be allowed to proceed to his post in Formosa without
punishment; that the Arny had purposely delayed Aizawa's
trial and had given him a pension; that after the sentence
of death pronounced upon him by the Court Martial he had
gone so far as to appeal the decision; and that a matter
such as this would undoubtedly have a bad influence on
public opinion. Mr. Tsumura formally apologized for his
rash words and resigned is seat under pressure, reported-
ly because of the ire of the Army and the Navy over his
8lur

=== Page 407 === (confidence 0.98)
一4 -
slur about officers in the two services. Homever, a
member of the House of Peers told the Counselor of
the Embassy confidentially that Tsumura had been in
bad odor with his colleagues; that he had been ap-
pointed to the House of Peers as a representative of
the highest taxpayers; that he had for some time used
his position in the House of Peers as a moans of in-
creasing the sale of his patent medicine: and that
members of the Upper House had been anxious to get rid
of him on almost any pretext.
Legislation Enacted
The most important debates centered around the
bills for the protection of national mobilization secrets,
the control of seditious publications, and the establish-
ment of retirement allowance funds for employees by
their employers. According to the newspapers, the first-
named bill failed of passage since the political parties
were bitterly opposed to the vagueness of its terms and
consequently held the suspicion that the Government woul d
be able to use the bill, if enacted into law, in an arbi-
trary and dictatorial fashion. After much discussion
and amendment the employees' retirement al lowance bill,
the only social measure of importance introduced in the
Diet, and the seditious publications bill were passed.
The latter bill, as amended in the Diet, forbids the
publication of "mysterious literature", which includes
anonymous documents and letters tending to disturb mili-
tary order, financial circles, or public thoughts, and
which

=== Page 408 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
whioh provides imprisonment for not more than three
years if such literature is deliberately circulated
for the purpose of creating a disturbance and a maxi-
mum penalty of two years if the intent to create a
disturbance is la cking.
Of the forty-five bills passed by the Diet forty-
three were measures drafted by the Okada Cabinet and
the other two, drafted by the present Cabinet, were
those mentioned above. Among the other more important
enactions were the following: a bill for the control
of the automobile industry in Japan by the establish-
ment of a license system under which new foreign auto-
mobile firms shall not be allowed to operate in Japan
unless Japanese subjects are admitted to ownership of
a majority of the stock;* a bill extending the life of
2
the lam for the control of basic industries; a bill for
3
the creation of a central bank for commercial and in-
4
dustrial guilds; a shipping service control bill pro-
viding that when the Government recognizes the existence
of ruinous competition in a steamship service it will
have the authority to advise the interested parties to
stop com eticion and in the event of refusal the Govern-
ment will be empowered to prohibit navigation; a bill
for the control of the fertilizer industry; three rice
control bills; a bill for the control of exports of
1
cotton textiles, silk and rayon textiles, procelain, rubber
shoes, matches, electric lamps, and other commodities.
The Government's general budget bill amounting to
¥2,310,529,000 for the fiscal year 1936-37, about
¥28,000,000

¥28,000,000 in excess of the l936-37 budget drafted
by former Finance Minister Takahashi, was approved by
both Houses. This excess is principally accounted for
by increased military expenditures, which the Army demanded
as a condition for participating in the recently formed
Hirota Cabinet. Thus, the budget for the coming fiscal
year is more than the 1935-36 budget by 195,115,000. The
general budget figure does not include the Special Accounts,
such as expenditures for Chosen, Taiwan, the Kwantung
Leased Territory, communications, etc., which it is thought
111 amount to about ¥829,000,000.
All five political parties of the Lower House made
statements on May 26 commenting upon the Diet session.
The Minseito announced its decision to maintain a permanent
committee to see that the Government fulfils its promises
to carry out certain resolutions attached to various laws
enacted. In the Seiyukci announcement it was asserted that
the party had co-operated with the Government and with the
Army during the Diet session and that it was now up to the
Government and the Army to show what they would do; that
if administrative reforms and strict Aray discipline were
not achieved the Government and the Army would be responsi-
ble to the peoples' representatives at the next Diet session.
The Kokunin Domei atated that the ma jor political parties
seemed to be paying only lip service to the cause of
administrative and economic reform. The Shakai Taishuto
stated that the present Cabinet seemed to be little different
from the Saito and Okada régimes. The Showakai statement
praised

=== Page 410 === (confidence 0.99)
-7 -
praised the Government for the passage of its bills
in the Diet and in general was more favorable toward
the Government than the statements of the other poli-
tical parties.
Conolusion
The recent session of the Diet was charaoterized
by a freedom of speech, a seriousness of purpose, and
a lack of political maneuvering that were foreign to
other sessions in the immediate past. This session dif-
fered from other recent sessions in two distinct respects:
Diet members criticized the Army and the Government
bluntly yet at the same time approved all of the Govern-
ment's bills, save one, whereas in the two prior sessions
the Government's program of legislation was blocked for
the ends of the political parties and the parties criti-
cized the Government only mildly. Most of the bills
passed in the recent session have little political, economic,
or social significance. In the Diet session just ended
the political parties consistently censured the Government
for past evils and warned it with regard to the future.
This anomolous situation can be largely explained by
the February 26 Incident, which overthrew t.e Okada Cabinet
and brought about the formation of a new Government, whose
announced policy is one of social and economic reform to
remedy certain maladjustments. Apparently the political
parties decided to exert one more effort for parliamentary
politics and at the same time to be tolerant of the obvicus-
ly vague measures thus far adopted by the Government for
"renovation",

=== Page 411 === (confidence 0.97)
- 8 -
"renovation", thus giving the present Cabinet a chance
to carry out its promises in the course of time. As
stated in previous despatches, there is a definite possi-
bility of further internal political disturbances if the
Army does not execute its pledge to establish stricter
discipline in its ranks and if the Government does not
fulfil its promises of reform in economic and social
matters.*
The Embassy will submit a further report on the
legislation enacted during the recent Diet session hen
published in the OFFICIAL GAZETTE.
Respectfully yours,
Joseph C. Grew

September 14, 1936.
Tokyo's No. 1998 of August 21, 1936,
contains a digest of the forty-five laws
passed at the extraordinary session of the
Diet in May.
The Embassy states that translations of
the more important laws not already reported
are being prepared, and will be sent in the
near future.
Note that laws as follows were enacted:
(a) a law extending the term of
the Law for the Regulation and
Protection of Foreign Trade;
(b) a law requiring a permit to
export certain important staples;
(c) a law amending the Aviation
Law so as to prohibit foreign airplanes
from transporting passengers or
freight for pay between Japan and any
foreign country. This law seals Japan
against foreign commercial air
transportation.
