DIGEST OF TE FORTY-FIVE LANS LASSED AT TE DIET SESSION
W. ICT CLOSED ON LMAY 27, 1936.
Law No. 1 (Promulgated May 22, 1936)
A Law Providing for the Amendment of Law No. 45 of 1934,
namely, a Law Providing for the Regulation and Protection of
Foreign Trade.
The original law as promulgated in April, 1934, was ef-
fective only for a period of three years from the date of
its enforcement. By the new measure, the period of the
effectiveness of the Law has been extended from three years
to six years. In other words, the Law remains effective
until April 30, 1940.
Law No. 2 (Promulgated May 22, 1936)
A Law Providing for the Amendment of the National Tax
Collection Law (Promulgated on April 1, 1897).
An amendment made to the original Law (2nd Paragraph,
Article 5) provides that to meet the expenses necessary
for collecting national taxes (land tax and other taxes
imposed by Imperial ordinance. in cities, towns, and villa-
ges the Government shall grant to such cities, tovms, and
villages a certain amount to be determined by Imperial
ordinance. Under the former system, the amount of the
grant-in-aid by the Government to cities, towms, and villa-
ges for defraying the exoenses necessary for the collection
of such taxes vas fixed at 3. of the amount of taxes collect-
ed by the respective cities, tovms, end villages.
Law No. 3 (Promulgated May 25, 1936)
A Law Providing ror the Issuance of a etional Loan in
Order to Meet the xpenditures Stipulated in the General
Budget for the Fiscal Year 193o-37.
Article 1 of this Law provides that for the purpose
stated above, the Government  ay issue public bonds or borrow
money to the extent of Yen 513,000,000 apart from the amount
of public loans which the Government is authorized to issue
under the terms of laws other than the present Law. Article
2 provides that for the purpose of obtaining the amount of
money which must be carried forward to the fiscal year
1937-33 in the General Budget for 1936-37, the Government
may issue public 1 ans or borrrow money a art irom the
amount of loans the Government is authorized to issue under
law: other than the present one but that the total emount
of money to be raised under Articles 1 and 2 of the present
Law shall not exceed Yen 513,C00,000.
The Law further provides that in case there is a necessity
to cover the difference in the face value end the iscue value
of such loans issued under Articles 1 and 2, the Government may
issue

=== Page 417 === (confidence 0.98)
- 2 -
issue public loans or borrow money beyond the pre-
scribed limit of Yen 513,000,000.
Law No. 4 (Promulgated May 25, 1936)
A Law Providing for the Transfer of Special Accounts
Funds to the General Accounts for the Purpose of Meeting
the Expenditures Stipulated in the General Budget for
the Fiscal Year 1936-37.
For the purpose stated above this law authorizes the
Government to transfer to the general accounts funds
belonging to the special accounts for Communications,
Railways, the Kwantung Bureau, the Chosen Government-
General, the Taiwan Government-General, the Karafuto
Office, and the South Seas Office.
Law No. 5. (Promulgated Liay 25, 1936)
A Law Providing for the Amendment of Law Mo. 1 of 1932.
Law No. 1 of 1932 authorized the Government to issue
a public loan or borrow money to the extent of Yen 820,
600,o00 for the purpose of defraying the expenses of the
Manchurian Incident, apart from the amount of the loans
which the Government was authorized to raise under Im-
perial ordinances o. 6, .o. 14 and No. 19 of 1932.
Under the tems of the new Lew, the limit of the amount
of loans to be raised or money to be borrowed by the
Government in connection with the Manchurian Incident
has been increased from Yen 820,600,000 t0¥994,500,000.
Law No. 6 (Pronulgated ..ay 25, 1936)
A Lew Providing for the Amendment of Law Ho. 7 of 1934.
Law o. 7 of 1934 provided for the issuance of a
national loan to the amount of Yen 50,0c0,000 for the
purpose of granting cash gratuities to officers and
others who rendered meritorious services in connection
with the .anchurian Incident. Under the terms of the
present amendment law, the limit of the loan to be
issued by the Government has been incret sed from
Yen 50,C00,000 to Yen 58,4c0,000.
Law No. 7 (Pronulgated …ay 25, 1936)
A Law -rovi ing for the Amendment of Law  o. 52 of
1923 (A Law Concerning the ualifications of Judicial
Probationers and Lawyers.)
The amendment has no significance, it being merely
an alteration of e few words used in the text of the
ola law.
Law No. 8 (Promulgated May 25, 1936)
A Law rroviding for the Amendment of the Law Re-
lating to the Special Loans made by, the Bank of Japan
during the financial anic of 1927 Andemnification of
losses incurred thereby.

=== Page 418 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
To relieve the banks in distress which were involved
in the financial panic of the spring of 1927, the Govern-
ment enacted the Special Aocommodation Act authorising
the Bank of Japan to advance loans to those hanks which
were in distress in May, 1927, with the guaranty of the
Government to indemnify the losses incurred by the
Bank on that account. The ten-year period for the special
accomodation by means of discount of bills drawn for
the purpose of rewriting the bills mentioned in Article
1 of the old lew is extended by the new law to twenty
years.
Law No. 9 (Promulgated May 25, 1936)
A Law Relating to the Control of the Method of Dis-
posal of Silk Worm Cocoons.
This is a new act defining the methods of disposing
of silk worm cocoons raised by sericulturists as follows:
(1) transactions in dried cocoons, (2) transactions in
raw cocoons according to contracts made before the har-
vesting of the cocoon crop, (3) reeling into yarns by
sericultural or other industrial associations, end (4)
other vays specially designated by Imperial ordinance.
The law further provides for detailed rules for the
official examination of cocoons.
Law o. 10 (1romulgated May 25, 1936)
A Law -roviding for the Amendment of the Law of Seri-
cultural Associations (originelly enacted as Law o. 24 of
1931 and promulgated on March 30 of that year).
The revised measure affects 19 articles of the original
Law Providing for the Organization of Sericultural Asso-
ciations, qualifications of the members thereof, rules
relating to the dissolution of such associations, etc.,etc.
Law No. 11 (Promulgated ay 25, 1936)
A Law Froviding for the Amendment of the Sericultural
Industry Law (originally enacted as Lew Ho. 47 of lall
and promulated on iarch 29 of that year).
The revised law affects several articles of the original
Law Concerning the steblishment of Joint Sericultural
Associations for the Purpose of Promoting the Develonment
of Sericultural Interests and accelerating the improvement
of sericultural products through the cooperation of
members. The revision does not in the least affect the
fundamental nature of the original law.
Law No. 1 (Promulgated May , 1936)
A Law Iroviding for the Amendment of the Labor xchange
Law (originally promulgated as Law No. 55 of April 9, 1921)
The new law provides merely for slight changes in the
phraseology of eight articles of the old law.

Law No. 13 (Promulgated May 26, 1936)
A Law Proviuing for the Amendment of the Chinese
Cultural Undertakings Special Account Law (originally
promulgated as Law No. 36 of arch 31, 1923).
The amendment adds a new paragraph to the supple-

re: ding, "For the time being, commencing with the
1936-37 fiscal year, the amount of the expenditure
from the Special Account for Chinese Cultural Under-
takings may exceed the limit specified in Article
7 of this law, but not by more than Yen 1,000,000"
(Article 7 referred to reads, "The expenditure to be
disbursed from the Special Account shall not exceed
Yen 3,000,000 a year, excluding the funds contributed".)
Law No. 14 (Promulgated ey 26, 1936)
A Law Froviding for the Establishment of a Central
Bank for Comnercial and Industrial Cooperative Societies.
The purpose of the new law is to afford monetary
facilities for the benefit of commercial cooperative
societies, industrial coonerative societies, end export
associations. The principal provisions of this law are
the following: the Central Bank for Commercial and In-
dustrial Cooperative Societies shall be a juridical
person and its head uarters shall be established in
Tokyo; the Bank shall have a capital of Yen 10,O00,000
end shall remain in existence for a period of fifty
years, which period may be extended subject to the
permission of the competent minister of tate; and
for obtaining funds the unk may issue commercial
and industrial debentures to an amount not exceeding
ten times its paid up cupital.
Law No. 15 (Promulgated may 2, 1936)
A Law Providing for the stablisiment of the North-
eastern Industrial Company.
This uct provides for the organization of a seni-

Gover. ment; cnd the Company shell be concerned ith such
enterprises as fertilizers and chemical industries, the
exploitation and develo ment of merine and mineral
products, lund reclamation work, etc.

Law No. 21 (Promulgated May 27, 1936)
A Law Providing for Revision of the Law Amending

Law No. 22 (Promulgated May 27, 1936)
A Law Providing for the Autonomous Control of
Rice and Cereals.
This act provides for the autonomous control
of rice and other cereals in Japan Proper, Chosen,
and Taiwan for the purpose of regulating supply
and demand in connection with the present over-
supply. To the above-mentionedrice and cereal
control societies are to be organized in all dis-
tricts throughout Japan Proper, Chosen and Taiwan
by rice producers and owners of rice producing
land who shall hold under joint control any super-
fluous supplies of rice or other cereals.
Law No. 23 (Promulgated May 27, 1936)
A Law Providing for the Amendment of the Law for
the Control of Rice and Cereals (promulgated as
Law No. 23 of 1933).
Most of the amendments are unimportant in char-
acter. However, the law provides that wheat and
wheat flour shail be added to the items mentioned
in Articles 8, 9, and 12 of the law of 1933. The
Articles of the old law in question provide that
the Government might for a certain limited period
restrict imports of, or increase, decrease or abolish
import duties on rice, millet, Kaoliang millet, panicum
and panicum millet. The addition of wheat and wheat
flour to this list of cereals may possibly be ex-
plained by the restrictive tariff measures against
Japanese impor ts recently adopted by the Australian
Government.
Law No. 24 (Promulgated May 27, 1936)
A Law Providing Subvention for Joint Storing of
Unhulled Rice.
This measure provides that, when industrial co-
operative societies, agricultural associations, or
other similar bodies designated by Imperial Ordinance
store unhulled rice for the purpose of regulating

=== Page 422 === (confidence 0.99)
-7-
the quantity of rice arriving on the market from
producing districts or for the purpose of providing
for short crop or famine, the Government may deliver
to such bodies a quantity of rice belonging to the
Special Account for Rice Demand and Supply Control,
not exceeding 300,000 koku a year.
Law No. 25 (Promulgated May 27, 1936)
A Law Providing for the Amendment of the Law
Relating to the Control of Important Industries
(promulgated as Law No. 40 on April 1, 1931).
The new law extends the life of the old one for
five years or from June 193o to June 194l. The other
amendments are unimportant, since they are merely
minor changes in phraseology.
Law No. 26 (Promulgated May 27, 1936)
A Law for the Control of Important Expor ts.
This measure provides that important staple goods
shall not be exported for sale in foreign markets
unless a pernit for such exportation has been obtained
from the competent Ministry after a proper official
examination. It is also provided that the classes of
important export goods to which this law applies shall
be designated by separate order or ordinance.
Law No. 27 (Promulgated May 27, 1936)
A Law Providing for the Amendment of the Law
Pertaining to the Control of Expor ts of Silk Textiles
(originally promulgated on ∆pri1 1, 1927).
This amendment measure changes five articles of
the original law so that now they do not conflict
with the new Law for the Control of Important Export
Goods (mentioned above).
Law No. 28 (Promulgated May 27, 1936)
A Law Providing for the Revision of the Export
Association Law (originally promulgated March, 1925).
The amendments contained in this law are un-
important, affecting the penal clauses of the law
principaliy.
Law No. 29 (Promulgated May 28, 1936)
A Law Providing for the Protective Observation
of Persons Charged with Crimes Involving Dangerous
Thought.
This law provides that persons charged with crimes
involving dangerous. thought whose sentences are pend-
ing or who have served their terms or who have been
provisionally released, shall be placed under the "pro-
tection" and surveillance of the competent authorities
for a period of two years. During this period special

=== Page 423 === (confidence 0.99)
-8-
officers shall be designated to observe the
thoughts and actions of such persons lest the
latter should commit crimes of the sort already
committed.*
Law No. 30 (Promulgated May 28, 1936)
A Law for the Control of the Fertilizer
Industry.
This new enactment aims, as stated in
Article 1, at maintaining a smooth relation
between demand and supply of fertilizers, at
regula ting their market price, and at helping
in the improvement and development of the fer-
tilizer manufacturing industry. For the further-
ance of these objects, the law empowers the
Government to order manufacturers to organize as-
sociations for the control of the quantity of
fertilizers to be manufactured by its members,
of sales prices, and of other matters connected
with production, supply, demand, etc. The kinds
of fertilizers to which the law applies are to
be determined by separa te order of ordinance.
Law No. 31 (Promulgated May 28, 1936)
A Law Providing for the Revision of the
Horse Racing Law (promulgated April 10, 1936
as Law No. 47).
The revised law provides that the only as-
sociation authorized to hold professional horse
races shall be the Nippon Race Association, which
shall be reorganized as a juridical person with
the object of promoting the improvement of horses
and the spreading of knowledge relating to horses.
It is further provided by the new law that
one year after the reorganization of the Nippon
Race Association all other horse racing associa-
tions shall be dissolved.
Law No. 32 (Promul gated May 28, 1956)
A Law Providing for the Revision of the Iron
Industry Encouragement Law (promulgated as Law
No. 49 of 1926).
The revisions made in the old law are un-
important, consisting principally of minor alter-
ations of a few phrases in the text.
Law No. 33 (Promulgated May 29, 1936)
A Law Providing for the Control of the
Automobile Manufacturing Industry.
This law provides for the control of the
automobile industry in Japan by the establishment
of a license system under which new foreign auto-
mobile firms shall not be allowed to operate in
* A translation of this law will be transmitted to the
Department in the near future.

=== Page 424 === (confidence 0.99)
-9-
Japan and already existing foreign firms shall
not be allowed to increase their businesses
unless Japanese subjects are admitted to owner-
ship of a majority of stock. The purpose of
this law, as stated in Article 1, is to strengthen
national defense and to promote industrial progress
by establishing an automobile manufacturing in-
dustry in Japan.
A translation of the complete text of the law
was transmitted to the Department with the Embassy's
despatch No. 1891 of June 11, 1936. Translations
of the texts of three Inperial Ordinances relative
to the Automobile Manufacturing Industry Law, as
published in the OFFICIAL GAZETTE of July 10, 1936,
were transmitted to the Department with the Embassy's
desp: tch No. 1944 of July 4, 1936.
Law No. 34 (Promulgated May 28, 1956)
A Law Providing for the Revision of the Aviation
Law (Promulgated as Law No. 54 of 1921).
The revision covers ten articles of the law,
nine of which have been only sli htly amended. An
important amendment, however, has been made in the
text of Article S5, vhich as revised reads: "No
aeroplanes Which aie not Japanese aeroplanes shall
engage in the transportation of passengers or
freight receiving fare therefor between a place
outside Japan and a place in Japan," whereas the
former text read, "No aeroplanes which are not
Japanese aeroplanes shall enguge in the transporta-
tion of passengers or freight receiving fare there-
for between a place in Japan and another place in
Japan."
Law No. 35 (Promulgated May 29, 1936)
A Law Providing for the Control of Shipping
Services.
This act stipulates that when the Government
recognizes the existence of ruinous competition in
a steamship service it shall be empowered to ad-
vise the interested parties to stop the competition
and in the event of refusal shall be authorized to
prohibit navigation.
Law No. 36 (Promulcated June 1, 1936)
A Law Pertaining to the Revaluation of the
Rentel Value of Land.
Under authority of Clause I of Article 9 of
the Land Tax Law, the present law provides de-
tailed regulations for the revaluation of the
rental value of land and stipulates that new
taxes based on this revaluation shall go into
effect on January 1, 1937.

=== Page 425 === (confidence 0.97)
- 10 -
Law No. 37 (Promulgated on may 3u, 1936)
A Law Relating to Enforcereut of the Law for the
Revaluation of the Rental Value of Land in Connection
with the Arable Land Adjustment Law.
This is a companion law of Law No. 36 mentioned above.
It sets forth regulations for the enforcement of those
provisions of Law o. 36 that come within the purview of
the Arable Land Adjustment Law.
Law o. 38 (Promulgated may 30, 1936)
A Law Providing for the Amendment of the Customs
Tariff Law.
The measure affects ten items of the import articles
enumerated in the Import Tariff Schedule annexed to the
Customs Tariff Law. It increases the duty on vegetable
volatile oil (Tariff o. 95), mineral oil (Tariff o.
112), paraffin oil (Tariff No. ll4), acety1 salicylic
acid (Tariff 1o. 158), antifebrin (Tariff 1o. 209),
pyramidon (Tariff o. 209-3), carbonate of guiaico1
(Tariff No. 216-2), iron (Tariff o. 462), aluminum
(Tariff o. 463), etc.
A separate despatch dealing with the exact amounts
of the various increases in duty will be forwarded to
the Department in the next pouch.

One. The Government has today dissolved the Lower
House and a general election is set for April 30.
Two. Of the Government bills which fail of passage
by reason of dissolutior the most important from the point
of view of Government finance is the tariff bill. Less im-
portant are bills on farming lands, xport control tax and
revision of iron manufacturing law. The budget was passed.
Three. During the last few days the Lower House has
shown itseli recalcitrant because of the Government's failure
actively to push the election revision bill through the
Upper House. This bill is not radical hut would relieve
tho parties from certain election restrictions which have
been enforced since the Inukai cabinet. The Government's
excuse for not pushing the bill is that the bill while
FULED
embodying some of the reco mondations of the election law
revision com.ission does not embody all such recommendations.

=== Page 449 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
Apr11 22, 1937
Tokyo's No. 2339 of April 1, 1937,
recounts proceedings in the Diet during
the period March 17 to March 31.
It was during the period under review
that the Lower House was dissolved, a
situation which was described in another
despatch. As stated by Premier Hayashi
this action was accounted for by the
failure of the parties to "appreciate the
current situation". It is difficult to
see how the coming election returns can
appreciably alter the present composition
of the Diet. It may be, however, that
the Cabinet is hoping to make a deal
with one of the major parties, possibly
the Minseito.
Among the Government's legislative
projects which the Diet failed to approve,
to the displeasure of the Government,
were bills relating to the following:
import tariff, exemption of import duty
on iron, revision of the law on cases
entrusted to Japanese courts by foreign
courts, revision of the law for the pro-
tection of military secrets, establish-
ment of national health insurance system,
establishment of the Imperial Fuel Develop-
ment Company, etc.

in its owm way and without compromising with the political
parties.

DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
Apri1 22, 1937
T
Tokyo's No. 2350 of Apr11 2, 1937, 1s
a well written appraisal of the situation
in conneotion with the dissolution of the
Diet. It is suggested that you will wish
to read in toto.
Following the passage by the Diet of
the budget bill, the Government was in
position to be loss conciliatory in its
attitude toward the Diet. The two major
parties had joined forces in support of a
bill for the revision of the olection law
whioh was distasteful to the Government.
With the budget bill out of the way the
parties asked the Government, in return
for support of the remaining Government
bills, to give support to their election
revision bill. When the Government showed
no indication of doing so the Lower House
recessed. This seemed to the Cabinet an
unwarranted provocation.
The following day the Diet was ordered
dissolved, and April 30 was named as the
date for a new election.
The Cabinet's purpose in obtaining an
order for dissolution was obviously to dis-
cipline the Diet for having showed a
recalcitrant attitude toward the remaining
Government

=== Page 458 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
- 2 -
Government bills. In a larger sense the
order for dissolution was a manoeuver by
the military leaders to overcome the grow-
ing opposition of the Diet to military
dominanoe in the Government. It was a
disciplinary action taken by the Cabinet
against the Diet -- the very reverse of
anything whioh could happen under a
parliamentary form of government. There
has been little popular outcry against the
Government's action, chiefly because of the
fact that the people have no great respeot
for the politicians and are apt to share
the Government's distrust of the Diet.
It appears that the Government is hop-
ing that a new government party will be
created to which it might throw the full
weight of its support. The Embassy suggests
that this may mark the beginning of an
attempt to form a fascist party. However,
recent press reports from Tokyo indicate
that there is little popular support of
any effort to form a new military-fostered
party.

SUBJECT: AN APPRAISAL OF THE DIET DISSOLUTION.

FE (381)
The Honorable
The Secretary of State,
Fub
ashington.
sir:
March 31, 4 p. m., reporting the dissolution of the Japan-
ese House of Representatives on that day and reporting the
fixing of April 30 for a general election of a new House,
I have

=== Page 460 === (confidence 0.99)
-2 -
I have the honor to submit the following comments with
regard to the dissolution and the present political
outlook.
Following the passage by the House of Peers on
March 29 of the five bills embodying the new budget,
previously passed by the House of Representatives, the
Government was in position to be less conciliatory in
its attitude toward the Diet.
A number of Government
bills had not been acted upon, but, with the budget passed,
none were vital.
The Minseito and Seiyukai parties mean-
time had joined foroes in support of a bill for the re-
vision of the election law, and they had sucoeeded in
passing it through the Lower House.
This bill contains
no radical features but would free the parties from a
number of hampering restrictions which have been enforoed
during the past five years. It is probably true, as the
Shakai Taishuto (proletarian or social mass party) has
since charged in a campaign statement, that the freer pro-
visions of the bill would offer greater opportunity than
now exists for abuse by the parties; but nevertheless the
proposed election law revision is not extreme and the Gov-
ernment, even since dissolution, has not chosen to attack it.
The parties would be far from free even with the passage of
the bill.
Although they had not risked an attempt to drive bar-
gains with the Government in return for the passage of the
budget, when the budget was out of the way the Seiyukai and
Minseito did ask the Government, in return for support of
the remaining Government bills, to press their election
revision bill through the House of Peers.
.On Tuesday,
March 30, the Upper House committee having jurisdiction
of the

=== Page 461 === (confidence 0.99)
- 3 -
of the bill met briefly and adjourned.
The Cabinet's
attitude on putting the bill through was not indicated,
but the Seiyukai and Minseito deduced, probably rightly,
that the Government would not assist.
When the Lower
House met in the afternoon of the same day it immediately
recessed.
The Cabinet met later in the afternoon and discussed
dissolution.
There was little enough provocation to the
Government in the recessing of that day's session of the
House of Representatives, but the Cabinet seemed intent on
making the most of it.
The meeting of the Cabinet was
concluded by statements to the press in which Navy Minister
Yonai (not the War Minister, be it noted) was represented
as favoring dissolution. Domei translated Yonai's words
thus: "The Minseito and the Seiyukai, in tryins to force
the Government into having their election bills passed by
delaying the proceedings in the Diet can not be ragarded
as having any sincerity.
. To make them reflect on their
attitude we should dissolve the Diet."
It was thus stated
as a disciplinary matter. Politically speaking, the Cabi-
net was well advised to have this come fron Yonai, wh is
a popular and greatly respeoted member of the Cabinet.
consequence the attack on the Diet appears as a Government
matter, not merely an Army matter; and it indioates also
that the Navy group, however opposed to Itagaki and his
followers, is not less opposed to the existing political
parties.
Next morning, Wednesday March 31, the Cabinet obtained
an order from the Emperor for dissolution. It was the
final

=== Page 462 === (confidence 0.99)
- 4 -
final day of the six-day prolongation of the session.
The
70th session of the Diet was thereby brought to a olose, and
April 30 was named in the order as the date for election of
members of a new Lower House. (Artiole 45 of the constitu-
tion, article 18 of the election law.)
Premier Hayashi accompanied this action with a statement
to the press in words of astonishingly frank sermonizing to
the Diet.
The following passages are quoted from the Domei
translation:
We have carried out dissolution
of the House of Representatives as a step toward reform of
the Diet.
I expected the parties to give us
more positive assistance in the execution of our convictions
and to appreciate the current situation as it is appreciated
by us.
In the final analysis, it is the question
of appreciation of the current situation that is separating
us from the parties.
I desire that the parties
think over the points on which they differ from us.
I think there would be nothing wrong should the Government
decide to cooperate with a new party that might come into
existence and would support it.
The Government has not yet
considered whether it should take positive action in the di-
rection of forming a new party.
It would be good if a party
ready to support the Government came into being of its own
accord.
There is no intention on my part to take the initi-
ative in forming such a party. I am afraid I have yet to
make up my mind regarding the matter. It is impossible to
make any forecast about the outcome of the election.
We shall determine our attitude toward the situation when the
election is over."
Up to

=== Page 463 === (confidence 0.99)
-5-
Up to the present time this statement by the Premier
remains the most authoritative olue. Dissolution was a
cool, disciplinary decision of polioy, not necessitated by
any active opposition.
. The Premier's statement is the state-
ment of a man innately distristful of party government and
yet not clear what is the next step. He has practically
nothing to say in justification of dissolution; it follows
from conviction rather than for cause.
The action is for
discipline, taken by the Cabinet against the Diet -- the
very reverse of anything which could occur under a parlia-
mentary form of government.
Then, uncertain of the capacity
of the Government to set up a party itself, the Premier in-
vites an approach to the Government by any new group which
may wish to take up the Government's cause. In an interview
with newspaper men on the day of dissolution he further said:
"The Government hopes most earnestly that a party will ap-
pear, or that men will appear in politics, who are awake to
the times. We should probably be sympathetic toward each
other.
More can not be said until things take
on more definite shape."
1/
▲ clipping of the most recent press orticle on campaign
preparations is enclosed herewith.
Premier Hayashi, as an rmy man, has without doubt
given careful thought to proposals of such a new party as
was discussed in the mbassy's despatches Nos. 2268 and
273 of February 18, 1937.
. The great difficulty of making
such a new party effective in an election in the near future
appears to have been weighed and conceded.
But within the
field of politics are there not individuals or groups to
serve temporarily in a similar way?
The Government holds
out an invitation -- not sure even in its own mind what
would

=== Page 464 === (confidence 0.98)
-6-
would be the direction of events if it oould direot them,
but desiring to build up something to replace the existing
parties. Meantime, for safety, the Premier refuses to
state what sort of result in the elections would be taken
as defeat of this non-party Cabinet.
In bringing on this unnecessary and anomalous election
the Government has certain advantages in its favor.
people have no great respect for the political parties,
and condone the Government's distruct of them: there has
been no popular outory against the Cabinet's tactics in the
dissolution, nor against causing an eleotion which offers the
people no effeotive choice in anything. Second, the Govern-
ment has little to los. If a new Lower House is returned
even in about the present proportions, the Government would
simply have gained a few months' time. Third, any new
party development holds the possibility of working to the
Government's advantage.
The next four weeks will probably witness an effort by
the Government to draw, from existing political circles,
what supporters it can induce to give up the old alignments.
To these perhaps a few candidates put up by a military-
fostered party will be added. It is likely that, in an
opportunist way, the beginnings of a fascist party will be
pieced together.
.The Government is ready to assist any
talent that offers.
Respectfully yours,
2.
Joseph C. Grew.

This despatch from Tokyo discusses pro-
ceedings of the special session of the Japanese
Diet which convened on July 23.
Discussions in the Diet have been dominated
by the state of affalrs in China which have con-
firmed the view that there exists no body of.
opinion opposed to the use of force by Japan in
China. Premier Konoe stated that Japan has been
compelled to make an important decision in re-
gard to the North China Incident, in view of
Japan's desire to preserve peace in Eastern Asia.
He declared that the present crisis is one of
those trials that will enable Japan to rise to
greater heights of prosperity and power.
In a speech before the Diet Foreign Minister
Hirota declared: "Japan-American relations have
been of late really good, being marked by ever-
growing amity and good will. I am glad to say
that the economic mission which visited the
United States some weeks ago has by its candid
exchange of views with various circles accom-
plished much toward establishing closer contact
between our two nations economically and other-
wise." He also stated: "It is gratifying to note
the growth of certain tendencies favorable to
restoration of the freedom of trade. I desire
to take this opportunity to declare the readiness
of the Japanese Government to participate
heartily in any international undertakin to con.
vert these tendenoies into reality."
The

=== Page 467 === (confidence 0.99)
DEPARTMENT OF STATE
DIVISION OF FAR EASTERN AFFAIRS
-2-
The session gave favorable consideration
to a large number of appropriations, mostly for
military purposes in China. Direct expenditures
for the current North China Incident amount to
approximately one-half billion yen, according
to a statement by the Finance Minister. He
reported that during the first six months of
1937 imports were in excess of exports by
yen 641,000,000, which 1s double the adverse
excess of the same period of 1936.
In reply to an interpellation in t he House
of Peers the Premier defined international jus-
tice as a state of fair distribution of territory
and natural resources.

The Honorable

Sir:
On July 23, 1937, the seventy-first session of the
Japanese Diet convened. This is a session required by the
45th article of the Japanese constitution, which states that
when the House of Representatives has been ordered to dissolve,
members shall be caused by imperial order to be newly elected,
and
F/G

=== Page 469 === (confidence 0.99)
76-2
-2-
and the new House shall be convoked within five months from
the day of dissolution. The previous House was dissolved
at the close of March by the Hayashi Cabinet, and the present
seventy-first session is the first convocation of the member-
ship ohosen by the general election of April.
Organizing activities occupied the first days. On
July 25 the Emperor formally opened the session. The Cabinet
had announoed that the session would be for two weeks, which
expires on August 8.
The seventy-first session thus far has uncovered no sur-
prises, and no surprises are expected in the remaining days.
The state of affairs in China has dominated the discussions,
which have confirmed the view that there exists no body of
opinion opposed to the prevailing belief that, failing a
modification of Chinese attitude, resort to force by Japan
is justified. Addresses of Government leaders, and the re-
action to such addresses, have served as an important inder
to Japanese policy. Secondly, the session has given favor-
able consideration to a number of large appropriations, dom-
inantly for the China incident. Thirdly, a number of Govern-
ment bills have been submitted, though not yet acted upon.
I. Addresses by Government leaders.
On July 27 Premier Konoe delivered his opening address to
the seventy-first session of the Diet and asked for support in
the national emergency; stated that the Government is pre-
pared to carry out reforms where reforms are needed, accom-
plishing them one by one; and explained that bills ready for
presentation to this Diet, following so closely on formation
of his Cabinet, must of necessity be few. He expressed regret
for the north China incident, said that the Japanese Govern-
ment has been compelled to make an important decision with re-
gard

=== Page 470 === (confidence 0.99)
76-3
- 3 -
gard to it, asserted that Japan's object is the preservation of
peace in eastern Asia, spoke on behalf of national defense
measures, a comprehensive national economic plan, and the con-
ception of Japan and Manchuria as a single unit, deferred the
question of administrative, parliamentary and educational re-
form, and announced that a bill is being submitted to provide
for a separate department of health and social affairs. He
said that the present crisis will lead to greater heights of
1/
power and prosperity. Text of the address is given in trans-
lation in enclosure.
In replying to an interpellation in the House of Peers on
July 28 the Premier defined social justice as a state under
which all persons can earn a living, and defined international
justice as a state of fair distribution of territory and nat-
ural resources.
Foreign Minister Hirota also delivered an address to the
Diet on July 27. The translation supplied by the Foreign Office
2/
is emboded in enclosure 2. In referring to relations with China
Mr. Hirota spoke of the necessity of harmony between Japan,
"Manchukuo", China, and the Soviet Union; in connection with
anti-Japanese sentiment in China mentioned past recalcitrance
of the Nanking Government in negotiations with regard to it;
alluded to Japan's policy of stopping the communist invasion
of the Orient; repeated that Japan desires to settle the north
China incident by local settlement and without aggravation;
pleaded for Chinese reconsideration and faithful execution of
the settlement of July ll; asserted that China has it in her
hands either to settle the issue or to make it more serious;
and declared that Japan is prepared to use all available means
which may be called for in the defense of Japanese nationals.
With regard to the Soviet Union the Foreign Minister again
favored

=== Page 471 === (confidence 0.99)
76-4
一 4 -
favored a commission to determine the Manohurian frontier and
a commission on border incidents, and in reference to fishery
rights made the declaration that it is Japanese polioy never
to tolerate anything in substance destroying enterprises
sanctioned by treaty. In reference to Great Britain he spoke
highly of the Chichibu visit to London and of an agreement of
view between the two Governments concerning the advisability
of frank conversations to adjust relations (Mr. Hirota ignored
reports of British breaking off of such conversations). His
remarks about relations with the United States were in trans-
lation as follows: "Japanese-American relations have been of
late really good, being marked by ever-growing amity and good
will. I am glad to say that the economic mission which visited
the United States some weeks ago has by its candid exchange of
views with various circles. accomplished much toward establish-
ing closer contaot between our two nations economically and
otherwise."
The Foreign Minister concluded his address with a dis-
cussion of the necessity of expansion of Japan's export trade.
He pledged the Government to furtherance of such commercial
interests and to endeavors to insure freedom of trade and free
access to natural resources. He also said: "It is gratifying
to note the growth of certain tendencies favorable to re-
storation of the freedom of trade. I desire to take this
opportunity to declare the readiness of the Japanese Govern-
ment to participate heartily in any international under-
taking to convert these tendencies into reality."
The following day, July 28, in response to an interpel-
lation in the House of Representatives, Foreign Minister
Hirota made a further statement on the subject of the con-
versations with Great Britain, as follows:
Embassy's

=== Page 472 === (confidence 0.99)
76-5
- 5 -
(Embassy's translation): "Our Foreign policy, as I stated
in my acdress yesterday, has as its background the maintenance
of peace and order in the Far East; and to effect such maintence
close association between Japan, Manchukuo and China is
necessary. It is, therefore, greatly to be regretted that the
present situation has developed. The fact is that before the
situation arose conversations between Japan and Great Britain
with regard to Far Eastern questions had made substantial pro-
gress. However, even though methods were devised for the ren-
dering of assistance to China by Japan and Great Britain, such
methods would be entirely inapplicable in the existing cir-
cumstances, and due to the deplorable situation which has
arisen the conversations have terminated for the time being."
To another interpellation in the House of Representatives
on July 28 Mr. Hirota said (Embassy's translation): "Replying
to a request by Dr. Akamatsu for expression of view with re-
gard to the future development of the situation in North China,
I may say that the policy of the Government thus far has been
to seek settlement locally and to prevent aggravation of the
situation. We have had in the past various agreements with
Sung Che Yuan and with other Chinese authorities, and if these
agreements had been carefully observed peace and order in North
China could have been maintained; but unfortunately the Chinese
did not carry out their obligations. And the latest reports
indicate that disorder has broken out in North China, and anxiety
is felt not only for Japanese nationals but for nationals of
Western countries as well. In the present state of mind of the
people of China, improvement in relations between Japan and China
is a matter of extreme difficulty. With reference to the state
of feeling in China, it may be stated that of course there has
been animosity toward Japan, and from that there is developing
a

=== Page 473 === (confidence 0.99)
76-6

a feeling of contempt toward this country. We feel that some
method must be devised to urge reconsideration of such an attitude".
Finance Minister Kaya also addressed the Diet on July 27.
EA
He proposed a revaluation of specie reserves of the Bank of
Japan, stating nevertheless that the Government has no intention
of changing the present foreign exchange value of the yen, one
shilling and two pence (Embassy's telegram No. 215, July 21, 4 p.m.).
He reported that, for the Japanese Empire including colonies,
exports during the first six months of 1937 were ¥1,602,000,000
and imports ¥2,243,000,000 an adverse excess of 641,000,000
which is double the adverse excess of the same period of 1936.
He went on to refer to gold shipments and stated that for the
future the Government desires to limit the amount of annual gold
shipped within the limits of the annual domestic production of
gold. He mentioned the striking rise of commodity prices since
December l936 and said that the matter is being referred to the
price control commission. He enumerated certain appropriations
necessary for the north China incident (which are referred to
hereunder).

Address of Mr. Koki Hirota, Minister
for Foreign Affairs, at the Seventy-
first (Extraordinary) Session of the
Diet, on July 27, 1937.
I am happy to have this opportunity today of stating my
views concerning the foreign relations of Japan.
It is not necessary for me to expatiate afresh on our
national policy, which aims at securing Japan's position as
a stabilizing force in East Asia, and contributing toward the
establishment of true peace throughout the world. I believe
that in order to carry out this policy we should, first of all,
consider the relations between Japan, Manchoukuo, China, and
the Soviet Union.
In surveying the conditions of present day China, our
Government can not but profoundly regret to note that anti-
Japanese sentiments and movements have been encouraged and
systematically exploited for the purpose of unifying public
opinion and arousing nationalistic consciousness, and that
untoward incidents evidently resulting therefrom are taking
place in various quarters in China. At the time of the
Chengtu Incident Iast year, the Japanese Government took
occasion to ask the Chinese to rectify their attitude toward
Japan, which constituted the fundamental obstacle to friendly
intercourse between Japan and China, and to invite the Nanking
Government to demonstrate their sincerity regarding the concrete
questions bound up with the amelioration of the relations
between the two countries. Unfortunately, as you know, the
negotiations came to an impasse, owing to Chinese recalcitrance.
Since then, Sino-Japanese relations have been, frankly speaking,
far

=== Page 478 === (confidence 0.99)
- 2 -
far from satisfactory. I need not repeat here that Japanese
policy in East Asia is directed solely toward the realization
of the stability of that region through conciliation and co-
operation between Japan, Manchoukuo and China, and by stopping
the Communist invasion of the Orient. The Japanese Government
therefore earnestly hope that China will as soon as possible
come to have a full understanding and appreciation of our
basic policy.
Today in China not only is such understanding or
appreciation absent, but anti-Japanese sentiments have been
still more intensified, of which the Lukouchiao Incident of
the 7th of this month, was only the logical consequence. As
regards the present incident, the Japanese Government have
maintained, as announced in their official statement of July
llth, a policy of seeking a settlement on the spot and non-
ageravation of the situation. Accordingly, the Government
have been doing thoir best to effect a peaceful local solution.
and at the same time to induce the Nanking Government to t ake
proper steps for an early settlement of the question. It is
my earnest hope that prompt reconsideration on the part of
the Chinese authorities will lead to a faithful execution of
the terms of settlement arrived at on the night of July llth.
The governments of the Powers have been informed in detail
through our diplomatic representatives of this policy of the
Japanese Government, and I believe they understand fully
Japan's attitude of patience and self-restraint. On the other
the
hand, there is no knowing what will be possible repercus-
sions among the Chinese masses, dependent as this is upon
what stand the Nanking Government may take. There exists,
it must be admitted, the danger of an untoward outbreak at
any

=== Page 479 === (confidence 0.98)
- 3 -
any moment. We have more than once called the attention of
both the Chinese central government and the local authorities
to the necessity of control of anti-Japanese activities and
proteotion of our nationals in China. In order to insure the
safety, of our fellow-countrymen the Japanese Government are
prepared to use all available means which may be called for
by the development of the situation. Thus, in a word, China
holds the key of the settlement of the present incident, as
it depends entirely upon what course she may choose to follow.
I confidently hope that the Nanking Government will adopt
such effective and appropriate measures as will accord with
our desire and bring about an early and amicable settlement.
Eu
The Government are giving careful consideration to our
relations with the Soviet Union. Incidents have continued
to occur along the ManchoukuoSoviet frontier. The most
serious case was the recent illicit invasion and occupation
of Manchoukuo islands in the Amur, which led to an armed clash
of the Japanese-Manchoukuo forces with the Soviet intruders.
The situation threatened for a time to develop into one of
ortreme gravity. However, the affair ended, as you know, in
a peaceful settlement, the Soviet Government agreeing to
restore the stetus quo ante in that region. The Japanese
Government are deeply concerned over the state of things on
the Manchoukuo-Soviet frontier, which gives rise to frequent
friction. What is needed first of all is to take practical
steps to prevent these border disputes. For that purpose,
we should lose no time in setting up those two commissions
for the demarkation of the border line and the settlement of
disputes, which for some years past have been under con-
sideration, and also devise other means of removing the tension
all along the frontier. I cannot but urge most strongly upon
the Soviet Government to co-operate freely and unreservedly
with

=== Page 480 === (confidence 0.98)
EU
- 4 -
with us in this task for the sake of the peace of East Asia.
Again, it is a matter of no small concern for the Govern-
ment whether or not our nationals engaged in fishery in the
northern waters and those having oil and coal concessions in
North Saghalien are to be accorded full guarantees for the
legitimate operation of their enterprises. It is our policy
never to tolerate the creation of any such circumstances as
will in substance destroy those enterprises sanctioned, as
they are, by treaty. In short, there remains between Japan
and the Soviet Union a number of issues stillpending, for
the solution of which the Government will do their utmost.
As for our relations with Great Britain, I am deeply
moved when I say that a short while ago on behalf of His
Majesty the Emperor, His Imperial Highness Prince Chichibu,
accompanied by Princess Chichibu, attended the Coronation
ceremonies of the King and Queen of England, and by their
mission they have enchanced the traditional friendship that
binds our two nations. It has always been the consistent
policy of the Government to promote Anglo-Japanese friend-
ship, and more recently the two governments have come to an
agreement of views regarding the advisability of ontering
into frank conversations with the object of adjusting the
relations of the two countries. We hope to bring about an
early attainment of that aim.
Japanese-American relations have been of late really
good, being marked by evergrowing amity and good will. I am
glad to say that the Econonic Mission which visited the United
States some weeks ago, have by their candid exchange of
views with various circles accomplished much toward establish-
ing closer contact between our two nations economically and
otherwise.
Last

=== Page 481 === (confidence 0.98)
- 5 -
Last year this country concluded the Anti-Comintern
Agreement with Germany. The Government are striving for an
effective application of the said agreement, and at the same
time for the furtherance of friendly and intimate relations
between Japan and Germany.
Now to turn to our trade relations with other countries.
Obviously the expansion of our xport trade constitutes
not only an indispensable condition of our national existence,
but one of the most important means of balancing our inter-
national accounts under the present economic circumstances of
the country. But as a matter of fact, the foreign Powers, for
economic, financial, or other reasons, still continue to main-
tain commercial barriers of various kinds against Japanese
goods. The Government are working for a smooth development
of our export trade by concluding suoh individual agreements
with the governments of those countries as may be best suited
to their respective circumstances, or by arranging for private
agreements to be arrived at between our business interests
and those of other countries. I am glad to say that since
last spring our trade negotiations with India, Burma, the
Netherlands East Indies, and Turkey, have all been brought to
a successful conclusion. The Government will strive more
energetically than ever for the maintenance and furtherance
of Japan's commercial interests, and at the same time make
all possible endeavours to insure free access to raw material
and natural resources, and to promote the freedom of inter-
national trade. It is gratifying to note the growth of centain
tendencies favourable to the restoration of the freedom of
trade. I desire to take this opportunity to declar the
readiness of the Japanese Governnent to participate heartily
in any international undertaking to convert these tendencies
into a reality.
As

=== Page 482 === (confidence 0.95)
一 6 -
As may be seen from what I have stated above, Japan's
foreign relations at present are fraught with problems of
great difficulty. For an effective execution of our foreign
policy at this time a true national unity is required - a
unity which is based upon a full comprehension of the inter-
national situation. And I appeal to you for support and
co-operation.

=== Page 484 === (confidence 0.98)
(b) The Diet.
Until the close of the month the proceedings of the special Diet :
sic, which was convened on July 23, were concerned largely with
the North China situation.* The Government's supplementary budgets
for financing Japanese military operations in China were quickly
passed by both Houses.**
In an address delivered before the Diet on July 27 Premier
Konoe stated that in sending troops to North China the Japanese
Government had no other purpose than to "preserve the peace of
East Asia" and that he hoped most fervently that reconsideration
and self-discipline on the part of the Government and people of
Chine would make speedily possible a fundamental adjustment of
Sino-Japanese relations. Subsequently, in reply to an interpellation,
the Premier said that what Jepan wanted of China was not territory
but cooperation.
Mr. Hirota, the Minister for Foreign Affairs, delivered an
address to the Diet in which he said that he believed that foreign
governments understood fully Japan's attitude of "patience and self-
restraint" with regard to North China. General Sugiyama, the War
Minister, told the Diet that the origin of the present North China
Jificulties was the general anti-Japanese movement in China which
"as an outcome of the Nanking Government's thorough anti-Japanese
rolicy and of China's over-estimation of its national strength.
On July 28 Premier Konoe stated in reply to an interpellation
that in his oninion it was premature to talk at the present time of
erthrowing the National Government since there were many persons
i1 that Government who understood Japan, including General Chiang
Kai-shek.
